**Capture warning: this message is truncated at 20,000 characters. Its ending is missing.**

## Ruleset 3.4 — Chakra Nature & Affinity

Chakra nature should determine **what kind of elemental transformation a character can perform, how naturally it comes to them, and how difficult further elemental development becomes**.

The most important distinction should be:

> **Affinity is potential. Nature Transformation is skill. Jutsu are applications.**

A character may be naturally suited to Fire Release and still know almost no Fire techniques.

Likewise, someone can eventually learn an element they have no natural affinity for, but doing so should generally require substantially more training.

---

# 3.4.1 The Five Basic Nature Transformations

The five standard elemental transformations remain:

- **Fire Release**
- **Wind Release**
- **Lightning Release**
- **Earth Release**
- **Water Release**

Each should have its own trainable Skill.

For example:

> **Fire Nature Transformation — Intermediate III, 42/100**

This Skill represents the character's general ability to convert ordinary chakra into that elemental nature.

It does **not** represent mastery of individual Fire Release jutsu.

---

# 3.4.2 Affinity and Skill Must Remain Separate

A character might have:

> Natural Affinity: Fire  
> Fire Nature Transformation: Basic IV

Another might have:

> Natural Affinity: Water  
> Fire Nature Transformation: Advanced I

The second character is currently better at Fire Release despite lacking the natural Fire affinity.

Affinity affects:

- Learning speed.
- Initial ease.
- Efficiency.
- Natural ceiling pressure.
- Difficulty of advanced mastery.

It should not function as an automatic permanent superiority bonus.

---

# 3.4.3 Primary Affinity

Most chakra-using characters should have one **Primary Nature Affinity**.

This is the element their chakra naturally transforms into most easily.

Primary affinity should generally provide:

- Faster early learning.
- Reduced difficulty for basic transformation.
- Better initial efficiency.
- Easier progression into advanced elemental control.
- A higher likelihood of discovering the nature naturally.

The affinity itself does **not** grant jutsu.

A character can know they have Wind affinity and still be incapable of producing a useful Wind Release technique.

---

# 3.4.4 Affinity Discovery

Affinity should not necessarily be known automatically.

It can be discovered through:

- Chakra paper.
- Specialized testing methods.
- Elemental training.
- An instructor recognizing tendencies.
- Accidental manifestation.
- Clan knowledge.
- Certain sensory techniques.

This matters particularly for children and civilians.

An Academy student might possess a Lightning affinity for years without knowing it.

---

# 3.4.5 Multiple Natural Affinities

Some characters should naturally possess more than one affinity.

This should be uncommon but not inherently extraordinary.

Possible configurations include:

> Fire — Strong  
> Earth — Moderate

or:

> Water — Strong  
> Wind — Strong

Multiple affinities can result from:

- Genetics.
- Clan heritage.
- Unusual chakra composition.
- Exceptional innate talent.
- Certain special abilities.

Having two affinities does **not** automatically mean possessing a combined Kekkei Genkai.

A character naturally talented in Water and Wind is not automatically an Ice Release user.

---

# 3.4.6 Affinity Strength

Affinity shouldn't necessarily be binary.

Internally, it can exist along a spectrum such as:

**Strong Affinity**  
The chakra readily transforms into that nature.

**Normal Affinity**  
A genuine natural inclination.

**Weak Affinity**  
Some innate compatibility, but not enough to count as a primary affinity.

**Neutral**  
No meaningful natural advantage or disadvantage.

**Difficult Compatibility**  
The character can potentially learn it, but transformation comes unusually poorly to them.

We don't necessarily need to expose these exact labels constantly.

The simulation can track underlying compatibility.

---

# 3.4.7 No Element Should Be Universally Incompatible

Ordinary characters should generally be capable of learning any of the five basic natures with enough training.

There should usually be no rule like:

> "You were born Fire affinity, therefore you can never use Water Release."

Instead, off-affinity elements become progressively harder.

This supports characters who intentionally broaden their arsenal over a long career.

---

# 3.4.8 Nature Transformation Skill Structure

Because elemental transformation is important but narrower than Chakra Control, I recommend:

> **Basic I–V**  
> **Intermediate I–V**  
> **Advanced I–V**  
> **Expert I–V**  
> **Master I–V**

for each element.

For example:

> Water Nature Transformation — Advanced II, 71/100

Characters therefore progress independently in every nature.

Someone might have:

> Fire — Expert I  
> Earth — Intermediate IV  
> Lightning — Basic II  
> Wind — Untrained  
> Water — Untrained

This creates meaningful specialization.

---

# 3.4.9 What Nature Transformation Skill Actually Represents

The Nature Transformation Skill should govern:

- Successfully converting chakra into the element.
- Transformation stability.
- Elemental purity.
- Efficiency.
- Intensity.
- Fine elemental control.
- Maintaining transformation.
- Manipulating elemental properties.
- Combining elemental chakra with Shape Transformation.
- Meeting prerequisites for elemental jutsu.

It should **not** replace:

- Chakra Control.
- Chakra Output.
- Shape Transformation.
- Individual jutsu mastery.

A complicated elemental technique may require all four.

---

# 3.4.10 Basic Nature Transformation

At **Basic**, the character is learning to produce the nature itself.

Typical progression might involve:

- Brief elemental manifestation.
- Applying elemental chakra to an object.
- Changing chakra properties.
- Maintaining small amounts.
- Performing simple elemental exercises.
- Learning low-complexity elemental jutsu.

Basic proficiency does not mean the character can freely manipulate the element.

---

# 3.4.11 Intermediate Nature Transformation

At **Intermediate**, the shinobi can reliably produce and use that element.

Capabilities may include:

- Combat-usable elemental techniques.
- Moderate-scale transformation.
- Maintaining elemental effects.
- Basic environmental manipulation.
- Elemental weapon enhancement.
- Using the element while moving or under pressure.

This is likely where most ordinary elemental specialists spend much of their careers.

---

# 3.4.12 Advanced Nature Transformation

Advanced practitioners begin manipulating the element with considerable sophistication.

They may:

- Change intensity or properties.
- Shape large amounts reliably.
- Use complex elemental jutsu.
- Maintain transformation under disruption.
- Efficiently combine nature and shape.
- Develop original elemental techniques.

Advanced elemental proficiency should be notably uncommon.

---

# 3.4.13 Expert Nature Transformation

Experts possess exceptional understanding of the nature.

They might:

- Use highly demanding elemental techniques.
- Modify properties during execution.
- Adapt techniques to environmental conditions.
- Reduce unnecessary chakra loss.
- Maintain very large or very precise transformations.
- Create unusual elemental applications.

Expert users can start pushing beyond standard village instruction.

---

# 3.4.14 Master Nature Transformation

A Master represents extraordinary understanding of that chakra nature.

This should enable:

- Extremely refined elemental conversion.
- Exceptional efficiency.
- Highly unusual original techniques.
- Large-scale manipulation where reserves/output permit.
- Extreme precision where Control permits.
- Rapid adaptation.
- Potential development of entirely new applications of the nature.

Again:

> Master Fire Nature Transformation does not automatically mean infinite Fire power.

Reserves, Output, Control, Shape Transformation, and technique mastery remain relevant.

---

# 3.4.15 Training Primary Affinity

A character's primary affinity should be significantly easier to train than other natures.

For example, early Fire training for a Fire-affinity shinobi might progress relatively quickly.

Their instructor may describe it as:

> "The transformation feels natural."

This should translate mechanically into:

- Lower learning difficulty.
- Faster Skill XP under appropriate training.
- Lower early chakra waste.
- Reduced failure severity.

The advantage should become somewhat less dramatic at very high mastery.

Even naturally gifted users must work extremely hard to reach Expert or Master levels.

---

# 3.4.16 Training Secondary Natures

Learning additional natures should require progressively more investment.

The first nature is usually easiest.

The second requires significantly more training.

The third becomes increasingly difficult.

The fourth and fifth should be exceptional accomplishments.

However, I do **not** think we should impose an arbitrary rule like:

> "Only Jōnin may learn a second element."

Rank does not grant elemental ability.

Instead, most Jōnin know multiple natures because they have:

- More years of training.
- Better Attributes.
- Better instruction.
- Greater Skill development.
- Broader mission experience.

Rank correlation emerges naturally.

---

# 3.4.17 Additional Nature Difficulty

Learning multiple elements should face two separate challenges:

**Compatibility Difficulty**

How naturally the element suits the character.

**Development Load**

How much time and practice is required to maintain proficiency in multiple complex Skills.

This means even an exceptionally talented character has limited training time.

Learning five elements comes at the opportunity cost of not specializing as deeply elsewhere.

That is a much better balancing mechanism than arbitrary hard caps.

---

# 3.4.18 Elemental Breadth vs. Specialization

The system should strongly support both.

### Broad Elementalist

> Fire — Advanced II  
> Water — Advanced I  
> Earth — Intermediate V  
> Lightning — Intermediate IV  
> Wind — Intermediate III

Extremely versatile.

### Specialist

> Fire — Master III  
> Earth — Basic II

Far narrower but dramatically more refined in their specialty.

Neither should automatically be superior.

The environment, mission, opponent, and jutsu selection determine which matters more.

---

# 3.4.19 Learning Through Jutsu vs. Learning the Nature

A character should not simply learn an elemental jutsu while having no ability to produce its element.

There are two pathways:

**Nature-first training**

Learn elemental transformation, then learn jutsu.

**Integrated training**

Develop the nature while learning a particular technique.

The second can work for simple jutsu but may result in narrow proficiency.

For example, someone may learn one basic Fire technique while their general Fire Nature Transformation remains poor.

This creates believable cases where characters know a technique without being broadly skilled elementalists.

---

# 3.4.20 Nature Transformation Prerequisites

Elemental jutsu should have minimum Nature Transformation requirements.

Example:

> Fire Release: Great Fireball Technique  
> Fire Nature Transformation: Basic III  
> Chakra Control: Basic IV  
> Output: Basic III  
> Individual Jutsu Mastery: Unlearned → trainable

A more advanced technique might require:

> Fire Nature Transformation: Advanced II  
> Chakra Control: Intermediate V  
> Output: Advanced I

Requirements should reflect actual mechanics rather than rank alone.

---

# 3.4.21 Elemental Power Is Not Just Nature Skill

The strength of an elemental technique should depend on combinations of:

- Nature Transformation.
- Chakra Output.
- Chakra investment.
- Chakra Control.
- Shape Transformation.
- Jutsu mastery.
- Relevant Attributes.
- Environment.

Nature Skill determines **quality of transformation**, not raw energy by itself.

---

# 3.4.22 Elemental Purity

Higher Nature Transformation can produce increasingly stable and refined chakra.

Poor elemental transformation might result in:

- Weak flames.
- Unstable lightning.
- Crumbling earth.
- Poorly controlled water.
- Dispersed wind.

Higher mastery produces more consistent elemental characteristics.

This gives Nature Transformation direct mechanical value beyond meeting prerequisites.

---

# 3.4.23 Elemental Properties

At higher levels, users should learn to manipulate properties of their element.

Examples:

### Fire
- Temperature.
- Burn persistence.
- Spread.
- Concentration.

### Wind
- Sharpness.
- Pressure.
- Dispersion.
- Directional flow.

### Lightning
- Voltage/intensity.
- Concentration.
- Conductivity.
- Neuromuscular disruption.

### Earth
- Density.
- Hardness.
- Shape.
- Stability.

### Water
- Pressure.
- Flow.
- Volume distribution.
- Cohesion.

These are **applications of elemental mastery**, not separate Attributes.

---

# 3.4.24 Canon Elements Do Not Have Fixed Real-World Physics

Naruto elements should remain internally consistent without becoming a physics simulator.

For example:

Lightning Release does not require us to calculate volts and amperage.

Fire Release does not require thermodynamic simulation.

Instead, real-world principles can guide believable interactions while Naruto's chakra mechanics remain primary.

This protects us from unnecessary scope creep.

---

# 3.4.25 Elemental Advantage

The traditional elemental cycle remains:

> **Fire > Wind > Lightning > Earth > Water > Fire**

But this should represent a **natural interaction advantage**, not automatic victory.

A weaker advantageous element can still lose to a dramatically stronger technique.

For example:

A tiny Water Release technique should not automatically extinguish an enormous Fire Release attack merely because Water has the advantage.

Resolution should consider:

- Technique strength.
- Chakra investment.
- Output.
- Mastery.
- Scale.
- Environmental circumstances.
- Elemental advantage.

Elemental affinity becomes one factor among several.

---

# 3.4.26 Strength of Elemental Advantage

Elemental advantage should matter most when two techniques are reasonably comparable.

If two techniques are close in effective strength:

> Advantage can decide the interaction.

If one is overwhelmingly stronger:

> Raw difference can overcome the disadvantage.

This prevents elemental matchups from becoming rigid rock-paper-scissors combat.

---

# 3.4.27 Elemental Interactions Beyond the Main Cycle

We should allow logical secondary interactions.

Examples:

- Lightning interacting strongly with water.
- Fire consuming flammable terrain.
- Earth blocking physical projectiles.
- Wind spreading fire.
- Water altering terrain.
- Lightning traveling through conductive surfaces.

These should emerge from circumstances and technique design.

They are not universal bonuses.

---

# 3.4.28 Environmental Sources

Some elemental techniques can use existing material.

Examples:

- Water from a lake.
- Earth from the ground.
- Existing fire.
- Conductive metal.
- Strong natural wind.

Using environmental material can potentially:

- Reduce chakra cost.
- Reduce Output requirements.
- Increase available scale.
- Speed execution.

But the technique must actually be designed to use the environment.

---

# 3.4.29 Creating the Element From Chakra

Characters should generally be capable of producing elemental effects directly from transformed chakra where Naruto mechanics allow it.

This prevents:

> Water Release is impossible because you're indoors.

However, creating large quantities from chakra should usually be more expensive than manipulating abundant existing material.

This creates meaningful environmental advantages without making characters helpless outside the right terrain.

---

# 3.4.30 Environment Should Not Become Mandatory Micromanagement

The simulation should track environmental effects when meaningful.

We should not constantly calculate:

> humidity +7%  
> Fire cost +1.3%

Instead, the engine should recognize broader situations:

> Heavy rain significantly hinders this Fire technique.

or:

> Fighting beside a lake substantially reduces the resource burden of this Water technique.

This keeps the system readable.

---

# 3.4.31 Yin Release

**Yin Release** should be treated as a special chakra transformation associated broadly with spiritual energy, form, imagination, and nonphysical manipulation.

It should not simply be a sixth elemental nature.

Potential applications include:

- Genjutsu foundations.
- Mental/spiritual effects.
- Creating form from chakra.
- Certain Hiden techniques.
- Specialized sealing effects.

Yin Release should have its own trainable Skill where relevant:

> **Yin Transformation — Basic/Intermediate/etc.**

Most characters may use minor Yin-related chakra functions without formally mastering Yin Release as a discipline.

---

# 3.4.32 Yang Release

**Yang Release** broadly concerns physical energy, vitality, and giving life/physical substance to chakra effects.

Potential applications include:

- Body enhancement.
- Healing.
- Growth-related abilities.
- Certain clan techniques.
- Vitality manipulation.

Again, ordinary chakra use can involve Yang components without the character being an advanced Yang Release practitioner.

Formal Yang mastery should be specialized.

---

# 3.4.33 Yin–Yang Release

Yin–Yang Release should represent an exceptionally advanced combination of both principles.

It should be:

- Extremely rare.
- Extremely difficult.
- Usually dependent on extraordinary prerequisites.
- Mechanically distinct from merely knowing both Yin and Yang techniques.

A character having:

> Advanced Yin  
> Advanced Yang

does **not automatically unlock Yin–Yang Release**.

There must be specific knowledge, capability, or special circumstances.

---

# 3.4.34 Combined Elemental Releases

Some characters can combine two chakra natures into a new transformation.

Examples include:

- Ice Release.
- Lava Release.
- Boil Release.
- Magnet Release.
- Storm Release.

These should generally use a **Combined Nature Skill**.

Example:

> **Ice Release Transformation — Intermediate II**

Rather than simply averaging:

> Wind + Water.

The component Nature Skills remain prerequisites, but the combined transformation becomes its own discipline.

---

# 3.4.35 Kekkei Genkai Requirement

Knowing both component elements is **not enough** to create a genetic combined release.

For example:

> Water mastery + Wind mastery ≠ Ice Release

unless the character possesses the required Kekkei Genkai or another legitimate mechanism allowing that combination.

This protects the meaning of bloodline abilities.

---

# 3.4.36 Non-Genetic Element Combinations

Characters should still be capable of combining ordinary techniques without possessing a Kekkei Genkai.

For example:

One character uses:

> Wind Release

to intensify another person's:

> Fire Release.

Or one shinobi individually casts a Water technique followed by Lightning Release.

That is **combination tactics**, not a combined chakra nature.

The distinction matters.

---

# 3.4.37 Artificial Combined Natures

The world may sometimes produce artificial methods for accessing combined transformations.

Potential sources:

- Genetic experimentation.
- Transplantation.
- Scientific ninja tools.
- Chakra implants.
- Seals.
- Special training methods.

These should carry their own compatibility, risk, and rarity rules.

They do not invalidate natural Kekkei Genkai.

---

# 3.4.38 Kekkei Tōta

Three-nature transformations such as Dust Release should require:

- All relevant Nature Transformation prerequisites.
- The actual special ability/heritage necessary.
- Extremely advanced control.
- Specific combined-transformation mastery.

They should be dramatically rarer than ordinary dual-nature Kekkei Genkai.

Again:

> Mastering three normal elements does not automatically create a Kekkei Tōta.

---

# 3.

### Assistant — f78196b8-c23c-4e15-88ec-e1b7bf2d2e83

**Capture warning: truncated at the 20,000-character reader limit; the ending is missing.**

## Ruleset 3.5 — Shape Transformation

Shape Transformation governs **how chakra is given form, structure, movement, density, and behavior**.

Where Nature Transformation answers:

> **“What kind of chakra is this?”**

Shape Transformation answers:

> **“What form does that chakra take, and how does it behave?”**

This should be one of the core technical skills behind advanced ninjutsu.

---

### 3.5.1 Shape Transformation Is Its Own Skill

Shape Transformation should be separate from:

- Chakra Control
- Chakra Output
- Nature Transformation
- Individual Jutsu Mastery

A character can therefore be excellent at one and mediocre at another.

For example:

> Fire Nature Transformation: Expert I  
> Shape Transformation: Intermediate II

This shinobi may produce exceptionally strong Fire chakra but struggle to turn it into highly precise or complex forms.

Another shinobi might have:

> Fire Nature Transformation: Intermediate III  
> Shape Transformation: Expert II

They may lack the same elemental refinement but compensate with highly sophisticated shaping.

---

## 3.5.2 Skill Progression

Shape Transformation is important enough to use a substantial progression track:

> **Basic I–V**  
> **Intermediate I–V**  
> **Advanced I–V**  
> **Expert I–V**  
> **Master I–V**

Unlike Chakra Control, I don't think it needs Master I–VII by default.

Chakra Control remains unusually broad and universally relevant.

Shape Transformation is extremely important, but more specialized.

---

# 3.5.3 What Shape Transformation Governs

The Skill can influence:

- Forming chakra into stable shapes.
- Maintaining those shapes.
- Altering size.
- Changing direction.
- Controlling density.
- Rotating chakra.
- Compressing chakra.
- Separating chakra into multiple structures.
- Controlling internal movement.
- Creating edges or points.
- Producing streams, spheres, fields, blades, shells, waves, threads, and similar forms.
- Changing shape after a technique has already been created.

It governs **form**, not necessarily elemental properties.

---

# 3.5.4 Shape Complexity

Every technique involving chakra form should have a **Shape Complexity**.

A rough conceptual scale might include:

### Very Simple
Basic directed chakra.

Examples:
- Chakra coating.
- Straight chakra discharge.
- Simple reinforcement.

### Simple
One stable geometric or directional form.

Examples:
- Sphere.
- Blade-like edge.
- Straight projectile.
- Basic stream.

### Moderate
Multiple shape requirements or dynamic form.

Examples:
- Expanding projectile.
- Curved trajectory.
- Controlled chakra threads.
- Rotating structure.

### Complex
Multiple interacting processes.

Examples:
- Constant internal rotation.
- Layered shells.
- Numerous independently moving components.
- Shape changes after release.

### Extreme
Highly unstable or simultaneous shaping operations.

Examples:
- Dense rotating chakra structures.
- Massive multi-layer constructs.
- Dynamic techniques requiring constant real-time correction.

Ruleset 2 would determine the actual difficulty of performing them.

---

# 3.5.5 Shape Stability

Creating a shape and maintaining it are different challenges.

A character may successfully produce:

> a chakra sphere

but fail to keep it stable while:

- moving,
- being attacked,
- increasing its size,
- increasing output,
- altering its direction.

Therefore techniques can have both:

> **Shape Creation Difficulty**  
> **Shape Maintenance Difficulty**

For simple jutsu, these may effectively be the same.

Advanced techniques may make the distinction important.

---

# 3.5.6 Shape and Chakra Control

Chakra Control and Shape Transformation should interact heavily without becoming redundant.

**Chakra Control** determines:

> how accurately the user manipulates chakra.

**Shape Transformation** determines:

> how effectively the user creates and maintains specific structures.

A medical-nin with extraordinary Chakra Control may still lack advanced Shape Transformation because most of their training focuses on precision rather than complex chakra geometry.

Likewise, a Shape Transformation specialist may create sophisticated forms but waste more chakra than a control specialist.

---

# 3.5.7 Shape and Chakra Output

Increasing chakra flow makes shape harder to maintain.

A shinobi might easily hold:

> 5 CP

in a precise sphere.

At:

> 30 CP

the same sphere may become much harder to stabilize.

At:

> 80 CP

it may violently destabilize.

This means high-power shaped techniques often require:

> **Output + Control + Shape Transformation**

not merely large chakra reserves.

---

# 3.5.8 Shape Density

Chakra can sometimes be compressed into denser forms.

Greater density may increase:

- Penetration.
- Impact.
- Stability.
- Energy concentration.
- Resistance to dispersion.

But compression increases difficulty.

High-density techniques therefore commonly require:

> strong Shape Transformation  
> high Chakra Control  
> sufficient Output

This gives us a mechanical foundation for concentrated techniques without simply labeling them “stronger.”

---

# 3.5.9 Compression Has Limits

A character cannot endlessly compress chakra into an arbitrarily tiny point.

Limits depend on:

- Chakra Control.
- Shape Transformation.
- Chakra Output.
- Technique design.
- User physiology.
- Chakra properties.

Attempting excessive compression can lead to:

- Instability.
- Chakra leakage.
- Premature release.
- Injury.
- Technique collapse.

---

# 3.5.10 Rotation

Rotational manipulation should be one specialized form of Shape Transformation.

It can affect:

- Stability.
- Cutting force.
- Defensive deflection.
- Internal pressure.
- Impact behavior.

The Rasengan is an obvious example of a technique where **rotation, containment, and high-density chakra must be controlled simultaneously**.

That is why it can remain extremely difficult even though it has no elemental transformation requirement.

---

# 3.5.11 Directional Control

Shape Transformation should also govern how chakra moves after being formed.

Possible levels include:

- Straight-line release.
- Simple arc.
- User-guided trajectory.
- Mid-flight correction.
- Multiple independent trajectories.
- Complex pursuit behavior.

However, autonomous tracking may require more than Shape Transformation alone.

Depending on the jutsu, it might also require:

- Chakra Control.
- Sensory ability.
- Sealing logic.
- Special techniques.
- Intelligence-based targeting mechanisms.

---

# 3.5.12 Shape After Release

Some techniques become independent once launched.

Others remain connected to the user.

This should be a technique property.

### Released Shape

Once fired, the user loses direct control.

Example:
- Conventional projectile.

### Guided Shape

The user continues manipulating it.

Example:
- A controllable chakra projectile.

### Tethered Shape

The technique remains connected through chakra.

Example:
- Chakra threads.

### Autonomous Shape

The technique follows predefined or special rules.

Example:
- Certain constructs or tracking effects.

More control after release usually increases difficulty and ongoing chakra demand.

---

# 3.5.13 Multiple Shapes

Creating several structures simultaneously should be harder than creating one.

For example:

> One chakra blade

is easier than:

> eight independently controlled chakra blades.

The burden may increase based on:

- Number of structures.
- Whether they move independently.
- Whether each requires active attention.
- Whether they use identical or different shapes.
- User mastery.

Highly mastered repeated structures can become much easier to maintain.

---

# 3.5.14 Repeated Identical Shapes

Producing multiple copies of the same simple shape should be easier than controlling multiple unique structures.

For example:

> Five identical senbon-shaped chakra projectiles

is easier than simultaneously controlling:

> a shield, whip, spear, sphere, and net.

This lets characters specialize in particular patterns.

---

# 3.5.15 Shape Transformation and Scale

Larger shapes are not always more complex.

A massive simple wall may require:

> high chakra expenditure and Output

but only moderate Shape Transformation.

A tiny, intricate chakra needle may require:

> low chakra expenditure

but very high Shape Transformation.

This distinction is important.

**Scale ≠ complexity.**

---

# 3.5.16 Shape Efficiency

Better Shape Transformation should reduce the chakra wasted maintaining unstable forms.

However, Chakra Control remains the primary general efficiency Skill.

Shape mastery specifically improves efficiency associated with:

- Maintaining structure.
- Preventing dispersion.
- Avoiding deformation.
- Keeping chakra where intended.

This creates synergy without overlap.

---

# 3.5.17 Shape Transformation Training

Training methods might include:

- Holding chakra in a sphere.
- Maintaining shapes while moving.
- Forming increasingly complex geometric patterns.
- Chakra thread exercises.
- Rotational chakra exercises.
- Controlling several shapes simultaneously.
- Compressing chakra.
- Changing shape without interrupting flow.
- Sustaining shapes under increasing output.

Original training methods should be allowed when mechanically sensible.

---

# 3.5.18 Progressive Shape Training

A plausible progression could be:

> Stable chakra gathering  
> → simple form  
> → stable form during movement  
> → multiple forms  
> → dynamic reshaping  
> → rotation/compression  
> → high-output shaping  
> → multiple simultaneous advanced processes

This is not a mandatory curriculum.

Different villages, clans, teachers, and characters can approach it differently.

---

# 3.5.19 External Aids

Tools can assist Shape Transformation.

Examples:

- Molds.
- Seals.
- Weapons.
- Chakra-conductive materials.
- Puppets.
- Scientific ninja tools.

An external structure may reduce how much shaping the user must perform personally.

For example:

A sword can physically provide the shape for chakra coating, making it much easier than creating a free-floating chakra blade of identical dimensions.

---

# 3.5.20 Weapons as Shape Anchors

This should be an important concept.

Chakra flowed into a weapon has an existing physical structure to follow.

That can reduce Shape Transformation requirements.

A character might therefore be capable of:

> chakra-enhancing a sword

long before they can create:

> a stable sword-shaped chakra construct without a weapon.

This makes equipment mechanically meaningful.

---

# 3.5.21 Shape and Elemental Transformation

Nature and shape can be trained independently.

Combining them creates additional difficulty.

A Fire user might first learn to:

> create Fire chakra.

Then:

> release Fire chakra.

Then:

> shape it into a projectile.

Then:

> precisely control that projectile's dimensions and movement.

Each step adds complexity.

---

# 3.5.22 Combined Nature + Shape Difficulty

An elemental jutsu may therefore require:

> Fire Nature Transformation: Intermediate III  
> Shape Transformation: Basic V  
> Chakra Control: Intermediate II  
> Chakra Output: Intermediate III

Another technique could require:

> Wind Transformation: Advanced II  
> Shape Transformation: Expert I  
> Chakra Control: Advanced IV  
> Output: Intermediate IV

Even two Wind jutsu can therefore demand completely different skill profiles.

---

# 3.5.23 Nature and Shape Can Conflict

Some elemental properties may make certain shapes harder.

For example:

- Highly unstable lightning may resist delicate shaping.
- Expanding flame may resist tight containment.
- Flowing water may naturally support some shapes but resist others.
- Dense earth may be easier to maintain once formed but harder to reshape rapidly.

This allows elements to feel mechanically distinct.

We should use these differences carefully rather than assigning huge universal modifiers.

---

# 3.5.24 Pure Shape Techniques

Some jutsu use shape manipulation without an elemental nature.

These should be fully viable.

Examples can include:

- Rasengan-type techniques.
- Pure chakra blades.
- Chakra shields.
- Chakra threads.
- Chakra spheres.
- Certain barriers.

This is especially important for characters without strong elemental specialization.

---

# 3.5.25 Shape Transformation and Rasengan-Type Techniques

A Rasengan-like technique is a good example of extreme Shape Transformation because the user must simultaneously manage:

- Chakra concentration.
- Rotation.
- Direction.
- Containment.
- Density.
- Stability.

Its difficulty comes from combining several shape operations, not from elemental transformation.

Adding an element then creates another layer:

> Shape Transformation  
> + Nature Transformation  
> + Control  
> + Output  
> + Technique Mastery

This gives us a natural mechanical explanation for why such techniques are so difficult.

---

# 3.5.26 Shape Specialization

Characters can become unusually skilled with certain shape families.

Examples:

- Chakra threads.
- Blades.
- Spheres.
- Barriers.
- Projectiles.
- Constructs.
- Whips.
- Rotational techniques.

These do not need to become separate universal Skills unless a discipline is large enough to justify it.

Most can be represented through:

> Shape Transformation + technique mastery + specialization traits

---

# 3.5.27 Complex Constructs

Large chakra constructs should require more than simple Shape Transformation when appropriate.

A humanoid construct, animal-shaped construct, or moving chakra structure may require:

- Advanced Shape Transformation.
- High Chakra Control.
- Sustained Output.
- Large reserves.
- Specialized knowledge.

If it acts independently, that may require an additional technique-specific mechanism.

Shape alone does not create intelligence.

---

# 3.5.28 Shape-Based Defense

Defensive shaping may include:

- Shields.
- Shells.
- Walls.
- Domes.
- Redirecting surfaces.

Defense effectiveness should depend on:

- Chakra invested.
- Shape stability.
- Density.
- Element.
- Technique mastery.
- Impact angle.
- Opposing technique strength.

A perfectly shaped shield with almost no chakra behind it is still weak.

---

# 3.5.29 Shape-Based Offense

Offensive shaping may increase effectiveness through:

- Sharpness.
- Compression.
- Rotation.
- Focused impact.
- Penetration.
- Multi-angle attacks.

Again, Shape Transformation changes **how chakra is applied**, not how much energy exists.

---

# 3.5.30 Sharpness

Chakra edges should depend heavily on Shape Transformation.

A poorly formed chakra blade may be:

- Thick.
- Unstable.
- Blunt.
- Uneven.

A highly refined blade can become:

- Thin.
- Stable.
- Concentrated.
- Extremely effective.

Wind Release can enhance cutting properties, but pure chakra shaping can still create cutting forms where appropriate.

---

# 3.5.31 Internal Shape Complexity

Some techniques may look simple externally while being incredibly complex internally.

A sphere might contain:

- Multiple rotational directions.
- Layered chakra flow.
- Internal compression.
- An outer containment shell.

Therefore visual simplicity should not determine mechanical difficulty.

Technique records should be able to store **internal Shape Complexity**.

---

# 3.5.32 Shape Transformation Under Pressure

Combat conditions can interfere with shaping.

Relevant factors include:

- Movement.
- Injury.
- Distraction.
- Sudden Output changes.
- Chakra disruption.
- Environmental interference.
- Enemy attacks.

Highly mastered techniques eventually become resilient to these conditions.

---

# 3.5.33 Shape Failure

Failed Shape Transformation can produce different outcomes:

**Deformation**  
The jutsu activates in the wrong form.

**Dispersion**  
Chakra escapes before completion.

**Instability**  
The shape forms but becomes difficult to maintain.

**Reduced Precision**  
The technique works with worse accuracy or penetration.

**Premature Release**  
The technique fires early.

**Collapse**  
The structure completely fails.

**Backlash**  
Compressed or unstable chakra harms the user.

The technique determines which failures are plausible.

---

# 3.5.34 Shape Failure and Chakra Cost

A failed shaping attempt may still consume chakra.

How much depends on when failure occurs.

For example:

> Failure before chakra gathering: almost no loss.

> Failure during shaping: partial loss.

> Failure immediately before release: most or all chakra may be lost.

This creates meaningful consequences without every failed jutsu costing the full amount automatically.

---

# 3.5.35 Shape and Hand Seals

Hand seals can assist Shape Transformation by guiding chakra into predefined patterns.

A jutsu with a complicated shape may use seals partly because they:

- Establish flow.
- Sequence transformations.
- Stabilize structure.

Greater mastery may allow seal reduction because the user internalizes those processes.

This reinforces the hand-seal rules we established earlier.

---

# 3.5.36 Shape and Jutsu Mastery

General Shape Transformation helps across techniques.

Individual mastery remains critical.

A character may have:

> Shape Transformation: Advanced III

but still initially struggle with a completely unfamiliar advanced shape.

After mastering the technique, execution becomes much easier.

Therefore:

> **General Skill determines capability.  
> Technique Mastery determines familiarity and optimization.**

---

# 3.5.37 Shape Adaptation

High mastery should allow characters to alter learned jutsu.

Examples:

- Make a projectile narrower.
- Change its trajectory.
- Widen a shield.
- Split one projectile into several.
- Compress an attack.
- Extend a blade.
- Reshape a stream.

However, modifications beyond the technique's normal design increase difficulty and may eventually count as a new variant.

---

# 3.5.38 Improvised Shaping

Characters should sometimes be able to manipulate chakra without using a formal named jutsu.

For example:

> extending a small chakra edge from a weapon

could be a general application rather than a named technique.

The more complicated the effect becomes, the more likely it should require:

- formal training,
- a developed technique,
- or significant experimentation.

This prevents the system from requiring a named jutsu for every tiny chakra action.

---

# 3.5.39 Named Jutsu vs. General Application

This distinction should be explicit.

### General Chakra Application
Simple enough to perform directly through Skills.

Examples:
- Minor chakra coating.
- Basic shaping.
- Simple surface adhesion.

### Formal Jutsu
A refined, repeatable technique with standardized execution.

Formal techniques gain benefits from:

- Jutsu mastery.
- Efficient execution.
- Reliable hand-seal patterns.
- Established training methods.

This gives formal jutsu a reason to exist even when freeform chakra manipulation is possible.

---

# 3.5.40 Original Shape Techniques

Our non-canon principle applies strongly here.

Characters can invent original shape-based techniques if they possess the required:

- Shape Transformation.
- Chakra Control.
- Output.
- Knowledge.
- Time.
- Experimentation.

For example, someone might develop:

> a rotating chakra disc,

> a flexible chakra wire,

> a layered chakra shield,

or:

> several orbiting defensive spheres.

The simulation should evaluate the mechanics rather than rejecting them because they are not canon.

---

# 3.5.41 Novelty Does Not Remove Requirements

A character cannot simply declare:

> “I shape chakra into an infinitely thin blade that cuts everything.”

The system asks:

- How thin?
- How stable?
- How much chakra?
- What density?
- What Shape Transformation is required?
- What happens against stronger chakra reinforcement?
- How long can it be sustained?
- What prevents collapse?

Original techniques must still obey established mechanics.

---

# 3.5.42 Shape Training and Development Speed

Progress should depend primarily on:

- Chakra Control.
- Chakra 

---

## Ruleset 3.6 — Jutsu Classification & Data Model

This section is where we turn everything we've built so far into a standardized system.

Every jutsu—canon, canon-derived, or original—should be represented through the same underlying framework.

That matters because otherwise individual techniques become inconsistent exceptions.

The goal should be:

> **Every technique is defined by what it requires, what it costs, what it does, how it scales, and how it can fail or be countered.**

The system should not need to “know” that a technique is powerful just because it is famous.

Its properties should explain why it is powerful.

---

# 3.6.1 A Jutsu Is a Structured Technique Record

Every formal jutsu should have a standardized record.

At minimum, that record should include:

- Name
- Classification
- Rank
- Discipline/category
- Chakra nature
- Shape requirements
- Chakra cost
- Output requirement
- Chakra Control requirement
- Skill requirements
- Attribute prerequisites
- Hand seals
- Activation time
- Range
- Area
- Duration
- Maintenance
- Scaling
- Targeting
- Environmental dependencies
- Risks
- Failure behavior
- Counterplay
- Learnability
- Source/access
- Mastery
- Tags

Not every technique needs every field to be important.

A very simple taijutsu technique might have no chakra nature.

A sealing technique may have no direct damage.

A passive special ability may not use hand seals.

But the same framework should still be able to describe all of them.

---

# 3.6.2 Jutsu Rank

The standard jutsu ranks remain:

> **E**  
> **D**  
> **C**  
> **B**  
> **A**  
> **S**

Rank should represent the **overall difficulty, danger, complexity, training burden, and expected shinobi expertise required to use the technique effectively**.

It should not simply mean:

> higher rank = more damage.

A non-damaging sealing technique could be A-rank.

A highly destructive but mechanically simple technique might be B-rank.

A dangerous self-sacrificial technique could be S-rank despite not being broadly useful.

---

# 3.6.3 Rank Is a Summary, Not a Requirement System

A jutsu rank should be a convenient summary of its difficulty.

It should not replace real prerequisites.

For example:

> **A-rank**

does not mechanically mean:

> “Must be Jōnin.”

Instead, the technique itself may require:

> Chakra Control: Advanced IV  
> Output: Advanced III  
> Nature Transformation: Advanced II  
> Minimum Chakra Attribute: 62  
> Jutsu knowledge prerequisites

A very talented Chūnin who meets those requirements could theoretically learn it.

Likewise, a Jōnin who does not meet them cannot simply learn it because of rank.

---

# 3.6.4 Ninja Rank and Jutsu Rank

Ninja rank and jutsu rank should correlate socially, not mechanically.

Villages may typically restrict:

- E/D-rank to Academy/Genin
- C-rank to Genin/Chūnin
- B-rank to Chūnin/Jōnin
- A-rank to Jōnin
- S-rank to elite specialists

But these are **training and access norms**, not universal laws.

Reasons someone might know an unusually advanced jutsu include:

- Clan teaching
- Exceptional talent
- Specialized mentoring
- Theft
- Battlefield necessity
- Forbidden research
- Personal invention
- Unusual inheritance

---

# 3.6.5 E-Rank Techniques

E-rank techniques should generally represent:

- Academy basics
- Foundational chakra exercises
- Very simple utility techniques
- Techniques with low danger
- Techniques requiring little specialization

They should not necessarily be useless.

A highly mastered low-rank technique can remain useful for an entire career.

---

# 3.6.6 D-Rank Techniques

D-rank techniques generally represent:

- Basic field techniques
- Low-complexity combat jutsu
- Basic support techniques
- Basic elemental techniques
- Techniques intended for early Genin-level application

They usually require modest control and low output.

---

# 3.6.7 C-Rank Techniques

C-rank techniques represent competent shinobi techniques.

They may require:

- Multiple Skills
- Moderate output
- Meaningful elemental transformation
- Better combat execution
- More complex hand seals

These should form a large portion of an ordinary shinobi's usable arsenal.

---

# 3.6.8 B-Rank Techniques

B-rank techniques represent high-complexity or high-impact abilities.

They often require:

- Advanced Skill investment
- Higher chakra cost
- More dangerous execution
- Better mastery
- More complicated tactical use

Many career shinobi may never become broadly proficient with many B-rank techniques.

---

# 3.6.9 A-Rank Techniques

A-rank techniques should be genuinely elite.

They may involve:

- Advanced transformation
- High output
- Significant risk
- Exceptional precision
- Multiple simultaneous chakra processes
- Rare specialized knowledge

A character knowing one A-rank technique does not automatically mean they broadly operate at A-rank in every discipline.

---

# 3.6.10 S-Rank Techniques

S-rank should be reserved for techniques that are truly exceptional in some combination of:

- Complexity
- Power
- Danger
- Rarity
- Required expertise
- Consequences
- Restricted knowledge

S-rank should remain rare enough that it actually means something.

A technique should not be S-rank just because it looks dramatic.

---

# 3.6.11 Kinjutsu Is Separate From Rank

**Forbidden Technique** should be a tag, not a rank.

A technique may be forbidden because it:

- Kills the user
- Damages lifespan
- Requires human sacrifice
- Violates village law
- Uses prohibited experimentation
- Poses unacceptable collateral risk
- Requires stolen biological material
- Threatens public safety

Therefore:

> D-rank Kinjutsu is possible.

And:

> S-rank does not automatically mean forbidden.

---

# 3.6.12 Jutsu Classification

A technique should have one or more broad classifications.

Examples:

- Ninjutsu
- Genjutsu
- Taijutsu
- Medical Ninjutsu
- Fūinjutsu
- Barrier Ninjutsu
- Sensory Technique
- Summoning
- Space-Time Ninjutsu
- Bukijutsu
- Puppetry
- Chakra Flow
- Hiden
- Kinjutsu
- Dōjutsu Technique
- Transformation Technique
- Clone Technique

These should act as **tags and training families**.

A technique may belong to multiple categories.

For example:

> Medical Ninjutsu + Chakra Flow

or:

> Fūinjutsu + Barrier Ninjutsu

---

# 3.6.13 Primary and Secondary Classification

Each jutsu should have:

> **Primary Classification**

and optionally:

> **Secondary Classifications**

For example:

> Primary: Ninjutsu  
> Secondary: Fire Release, Projectile

or:

> Primary: Medical Ninjutsu  
> Secondary: Chakra Flow, Support

This keeps the data readable without losing complexity.

---

# 3.6.14 Technique Role Tags

We should also track role tags separate from classification.

Examples:

- Offensive
- Defensive
- Support
- Mobility
- Utility
- Control
- Detection
- Concealment
- Healing
- Debuff
- Area Denial
- Capture
- Escape
- Tracking

These are useful for:

- NPC decision-making
- AI combat logic
- Mission planning
- Search/filtering

---

# 3.6.15 Chakra Nature Field

Every jutsu should indicate its nature requirement.

Examples:

> Nature: None

> Nature: Fire

> Nature: Water + Wind

> Nature: Yin

> Nature: Yang

> Nature: Special / Kekkei Genkai

If multiple natures are involved, the technique must specify whether they are:

- sequentially used
- simultaneously combined
- truly fused into a special release

That distinction is important.

---

# 3.6.16 Shape Complexity Field

Each technique should track:

> **Shape Complexity**

Possible abstract categories:

- None
- Very Low
- Low
- Moderate
- High
- Very High
- Extreme

Internally this can map to Skill thresholds.

This tells the engine how much Shape Transformation matters.

---

# 3.6.17 Chakra Control Requirement

Each jutsu should define the minimum Chakra Control required for competent execution.

For example:

> Chakra Control Requirement: Intermediate II

This is a threshold, not a guarantee.

Below the threshold:

- learning is extremely difficult
- execution may be unstable
- costs may increase
- failure consequences may worsen

Above the threshold:

- reliability improves
- efficiency improves
- execution becomes more flexible

---

# 3.6.18 Output Requirement

Techniques should specify whether they need:

- Burst Output
- Sustained Output
- Both

Example:

> Burst Output Requirement: Advanced I  
> Sustained Output Requirement: Intermediate II

This allows a technique to have:

- a difficult activation
- but relatively easy maintenance

or the reverse.

---

# 3.6.19 Chakra Cost

Chakra cost should not be one static number for every technique.

A technique can include:

### Base Cost
Minimum chakra needed to activate it.

### Variable Cost
Additional chakra invested to scale the effect.

### Maintenance Cost
Chakra consumed over time.

### Expansion Cost
Chakra required for greater range, area, targets, etc.

### Failure Cost
How much chakra is lost if execution fails.

This is more useful than a single “Costs 30 CP.”

---

# 3.6.20 Minimum Functional Cost

Every technique should also have a **minimum functional cost**.

This represents the lowest amount of chakra that can still produce the technique's intended effect.

A highly skilled character may reduce inefficiency.

They cannot reduce the technique below the physical minimum necessary to function.

---

# 3.6.21 Chakra Cost Scaling

Some techniques should allow variable investment.

For example:

> Base Cost: 12 CP  
> Additional Investment: up to +30 CP  
> Scaling: Moderate

This allows the technique to be used at different strengths.

Others may be fixed:

> Cost: 18 CP  
> Scaling: None

Fixed techniques remain useful because they are predictable and efficient.

---

# 3.6.22 Scaling Profile

Every jutsu should have one of several scaling profiles.

### Fixed
Additional chakra gives little or no benefit.

### Limited
Some improvement is possible.

### Moderate
Meaningful scaling within a normal range.

### High
Designed for large differences in chakra investment.

### Open-Ended
Rare techniques specifically designed to scale dramatically.

Even open-ended scaling still respects Output, Control, and stability limits.

---

# 3.6.23 What Can Scale

A technique should define exactly what additional investment improves.

Possible scaling dimensions include:

- Damage
- Force
- Area
- Range
- Duration
- Number of targets
- Defensive durability
- Penetration
- Healing amount
- Sensory range
- Binding strength
- Clone count

Extra chakra should not automatically improve every aspect.

---

# 3.6.24 Attribute Prerequisites

Some techniques should require minimum Attributes.

Examples:

> Agility 40  
> Chakra 55  
> Intelligence 60

These should only be used when the technique genuinely depends on that foundation.

We should avoid putting Attribute requirements on every simple jutsu.

The point is to prevent advanced techniques from being learned by characters whose fundamental capabilities make them implausible.

---

# 3.6.25 Skill Prerequisites

Jutsu should usually rely more heavily on Skills than Attributes.

Possible prerequisites:

- Chakra Control
- Chakra Output
- Shape Transformation
- Fire Nature Transformation
- Fūinjutsu
- Medical Ninjutsu
- Genjutsu
- Handsign Speed
- Sensory Skill
- Weapon Skill

This creates meaningful progression paths.

---

# 3.6.26 Hard vs. Soft Prerequisites

We should distinguish:

### Hard Prerequisite
The technique cannot function without it.

Example:
> Ice Release bloodline required.

### Soft Prerequisite
The technique can theoretically be attempted below the threshold, but with extreme difficulty or risk.

Example:
> Recommended Chakra Control: Advanced II

This allows edge cases without making everything possible.

---

# 3.6.27 Special Ability Prerequisites

Some jutsu require:

- Sharingan
- Byakugan
- Kekkei Genkai
- Summoning contract
- Curse mark
- Jinchūriki status
- Sage Mode
- Clan ability
- Specific implant

These should be explicit.

A character cannot reproduce a technique simply because they understand the chakra pattern if the biology or special ability is missing.

---

# 3.6.28 Knowledge Prerequisites

Some advanced jutsu should require conceptual knowledge.

Examples:

- Human anatomy
- Seal theory
- Poison chemistry
- Barrier theory
- Space-time theory
- Puppet engineering

This matters because not every difficulty is about chakra manipulation.

A character may be mechanically capable but simply not understand what they are doing.

---

# 3.6.29 Hand Seals

Each jutsu should define:

- Whether hand seals are required
- Standard number of seals
- Seal sequence complexity
- Whether reduced-seal execution is possible
- Whether one-handed seals are possible
- Whether seal-less execution is possible

Example:

> Hand Seals: 6  
> Seal Complexity: Moderate  
> Minimum Mastery for 4-Seal Variant: Proficient  
> Minimum Mastery for 2-Seal Variant: Mastered

This integrates directly with mastery progression.

---

# 3.6.30 Handsign Speed Requirement

Some techniques may require fast seals under combat conditions.

This should interact with:

> Handsign Speed Skill + Agility + mastery

A character may know a technique perfectly but still cast it too slowly to use effectively in combat.

That creates a meaningful distinction between:

> knowing a jutsu

and:

> being combat-proficient with it.

---

# 3.6.31 Activation Time

Every technique should have an activation profile.

Possible categories:

- Instant
- Very Fast
- Fast
- Moderate
- Slow
- Extended Preparation

Activation time can depend on:

- Hand seals
- Chakra gathering
- Shape formation
- Environmental preparation
- Tool setup
- Technique mastery

This should remain flexible rather than attaching fixed seconds to every ability unless needed.

---

# 3.6.32 Preparation

Some techniques require preparation before combat.

Examples:

- Setting seals
- Preparing puppets
- Drawing blood
- Marking locations
- Storing chakra
- Placing tags
- Creating traps

Preparation should reduce the difficulty or cost of certain techniques.

This creates meaningful strategic play outside direct combat.

---

# 3.6.33 Range

Techniques should specify range behavior.

Possible categories:

- Self
- Touch
- Melee
- Short
- Medium
- Long
- Extreme
- Variable

Internally, exact distances can still exist where useful.

Range should not be treated purely as flavor.

Increasing range may:

- increase chakra cost
- increase difficulty
- reduce precision
- require better mastery

---

# 3.6.34 Area of Effect

Techniques should define their area pattern.

Examples:

- Single target
- Line
- Cone
- Sphere
- Radius
- Wall
- Field
- Ring
- Directional arc
- Custom

Area should matter for:

- targeting
- collateral risk
- environmental interaction
- chakra cost

---

# 3.6.35 Targeting Type

Possible targeting models include:

- Self
- Single target
- Multiple targets
- Area
- Object
- Terrain
- Location
- Chakra signature
- Line of sight
- Contact-based

This helps determine how the technique interacts with concealment and movement.

---

# 3.6.36 Duration

A jutsu should specify whether it is:

- Instantaneous
- Brief
- Sustained
- Fixed duration
- Persistent
- Permanent until dispelled

This is especially important for:

- barriers
- seals
- genjutsu
- summons
- transformations
- clones

---

# 3.6.37 Concentration Requirement

Some techniques should require active concentration.

Possible categories:

### None
Once activated, no further attention is needed.

### Light
Can be maintained alongside normal actions.

### Moderate
Maintaining it while fighting is difficult.

### Heavy
The technique dominates attention.

### Total
The user can do almost nothing else.

Higher mastery may reduce concentration demand.

---

# 3.6.38 Maintenance Cost

Sustained techniques should specify their ongoing chakra demand.

Example:

> Activation: 15 CP  
> Maintenance: 2 CP/minute

Or:

> Activation: 30 CP  
> Maintenance: 4 CP/round

The exact timescale can depend on context.

We should avoid excessive turn-by-turn bookkeeping outside combat.

---

# 3.6.39 Environmental Requirements

Some techniques require specific conditions.

Examples:

- Existing water
- Soil
- Open sky
- Conductive surface
- Prepared seal
- Shadow
- Line of sight
- Blood sample
- Corpse
- Summoning contract

These should be explicit technique properties.

---

# 3.6.40 Environmental Modifiers

Some techniques work anywhere but become easier or stronger in favorable conditions.

Example:

> Water Source Bonus: Significant

This may reduce:

- cost
- activation time
- Output requirement

rather than just adding damage.

---

# 3.6.41 Technique Reliability

Each jutsu should have an intrinsic stability.

Possible values:

- Very Stable
- Stable
- Moderate
- Unstable
- Highly Unstable

A simple Academy technique may be extremely stable.

An experimental original technique may be highly unstable.

This affects:

- failure chance
- backlash
- maintenance
- training safety

---

# 3.6.42 Failure Profile

Every complex or dangerous technique should specify what failure means.

Possible outcomes:

- No activation
- Partial activation
- Reduced effect
- Chakra loss
- Misfire
- Backlash
- Injury
- Uncontrolled area
- Technique collapse
- Environmental damage

This is especially important for original techniques.

---

# 3.6.43 Risk Rating

Separate from rank, techniques can have:

> **Risk Rating**

For example:

- Minimal
- Low
- Moderate
- High
- Severe
- Lethal

A low-rank technique can still be dangerous under the wrong conditions.

A high-rank technique can potentially be relatively safe for a qualified user.

---

# 3.6.44 User Risk vs. Target Risk

These should be distinct.

Example:

> User Risk: Severe  
> Target Threat: Extreme

or:

> User Risk: Minimal  
> Target Threat: High

This is especially important for Kinjutsu and transformations.

---

# 3.6.45 Counterplay Tags

Every combat-relevant technique should expose plausible counters.

Examples:

- Dodgeable
- Blockable
- Interruptible
- Dispellable
- Absorbable
- Reflectable
- Sealable
- Detectable
- Breakable
- Elementally countered
- Requires contact
- Requires line of sight

Not every technique needs every counter.

But almost nothing should be completely without interaction.

---

# 3.6.46 Avoid Absolute Effects

Techniques should rarely use language such as:

> “Cannot be dodged.”

> “Cannot be broken.”

> “Always kills.”

Instead they should define extremely high difficulty or specific conditions.

True absolutes should be extraordinarily rare and strongly justified.

This helps keep the simulation coherent.

---

# 3.6.47 Technique Tags

Tags should provide compact mechanical information.

Examples:

- Projectile
- Melee
- Area
- Sustained
- Channeling
- Stealth
- Sensory
- Healing
- Piercing
- Binding
- Environmental
- Bloodline
- Clan
- Forbidden
- Clone
- Transformation
- Summoning
- Barrier
- Seal
- High Output
- Precision
- High Control
- Preparation
- Cooperative

Tags improve both NPC logic and database organization.

---

# 3.6.48 Cooperative Techniques

Some jutsu should allow or require multiple users.

A cooperative technique might specify:

> Minimum Users: 2

and define:

- how chakra costs divide
- whether users need matching natures
- whether one person leads
- what happens if synchronization fails

This supports squad-based shinobi techniques.

---

# 3.6.49 Combination Jutsu

Two techniques can sometimes interact without being a formal cooperative jutsu.

Example:

> Wind Release amplifies Fire Release.

The system should recognize:

**Formal Combination Technique**

versus:

**Emergent Technique Interaction**

This leaves room for creative combat.

---

# 3.6.50 Learnability

Each technique should have a learning classification.

Possible categories:

### Common
Widely available.

### Restricted
Limited by village or profession.

### Clan
Traditionally controlled by a clan.

### Secret
Known only to a small group.

### Forbidden
Legally prohibited.

### Lost
No

---
