## Ruleset 3.10 — Sustained Techniques, Concentration & Multiple Chakra Processes

This section should answer a simple but very important question:

> **What happens after a jutsu has already been activated?**

Some techniques are finished immediately after release. Others continue for seconds, minutes, hours, or even years.

The system needs to distinguish **duration, chakra upkeep, concentration, continued connection to the user, and independence** rather than treating every ongoing technique the same way.

---

### 3.10.1 Duration and Concentration Are Separate

A technique lasting a long time does not necessarily require constant concentration.

For example:

A seal might remain active for years after being placed.

A barrier might require constant focus for thirty seconds.

Therefore every sustained technique should separately define:

> **Duration**  
> **Maintenance Cost**  
> **Concentration Requirement**  
> **Connection Requirement**

These are different properties.

---

## 3.10.2 Technique Persistence Types

I think sustained effects should fall into several broad types.

### Actively Maintained

The user must continuously supply chakra and/or attention.

Examples may include:

- Certain barriers.
- Chakra threads.
- Continuous healing.
- Some sensory techniques.

If maintenance stops, the effect ends.

### Self-Sustaining

The user activates the technique, after which it can temporarily sustain itself.

It may still have a fixed duration or stored chakra supply.

### Tethered

The technique remains dependent on a chakra connection to the user.

Distance or disruption can break it.

### Autonomous

Once created, the effect can act independently within its defined rules.

### Persistent

The technique remains until a condition ends it.

Examples:

- Certain seals.
- Prepared traps.
- Long-term barriers.

### Permanent Until Broken

Rare effects may remain indefinitely unless removed, destroyed, dispelled, or otherwise resolved.

This should be uncommon and require an actual mechanism.

---

# 3.10.3 Concentration Levels

I propose five concentration levels:

**None**  
The technique requires no meaningful attention once active.

**Light**  
Can easily coexist with normal movement and combat.

**Moderate**  
Requires recurring attention and makes complicated simultaneous actions harder.

**Heavy**  
Consumes a substantial portion of the user's focus.

**Total**  
The user can do very little else while maintaining it.

The exact mechanical penalties are handled through Ruleset 2.

---

# 3.10.4 Concentration Is Not Chakra Control

Chakra Control helps manage sustained techniques, but concentration represents **mental attention**.

A character may have exceptional Chakra Control but still struggle to:

- maintain a complicated barrier,
- track several enemies,
- dodge attacks,
- and perform another jutsu

all at once.

This is where Intelligence, Perception, Willpower, experience, and mastery may also become relevant.

---

# 3.10.5 What Determines Concentration Burden

A technique's effective concentration burden should depend on:

- Base technique complexity.
- Chakra Control.
- Individual jutsu mastery.
- Number of concurrent processes.
- Physical movement.
- Injury.
- Fatigue.
- Emotional disturbance.
- Environmental interference.
- Sensory overload.
- Whether the technique is familiar or improvised.

A deeply mastered technique may eventually become almost automatic.

---

# 3.10.6 Automaticity

This connects directly to what we established under Chakra Control.

A process that once required:

> Moderate concentration

might eventually require:

> Light concentration

or effectively none during ordinary use.

Examples:

- Tree walking.
- Basic chakra suppression.
- Maintaining a familiar transformation.
- A highly practiced chakra coating.

This frees experienced shinobi to perform more complicated actions simultaneously.

---

# 3.10.7 Mastery Can Reduce Concentration

Individual jutsu mastery may reduce concentration burden.

For example:

> Functional: Heavy  
> Proficient: Moderate  
> Mastered: Light

But this should only occur when the technique's mechanics permit it.

Some techniques are intrinsically demanding regardless of familiarity.

---

# 3.10.8 Maintenance Cost

A sustained technique may have an ongoing chakra cost.

For example:

> Activation: 20 CP  
> Maintenance: 3 CP/minute

or:

> Activation: 8 CP  
> Maintenance: 1 CP every round

The simulation should use whatever time scale is appropriate rather than forcing every ability into combat-round accounting.

---

# 3.10.9 Maintenance Cost Types

Maintenance can work in several ways.

### Continuous

Chakra is steadily consumed.

### Periodic

Chakra is consumed at regular intervals.

### Conditional

Chakra is only consumed when the technique actively does something.

Example:

> Barrier costs extra chakra only when struck.

### Stored

Chakra is loaded into the technique during activation.

The technique then draws from that reserve rather than directly from the user.

### Divided / Locked

The chakra remains committed to the technique and is temporarily unavailable elsewhere.

This last category will matter especially for clone mechanics.

---

# 3.10.10 Locked Chakra

Some techniques should not technically “spend” all of their chakra immediately.

Instead, part of the user's reserve becomes committed.

Example:

> Maximum usable chakra: 200 CP  
> 50 CP committed to sustained construct

The character might temporarily function as though they only have:

> 150 CP freely available.

If the construct ends, some or all of the locked chakra may become accessible again depending on the technique.

This is different from normal expenditure.

---

# 3.10.11 No Universal Chakra Return

We should not assume committed chakra always returns.

Different techniques may:

- return most of it,
- return a portion,
- consume all of it,
- dissipate it,
- transfer it elsewhere.

The jutsu record defines what happens.

---

# 3.10.12 Multiple Simultaneous Chakra Processes

Characters should be able to maintain more than one chakra process at a time.

There should **not** be an arbitrary rule such as:

> “Maximum of three active jutsu.”

Instead, the limitation comes from:

- Chakra Control.
- Concentration.
- Output.
- Available reserves.
- Technique complexity.
- Mastery.

This creates organic limits.

---

# 3.10.13 Process Complexity

Each ongoing process should carry a rough complexity burden.

Examples:

**Very Low**

> Tree walking.

**Low**

> Maintaining basic chakra suppression.

**Moderate**

> Keeping several chakra threads active.

**High**

> Sustaining a complex barrier.

**Extreme**

> Maintaining a highly unstable transformation while simultaneously performing other advanced chakra manipulation.

The combined burden determines how difficult multitasking becomes.

---

# 3.10.14 Processes Do Not All Consume Equal Attention

Three trivial processes may be easier than one extremely difficult process.

Therefore we should not count:

> Number of techniques

alone.

We care about:

> **Combined Process Load.**

That is much more flexible.

---

# 3.10.15 Example of Combined Processes

Suppose a shinobi is:

- Water walking.
- Suppressing their chakra.
- Maintaining a sensory technique.
- Preparing a Lightning Release jutsu.

Water walking may already be automatic.

Chakra suppression may be light.

Sensory maintenance may be moderate.

The Lightning technique requires active execution.

A highly trained shinobi may manage all four.

A beginner may lose one or more processes immediately.

---

# 3.10.16 Complexity Escalation

Adding processes should generally create escalating difficulty rather than simple linear penalties.

The first additional process may be manageable.

The fourth complicated simultaneous process may be dramatically harder.

This means advanced multitasking remains exceptional without needing rigid caps.

---

# 3.10.17 Chakra Control Is the Primary Multitasking Skill

Chakra Control should strongly influence how many simultaneous chakra operations someone can manage.

This is another reason it deserves the expanded:

> Basic I–V  
> Intermediate I–V  
> Advanced I–V  
> Expert I–V  
> Master I–VII

progression.

At the highest tiers, characters should be capable of managing combinations that would overwhelm ordinary shinobi.

---

# 3.10.18 Willpower and Concentration

Willpower should become increasingly relevant when maintaining techniques through:

- Pain.
- Fear.
- Exhaustion.
- Mental interference.
- Injury.
- Prolonged stress.

Chakra Control handles the chakra process.

Willpower helps the character keep performing it despite disruption.

---

# 3.10.19 Perception and Active Control

Some maintained techniques require constant real-time feedback.

For example:

- Guiding projectiles.
- Adjusting barriers.
- Chakra threads.
- Active sensory scans.

Perception may therefore become especially important.

A character cannot accurately react to information they cannot perceive.

---

# 3.10.20 Losing Concentration

A concentration failure should not always instantly erase the technique.

Possible outcomes include:

**Minor Degradation**

> Effect becomes less precise.

**Instability**

> Maintenance becomes more difficult.

**Reduced Effect**

> Range, strength, or accuracy falls.

**Partial Collapse**

> Part of the technique fails.

**Complete Collapse**

> Technique ends.

**Backlash**

> Dangerous techniques harm the user.

The technique's failure profile determines the result.

---

# 3.10.21 Brief Distraction

A momentary distraction should be less severe than:

> being knocked unconscious.

A skilled user may briefly lose perfect focus while maintaining the technique.

This gives us degrees of concentration disruption instead of binary:

> focused / not focused.

---

# 3.10.22 Physical Damage

Taking damage does not automatically break concentration.

The disruption depends on:

- Injury severity.
- Pain.
- Surprise.
- Character Willpower.
- Technique complexity.
- Mastery.

A veteran can maintain simple techniques through injuries that would completely disrupt a novice.

---

# 3.10.23 Chakra Disruption

Techniques specifically attacking chakra flow should be especially dangerous to sustained effects.

They may:

- interrupt maintenance,
- sever connections,
- destabilize shapes,
- interfere with Output.

This gives chakra-disruption techniques valuable tactical roles.

---

# 3.10.24 Exhaustion

As chakra reserves fall, sustaining multiple techniques should become increasingly difficult.

This can happen because:

- less chakra remains,
- safe Output decreases,
- Control deteriorates,
- physical and mental fatigue accumulate.

A character may voluntarily drop nonessential effects to conserve resources.

NPCs should do this intelligently.

---

# 3.10.25 Voluntary Release

Users should normally be able to intentionally end their maintained techniques.

Doing so may:

- stop upkeep,
- release locked chakra,
- dismiss a construct,
- remove a barrier.

Some techniques may have special termination requirements.

But normal maintained jutsu should not trap the user indefinitely.

---

# 3.10.26 Technique Termination Can Have Consequences

Ending a technique is not always harmless.

Examples:

- A barrier may collapse abruptly.
- A summon may become angry.
- Stored chakra may disperse.
- A transformation may cause exhaustion.
- A dangerous construct may detonate.

These consequences should be technique-specific.

---

# 3.10.27 Unconsciousness

The default rule should be:

> **Actively maintained techniques usually end when the user becomes unconscious.**

But this is not universal.

A technique may continue if it is:

- self-sustaining,
- autonomous,
- seal-based,
- supplied by stored chakra,
- maintained by another entity,
- explicitly persistent.

This prevents arbitrary contradictions.

---

# 3.10.28 Sleep

Sleep should generally count similarly to unconsciousness for actively maintained techniques.

However, highly automatic passive effects may potentially persist if their mechanics permit it.

For example, a long-term seal does not vanish because its creator goes to bed.

---

# 3.10.29 User Death

User death should **not** automatically erase every technique they ever created.

Instead:

### Actively Maintained
Usually ends.

### Tethered
Usually collapses when the connection disappears.

### Self-Sustaining
May continue until its stored resources expire.

### Seal/Persistent Effect
May continue indefinitely.

### Autonomous Entity
May continue according to its own rules.

This is essential for worldbuilding.

Otherwise permanent seals and historical techniques could not exist.

---

# 3.10.30 Distance Limits

Some sustained techniques should have a maximum effective distance from the user.

Possible consequences of exceeding it:

- Technique collapses.
- Control is lost.
- Upkeep increases.
- Precision falls.
- Connection weakens.

Other techniques may have no meaningful tether once activated.

This must be explicit.

---

# 3.10.31 Distance and Difficulty

For tethered techniques, greater distance may increase:

- Control difficulty.
- Chakra expenditure.
- Delay.
- Sensory uncertainty.

Chakra threads are an obvious example.

---

# 3.10.32 Barriers and Obstructions

Physical walls should not automatically break chakra connections.

Whether an obstacle matters depends on the technique.

Possible blockers include:

- Specialized barriers.
- Sealing fields.
- dimensional separation,
- chakra-disruptive materials,
- extreme distance.

Again, the technique defines its dependency.

---

# 3.10.33 Cross-Dimensional Maintenance

Maintaining chakra connections across dimensions should generally be extremely difficult or impossible unless the technique specifically supports it.

This prevents ordinary sustained techniques from accidentally gaining space-time capabilities.

---

# 3.10.34 Maintaining Clones

Clone mechanics will get their own section, but this framework gives us the foundation.

Different clones may require:

- no concentration,
- ongoing chakra allocation,
- range limits,
- chakra division,
- maintenance.

We should not assume all clone types function identically.

---

# 3.10.35 Maintaining Genjutsu

Likewise, some genjutsu may be:

- momentary,
- self-sustaining,
- actively maintained.

A sophisticated illusion that reacts dynamically to the victim may demand greater ongoing concentration than a simple sensory distortion.

We'll define that later.

---

# 3.10.36 Maintaining Sensory Techniques

Sensory abilities may have two distinct burdens:

> keeping the sensory field active

and:

> mentally processing the information received.

A massive sensory radius might be sustainable chakra-wise but overwhelming cognitively.

This distinction will matter later.

---

# 3.10.37 Maintaining Medical Techniques

Continuous healing may demand:

- sustained chakra expenditure,
- high Control,
- constant concentration.

Interruptions can be particularly dangerous depending on the procedure.

A simple wound-closing technique and delicate internal surgery should not have the same maintenance burden.

---

# 3.10.38 Maintaining Barriers

Barrier techniques are an obvious use case.

A barrier might require:

> Activation Cost: High  
> Maintenance: Moderate  
> Concentration: Moderate  
> Extra Chakra When Struck: Yes

Another might use stored chakra and require almost no ongoing attention.

Both should be valid.

---

# 3.10.39 Maintaining Transformations

Transformation states should specify whether they are maintained through:

- constant chakra cost,
- fixed duration,
- stored resource,
- passive activation,
- continuous concentration.

There should be no universal transformation upkeep rule.

---

# 3.10.40 Autonomous Techniques

Some techniques may continue behaving after the user stops controlling them.

However, autonomy requires an actual mechanism.

Examples could include:

- predefined seals,
- programmed chakra behavior,
- independent summons,
- self-sustaining constructs.

Shape Transformation alone should not grant intelligence.

---

# 3.10.41 Commands vs. Direct Control

Autonomous effects may operate through commands rather than constant micromanagement.

For example:

> “Guard this doorway.”

This requires less concentration than directly controlling every movement.

But autonomous behavior is also less flexible.

That tradeoff can make such techniques distinct.

---

# 3.10.42 Persistent World Effects

Long-term techniques should become part of the simulation state.

Examples:

- Seals.
- Barriers.
- Traps.
- Stored chakra.
- Marked locations.
- Long-duration summons or constructs.

If they remain relevant after the scene ends, the simulation should preserve them.

This becomes especially important for the life-simulation side of the game.

---

# 3.10.43 Dormant Persistent Effects

A persistent technique does not necessarily need full active simulation every moment.

Like NPCs, it can become abstract when nothing is interacting with it.

The system only needs to instantiate detailed mechanics again when something relevant happens.

This prevents long-term effects from creating unnecessary bookkeeping.

---

# 3.10.44 Maintenance Training

Characters can deliberately train sustained manipulation.

Exercises might include:

- Maintaining chakra while performing physical tasks.
- Holding several shapes simultaneously.
- Sustaining barriers for longer periods.
- Maintaining control through distractions.
- Running multiple basic chakra processes.
- Increasing distance from a tethered technique.

This can improve:

- Chakra Control,
- technique mastery,
- concentration tolerance.

---

# 3.10.45 Multitasking Training

A character should not automatically become good at multiple-process control just because each individual process is easy separately.

They may need explicit experience combining them.

For example:

> Water walking + hand seals

then:

> Water walking + suppression + hand seals

then:

> maintaining a sensory field during combat.

This gives advanced Chakra Control meaningful practical development.

---

# 3.10.46 No Infinite Passive Buff Stacking

This framework should prevent a major exploit:

> activating dozens of low-cost sustained buffs before combat.

Even if each technique individually has low upkeep, simultaneous processes create:

- chakra commitments,
- concentration load,
- Output burden,
- incompatibilities.

Some genuinely passive techniques may stack freely.

But active chakra manipulation should not become unlimited.

---

# 3.10.47 Technique Compatibility

Some sustained techniques may interfere with one another.

Possible reasons:

- Conflicting chakra flow.
- Competing nature transformations.
- Same chakra pathways.
- Mutually exclusive transformations.
- Concentration overload.

Other techniques may synergize.

Compatibility should be based on their mechanisms, not arbitrary stacking rules.

---

# 3.10.48 Priority Under Overload

If a character exceeds what they can maintain, they should sometimes be able to choose what to preserve.

For example:

> Drop chakra suppression, preserve barrier.

or:

> Dismiss sensory technique, maintain healing.

If disruption is sudden, Ruleset 2 may determine which processes fail before the character can react.

---

# 3.10.49 NPC Resource Management

NPCs should understand ongoing burdens according to their intelligence and experience.

A skilled NPC may:

- cancel an unnecessary technique,
- conserve chakra,
- reduce sensory range,
- dismiss clones,
- abandon a barrier.

An inexperienced or panicked NPC may maintain too much and exhaust themselves.

This adds believable tactical behavior.

---

# 3.10.50 Example Sustained Technique

A barrier might internally read:

> **Barrier: Four-Wall Chakra Screen**  
> Activation Cost: 20 CP  
> Maintenance: 2 CP/minute  
> Concentration: Moderate  
> Sustained Output: Intermediate III  

---

## Turn a7d8b4d2-6722-45f6-8b66-013a85b9593a

### User

Ok

### Assistant — a9ee1272-18f2-4e5f-a53a-49f6df89f261

**Capture warning: truncated at the 20,000-character reader limit; the ending is missing.**

## Ruleset 3.11 — Clone Mechanics

Clones need their own framework because “clone” describes an effect, not one universal mechanic. A nonphysical illusion, a mud clone, and a Shadow Clone may all look like duplicates of the user while functioning completely differently.

The central rule should be:

> **Every clone technique defines what the duplicate actually is, what resources create it, what it inherits, how independently it acts, and what happens when it ends.**

This lets us support canon and original clone techniques without making Shadow Clone mechanics apply to everything.

### 3.11.1 Major Clone Families

We should recognize several broad families rather than creating rules from scratch for every technique.

| Clone Type | Typical Nature |
|---|---|
| **Illusionary Clone** | Image or sensory deception; normally nonphysical |
| **Physical Chakra Clone** | Tangible duplicate constructed primarily from chakra |
| **Elemental/Material Clone** | Physical body constructed from water, earth, lightning, wood, etc. |
| **Shadow Clone-Type** | Independent physical duplicate created through division/allocation of the user's chakra |
| **Specialized Clone** | Any unusual technique with its own mechanism |

These are categories, not rigid templates. Individual jutsu can override their normal expectations.

---

### 3.11.2 Every Clone Needs a Mechanical Profile

A clone technique should specify:

**Physicality** — illusionary, semi-physical, or fully physical.

**Creation Medium** — chakra, element, physical material, special ability, etc.

**Chakra Model** — spent, divided, locked, allocated, or independently supplied.

**Inherited Capabilities** — Attributes, Skills, jutsu, special abilities.

**Durability** — how much disruption the clone can tolerate.

**Autonomy** — how independently it can think and act.

**Connection** — range and dependence on the creator.

**Information Transfer** — whether memories or sensory information return.

**Termination** — destruction, voluntary dismissal, range failure, chakra depletion.

**Aftereffect** — chakra return, memory return, elemental explosion, remains, or nothing.

That profile becomes part of the jutsu data model.

---

## 3.11.3 Illusionary Clones

Basic illusionary clones should be extremely cheap compared with physical duplication.

They generally:

- Cannot physically interact.
- Cannot inflict ordinary physical damage.
- Do not require splitting the creator's chakra reserves.
- Mimic appearance and movement.
- Exist mainly to deceive observers.

Their effectiveness depends more on:

> technique mastery + Chakra Control + deception + opponent Perception

than raw Output.

A poor illusion may move incorrectly, fail to interact with terrain, or be easily recognized.

A skilled user could make an otherwise low-rank clone technique tactically useful even against powerful opponents.

---

## 3.11.4 Physical Clones

Physical clones occupy real space and can interact with the environment.

However, “physical” does not mean:

> identical to the original in every respect.

A physical clone's technique determines:

- Strength.
- Speed.
- Durability.
- Chakra supply.
- Jutsu access.
- Intelligence/autonomy.

Some may be little more than mobile decoys.

Others may approximate the original remarkably well.

---

## 3.11.5 Elemental and Material Clones

Elemental clones should be built from their respective medium.

Examples include:

> Water Clone  
> Earth Clone  
> Lightning Clone  
> original non-canon elemental clones

Their medium can affect:

- Durability.
- Mobility.
- Environmental requirements.
- Cost.
- Destruction effects.
- Elemental vulnerabilities.

A Water Clone and Lightning Clone therefore shouldn't just be the same stat block with a different visual effect.

---

## 3.11.6 Clone Destruction Effects

Some elemental clones may have special consequences when destroyed.

For example, a Lightning-based clone might discharge electricity when disrupted.

An original poisonous mist clone might disperse its substance.

These are **properties of the clone technique**, not universal elemental-clone benefits.

The effect must be included in the jutsu's cost, risk, and balance.

---

# 3.11.7 Shadow Clone-Type Techniques

Shadow Clone-style techniques should be mechanically distinctive because they create genuinely independent physical duplicates using the creator's chakra.

Their most important feature should be:

> **The user's usable chakra is divided or allocated among the active bodies.**

Creating additional clones therefore has a real cost beyond the initial activation.

This immediately prevents clone count from becoming a free action-economy multiplier.

---

# 3.11.8 Chakra Division

Suppose a user has:

> **240 usable CP**

Creating several Shadow Clones divides available chakra among the bodies according to the technique's rules.

The exact split doesn't necessarily need to be equal for every clone technique. Advanced variants could potentially permit deliberate unequal allocation.

But ordinary Shadow Clone execution may default to approximately even distribution.

That means:

> More clones = less chakra available to each body.

A user producing many clones sacrifices individual endurance and high-cost jutsu capability.

---

# 3.11.9 Minimum Viable Chakra

A physical Shadow Clone should require enough chakra to remain stable.

Characters cannot infinitely subdivide chakra into microscopic fractions and produce:

> 50,000 functional clones with 0.001 CP each.

Each clone technique has a **Minimum Viable Allocation**.

Below that threshold:

- creation fails,
- clones are extremely unstable,
- or the technique cannot produce additional bodies.

---

# 3.11.10 Clone Count Has No Arbitrary Universal Cap

We should not say:

> “Everyone may have five clones.”

Instead, clone count emerges from:

> Chakra Reserves + Output + Chakra Control + technique mastery + minimum allocation + user condition

A massive-reserve specialist may produce extraordinarily large numbers.

An ordinary shinobi attempting the same thing may incapacitate themselves.

That preserves unusual characters without creating protagonist privilege.

---

# 3.11.11 Creating Many Clones Requires Output

Having enough total chakra does not necessarily mean the user can divide and release it quickly enough.

Large clone creation may demand significant **Chakra Output**.

Someone with:

> enormous reserves but mediocre Output

might be capable of sustaining many clones but unable to create all of them instantaneously.

They could potentially create them in smaller waves.

---

# 3.11.12 Chakra Control and Clone Creation

Chakra Control matters because the user's chakra must be properly divided and distributed.

Poor control may cause:

- Uneven clones.
- Excessive chakra loss.
- Premature dispersal.
- Failed creation.
- One clone receiving too much while another receives too little.

Mastery can greatly improve this process.

---

# 3.11.13 Clone Attributes

Clones should not universally receive:

> “100% of every stat.”

The technique defines what gets replicated.

A Shadow Clone may reproduce much of the creator's practical capability, while a Water Clone could operate at reduced physical effectiveness.

Attributes may therefore be:

> Full  
> Reduced  
> Modified  
> Not Applicable

depending on the clone.

---

# 3.11.14 Skills and Knowledge

Intelligent clones generally begin with the creator's:

- Memories.
- Knowledge.
- Learned jutsu.
- Tactical understanding.
- Skill familiarity.

They are essentially a snapshot of the creator at creation.

But what they can actually execute depends on the clone body's limitations.

Knowing an S-rank jutsu doesn't help if the clone received too little chakra to perform it.

---

# 3.11.15 Special Abilities

Special abilities need explicit clone compatibility.

A clone should not automatically reproduce every rare ability the original possesses.

For example, an ability may depend on:

- Actual genetic tissue.
- A physical organ.
- Stored resource.
- External entity.
- Implant.
- Contract.

Some clone techniques may reproduce the functional chakra pattern.

Others cannot.

Each special ability should eventually define:

> **Clone Compatibility.**

---

# 3.11.16 Dōjutsu and Clones

A clone resembling a Sharingan user should not automatically mean the clone possesses fully functional Sharingan.

Whether it does depends on:

- clone type,
- technique mechanics,
- chakra allocation,
- dōjutsu properties.

This prevents visual replication from automatically duplicating every biological advantage.

---

# 3.11.17 Equipment

Clone-created clothing and basic equipment should generally be considered part of the clone manifestation when appropriate.

These objects do **not** automatically become real permanent items.

A clone shouldn't be able to reproduce:

> ten rare swords,

dismiss itself,

and leave ten permanent swords behind.

Unless a technique explicitly creates real physical matter, replicated equipment disappears with the clone.

---

# 3.11.18 Consumables Cannot Be Duplicated Freely

The same safeguard applies to:

- Pills.
- Explosive tags.
- Poison.
- Money.
- Rare materials.
- Scrolls.

A clone may visually or temporarily reproduce certain items as part of itself.

That does not create permanent economic resources.

Actual consumables used by clones must have a legitimate source or be chakra constructs with their own limitations.

---

# 3.11.19 Clone Autonomy

Physical intelligent clones should usually operate as independent agents after creation.

They possess the creator's memories and personality **at the moment of creation**.

Afterward, each clone:

- sees different things,
- makes its own decisions,
- develops separate experiences.

That means clones are not continuously receiving the creator's thoughts unless the technique specifically provides such a link.

---

# 3.11.20 No Automatic Hive Mind

This distinction is important.

If three Shadow Clones separate:

> Clone A does not automatically know what Clone B just discovered.

They must:

- communicate normally,
- use another ability,
- or wait for memory transfer when the relevant clone ends.

Techniques that genuinely establish telepathic clone networks can exist, but that is an additional ability.

---

# 3.11.21 Personality

Clones created from the user's own mind should generally share:

- Personality.
- Loyalties.
- Goals.
- Current knowledge.

But they may respond differently as their immediate experiences diverge.

A clone may become:

- frightened,
- angry,
- cautious,
- suspicious

based on what happens to it.

This helps avoid treating independent clones like remote-controlled drones.

---

# 3.11.22 Orders

The creator can give clones instructions.

Simple instructions might be:

> “Search the eastern forest and return if you find anything.”

A clone capable of independent thought can interpret changing circumstances rather than following every command literally.

Less intelligent constructs may instead follow rigid programmed behavior.

Autonomy is therefore technique-specific.

---

# 3.11.23 Clone Durability

Durability must also be technique-specific.

Some clones may disperse after:

> a meaningful clean hit.

Others may endure:

> substantial damage.

We should avoid a universal:

> “All clones have 1 HP.”

Instead, the technique defines whether durability is:

- Fragile.
- Reduced.
- Moderate.
- Near-original.
- Material-dependent.

Ruleset 2/combat rules determine actual resolution.

---

# 3.11.24 Damage Is Not Always Transferred

Physical damage suffered by a clone should **not** automatically injure the original.

If a clone is stabbed:

> the creator does not spontaneously gain a stab wound.

However, some techniques could transfer:

- pain,
- fatigue,
- neurological feedback,
- chakra disruption

if explicitly designed that way.

Shadow Clone memory transfer may cause the creator to remember being injured without reproducing the physical wound.

---

# 3.11.25 Clone Termination

A clone may end through:

> Destruction  
> Voluntary dismissal  
> Chakra exhaustion  
> Range failure  
> Technique cancellation  
> Special trigger

These should not always produce identical results.

For example, information transfer could theoretically fail if a specialized clone is completely severed from its creator rather than voluntarily dismissed.

The technique defines this.

---

# 3.11.26 Chakra on Dismissal

We should not establish a universal rule that all clone chakra returns.

Instead, the clone technique specifies what happens to its remaining allocation.

Possible outcomes include:

> Chakra returns substantially.  
> Some chakra returns.  
> Remaining chakra dissipates.  
> Chakra was already spent and cannot return.

For Shadow Clone-style techniques, I favor **partial or substantial recovery of unused allocated chakra**, while actual chakra expended by the clone remains spent.

That prevents clones from destroying chakra merely by existing while also preventing:

> cast jutsu with clone → dismiss clone → magically refund the spent chakra.

---

# 3.11.27 Memory Transfer

Shadow Clone-style techniques can possess one of their most important features:

> **When the clone ends, its memories and experiences return to the creator.**

This should include:

- What it saw.
- What it heard.
- What it learned.
- Decisions it made.
- Technique practice.
- Emotional experiences.

This makes clones extremely valuable for:

- Reconnaissance.
- Training.
- Research.
- Parallel problem solving.

---

# 3.11.28 Memory Transfer Is Not Physical Transfer

This is the most important anti-exploit principle.

A clone doing:

> 1,000 push-ups

does not cause the creator's muscles to undergo the physical adaptation of 1,000 push-ups.

The creator receives the **experience and memory** of doing them.

But not:

- new muscle tissue,
- cardiovascular adaptation,
- healed injuries,
- physical conditioning.

Therefore Shadow Clone training is dramatically more useful for some types of progression than others.

---

# 3.11.29 What Clone Training Transfers Well

Clone-assisted training should be particularly effective for:

- Studying.
- Technique theory.
- Jutsu practice.
- Chakra manipulation understanding.
- Hand-seal familiarity.
- Tactical experimentation.
- Sensory interpretation.
- Research.
- Knowledge acquisition.
- Problem solving.

It may also accelerate technical Skills where the returned experience genuinely teaches the original nervous/chakra system something.

But the growth engine determines how much permanent progress results.

---

# 3.11.30 What Transfers Poorly

Clone training should be much less effective for adaptations requiring the original physical body, such as:

- Strength.
- Endurance conditioning.
- Physical durability.
- Muscle growth.
- Bone conditioning.
- Cardiovascular development.
- Recovery from injury.

Clones cannot exercise on behalf of the original's muscles.

---

# 3.11.31 Chakra Skills Are Mixed

Chakra-related training falls between purely mental and purely physical.

A clone practicing Shape Transformation can return very useful experiential information.

However, the original's actual chakra pathways and developed capabilities still impose limits.

Therefore:

> Clone experience can dramatically accelerate understanding and technical refinement, but it cannot instantly force the original's foundational Chakra, Control, or Output beyond what their body can develop.

This is crucial.

---

# 3.11.32 No Flat “Number of Clones × XP” Formula

We should explicitly reject:

> 100 clones = exactly 100× XP.

Instead, each clone generates **experience**.

When that experience returns, Ruleset 1 evaluates what meaningful development results.

Relevant factors include:

- Novelty.
- Difficulty.
- Quality.
- Redundancy.
- Errors.
- User capacity.
- Fatigue.
- Assimilation.

This lets clone training remain exceptionally powerful without becoming mathematically absurd.

---

# 3.11.33 Redundant Training Has Diminishing Returns

Suppose 100 clones perform the exact same trivial exercise.

The creator receives 100 nearly identical memories.

The first experiences may be useful.

The ninety-eighth adds very little new information.

By contrast, 100 clones testing:

> different approaches, conditions, angles, or hypotheses

can generate substantially more useful information.

This makes intelligent clone training much more valuable than brute-force spam.

---

# 3.11.34 Incorrect Training Can Multiply Mistakes

Clones are not perfect teachers.

If the user misunderstands a technique and creates twenty clones practicing the same incorrect method, they may simply return:

> twenty reinforced versions of the wrong idea.

Instructors and feedback remain valuable.

This prevents clone training from replacing actual instruction.

---

# 3.11.35 Assimilation Burden

Receiving many simultaneous memories should create mental strain.

Large-scale transfer can cause:

- Headaches.
- Mental fatigue.
- Disorientation.
- Sensory overload.
- Reduced concentration.
- Need for recovery.

The burden depends on:

- Number of clones.
- Duration.
- Complexity of experience.
- Emotional intensity.
- Intelligence.
- Willpower.
- Familiarity with the technique.

This creates another natural scaling limit.

---

# 3.11.36 Emotional Transfer

A clone's emotional experiences should return along with memory when the technique supports full experiential transfer.

If a clone experiences:

- terror,
- pain,
- traumatic events,

the creator remembers that experience.

This means using clones for extremely dangerous tasks is not psychologically consequence-free.

---

# 3.11.37 Reconnaissance

Clones should be extremely effective reconnaissance tools when they possess memory transfer.

A clone can:

> scout → observe → dismiss → transfer information.

However, the information is only as accurate as what the clone perceived.

A fooled clone returns fooled information.

This preserves Perception, genjutsu, stealth, and misinformation mechanics.

---

# 3.11.38 Range

Clone techniques should define whether distance from the creator matters.

Possible models include:

> No practical local range restriction.  
> Increasing instability with distance.  
> Hard maximum range.  
> Requires chakra connection.  
> Independent once formed.

We should not give every clone a universal tether.

---

# 3.11.39 Communication at Range

Clones outside normal communication range cannot automatically report information until:

- they return,
- they dismiss and transfer memory,
- or they use an actual communication technique.

This makes timing important.

---

# 3.11.40 Clones Using Jutsu

A clone can use jutsu only when:

- its clone type permits it,
- it inherited the necessary knowledge/capability,
- it has sufficient chakra,
- it meets the technique's functional requirements.

A Shadow Clone with:

> 15 CP

cannot cast the original's:

> 40 CP technique

just because the original could have.

---

# 3.11.41 Clone Output

A clone's available Chakra Output may also be affected by the technique.

Some clone types may replicate the creator's Output capability but lack sufficient reserves.

Others may have specifically reduced Output.

This should be part of the clone profile.

---

# 3.11.42 Clones Creating Clones

This is an obvious exploit risk.

Some clone techniques may technically allow a clone to create another clone.

But doing so must obey the exact same resource rules.

If a Shadow Clone has 30 CP and produces another Shadow Clone, it divides **its own available chakra**.

It does not access the original's untouched pool.

Recursive cloning therefore rapidly runs into resource limits.

---

# 3.11.43 No Exponential Chakra Creation

At no point should:

> 1 user → 2 clones → 4 → 8 → 16

create additional net chakra.

Every branch divides already-existing resources.

The system should track provenance sufficiently to preve

---

### User

### Assistant — 317c62e9-339d-4cd6-a208-9126b4cf6589

**Capture warning: truncated at the 20,000-character reader limit; the ending is missing.**

## Ruleset 3.12 — Genjutsu Framework

Genjutsu should be a distinct discipline built around **manipulating perception, cognition, sensory interpretation, or the target’s chakra-mediated processing of reality**.

The most important balancing rule is:

> **Genjutsu is not synonymous with mind control.**

A genjutsu can deceive, confuse, misdirect, immobilize through perceived reality, create false sensory information, or manipulate emotional responses. Stronger techniques may interfere more deeply with cognition.

But direct behavioral control, memory alteration, personality manipulation, or complete domination should be exceptional abilities with much stronger requirements and counterplay.

---

### 3.12.1 Genjutsu Is a Specialized Skill

Genjutsu should have its own broad Skill progression:

> **Basic I–V**  
> **Intermediate I–V**  
> **Advanced I–V**  
> **Expert I–V**  
> **Master I–V**

I don't think it needs Chakra Control's Master I–VII depth.

Genjutsu Skill represents:

- Understanding how illusions interact with chakra and perception.
- Constructing convincing sensory experiences.
- Manipulating multiple senses.
- Controlling illusion complexity.
- Recognizing common genjutsu structures.
- Understanding induction and escape mechanisms.
- Adapting illusions dynamically.
- Managing multiple targets.

Chakra Control remains extremely important, but:

> **High Chakra Control ≠ high Genjutsu skill.**

---

## 3.12.2 Attribute Influences

Genjutsu should draw heavily from different Attributes depending on what the character is doing.

For **casting/designing** genjutsu:

- Intelligence
- Chakra
- Perception

For **maintaining or manipulating** complicated effects:

- Chakra
- Intelligence
- Willpower

For **detecting genjutsu**:

- Perception
- Intelligence
- Chakra Control

For **resisting or breaking genjutsu**:

- Willpower
- Chakra Control
- Perception

The exact combination belongs to Ruleset 2.

There should not be a universal:

> Genjutsu = Intelligence roll.

---

# 3.12.3 Genjutsu Delivery Methods

Every genjutsu must specify **how it reaches the target**.

Common delivery methods include:

### Visual

Requires the target to see:

- The user's eyes.
- A gesture.
- A pattern.
- A visual manifestation.
- A special dōjutsu.

Breaking line of sight may prevent induction depending on the technique.

### Auditory

Delivered through:

- Voice.
- Music.
- Sound.
- Rhythm.

Hearing protection or blocking the carrier may matter.

### Touch

Requires physical contact.

Usually easier to avoid but potentially difficult to resist once applied.

### Chakra Contact

Requires direct interaction between chakra networks.

Examples might include:

- Chakra threads.
- Medical contact.
- Particular weapons or techniques.

### Environmental Carrier

The illusion is transmitted through something in the surroundings.

Example:

- Mist.
- Sound field.
- Chakra-infused area.

### Seal or Trap

The effect activates through:

- Entering an area.
- Touching a marked object.
- Triggering a seal.

### Dōjutsu-Mediated

Special ocular abilities may induce genjutsu through their own specific mechanism.

These still need explicitly defined delivery conditions.

---

# 3.12.4 Delivery Does Not Guarantee Success

Meeting the delivery requirement means:

> **The technique can attempt to affect the target.**

It does not necessarily mean:

> **The target is automatically trapped.**

Actual induction depends on:

- Technique strength.
- Caster Skill.
- Individual mastery.
- Target defenses.
- Awareness.
- Conditions.
- Special abilities.

Ruleset 2 handles the resulting contest.

---

# 3.12.5 Genjutsu Effect Families

Genjutsu should be categorized by what they actually manipulate.

### Sensory Distortion

Changes an existing perception.

Examples:

- Distance appears wrong.
- Sound seems to come from another direction.
- An object appears displaced.

### Sensory Fabrication

Creates something that is not present.

Examples:

- Fake enemy.
- Imaginary fire.
- False voice.

### Sensory Suppression

Removes or conceals information.

Examples:

- Making someone fail to notice a doorway.
- Masking footsteps.
- Obscuring an ally.

### Environmental Illusion

Replaces significant portions of perceived surroundings.

### Perceptual Entrapment

Creates the experience of being immobilized, restrained, lost, or trapped.

### Emotional Manipulation

Artificially induces or exaggerates:

- Fear.
- Calm.
- Panic.
- Trust.
- Unease.

These should be harder than simple sensory deception.

### Pain or Sensation Manipulation

Creates convincing:

- Pain.
- Heat.
- Cold.
- Pressure.
- Falling sensations.

### Cognitive Interference

Disrupts:

- Attention.
- Memory access.
- Orientation.
- Decision-making.

### Compulsion

Directly pressures or commands behavior.

This should be rare and highly demanding.

### Memory Manipulation

Altering, suppressing, or fabricating memories should be exceptionally advanced.

---

# 3.12.6 Illusion Complexity

Every genjutsu should have an **Illusion Complexity**.

Possible levels:

> Very Low  
> Low  
> Moderate  
> High  
> Very High  
> Extreme

Complexity reflects things such as:

- Number of senses affected.
- Amount of detail.
- Duration.
- Responsiveness.
- Number of targets.
- Internal consistency.
- Cognitive depth.

A static false sound is dramatically simpler than:

> a fully interactive false environment affecting sight, sound, touch, balance, and perceived time.

---

# 3.12.7 Sense Count Matters

A genjutsu affecting only one sense is generally easier to maintain than one affecting five simultaneously.

For example:

> Visual-only illusion

may be relatively simple.

A technique producing:

- Sight.
- Sound.
- Smell.
- Touch.
- Temperature.

requires much greater control and complexity.

This also affects detection.

A visually flawless illusion that produces no expected sound might reveal itself.

---

# 3.12.8 Internal Consistency

Convincing genjutsu must remain internally coherent.

Suppose an illusion makes the target believe:

> a wall exists.

But the target hears footsteps through it, feels wind coming through it, and their sensory technique detects nothing.

Those inconsistencies create opportunities for detection.

Higher mastery allows the user to account for more of these details.

---

# 3.12.9 Static vs. Reactive Illusions

This distinction should matter heavily.

### Static Illusion

Follows a mostly predetermined pattern.

Example:

> hallway appears empty.

Cheap and easy to maintain.

### Reactive Illusion

Responds to what the victim does.

Example:

> an imaginary enemy actively reacts to attacks.

This requires more:

- concentration,
- information processing,
- Chakra Control,
- Genjutsu Skill.

### Adaptive Illusion

The caster continuously changes the illusion based on the target's beliefs and behavior.

These should be very advanced.

---

# 3.12.10 Detection and Escape Are Separate

This should be one of the core genjutsu rules.

A character can realize:

> **“This is genjutsu.”**

without immediately being able to escape it.

Likewise, someone might accidentally disrupt an illusion without ever realizing what happened.

So we distinguish:

> **Detection**

from:

> **Release/Breaking.**

---

# 3.12.11 Detecting Genjutsu

Characters may detect genjutsu through:

- Sensory contradictions.
- Environmental inconsistencies.
- Unusual chakra flow.
- Missing sensory information.
- Behavior that does not make sense.
- Prior knowledge.
- An ally warning them.
- Dōjutsu.
- Chakra sensing.
- Specialized anti-genjutsu training.

Detection is usually easier if the target is actively suspicious.

---

# 3.12.12 Suspicion Matters

A target expecting genjutsu should behave differently from an unsuspecting target.

Someone who knows:

> “The enemy is a renowned illusion specialist.”

may deliberately:

- avoid eye contact,
- test their surroundings,
- watch teammates,
- monitor their chakra,
- question unexpected perceptions.

This should provide meaningful defensive advantages.

Information therefore matters.

---

# 3.12.13 False Positives

Paranoia should not automatically defeat genjutsu.

If someone constantly assumes:

> everything is an illusion,

they may waste actions testing real threats.

A skilled genjutsu specialist can exploit that uncertainty.

This makes genjutsu psychologically useful even when the enemy knows illusions are possible.

---

# 3.12.14 Genjutsu Kai

**Kai** should be a learned chakra-control application used to disrupt hostile interference.

Kai generally involves:

> recognizing/suspecting the illusion → deliberately disrupting or correcting one's chakra flow.

Its effectiveness should depend on:

- Chakra Control.
- Genjutsu knowledge.
- Willpower.
- Recognition.
- Strength of the opposing technique.
- Remaining ability to act.

Kai should not be:

> “Press button to remove genjutsu.”

---

# 3.12.15 Kai Requires Opportunity

Some illusions may allow immediate attempts at Kai.

Others may interfere with:

- conscious thought,
- body control,
- awareness that anything is wrong.

The target must have enough agency to attempt disruption.

This is one reason deeper genjutsu becomes more dangerous.

---

# 3.12.16 Failed Kai

Failure should not necessarily make the situation worse automatically.

Possible outcomes include:

- No effect.
- Partial weakening.
- Brief clarity.
- Increased awareness.
- Chakra expenditure.
- Technique destabilization.

Some dangerous genjutsu may react to attempted escape, but only if explicitly designed that way.

---

# 3.12.17 Outside Chakra Disruption

An ally can potentially break a genjutsu by disturbing the victim's chakra flow.

This should depend on:

- The technique.
- Ally knowledge.
- Chakra Control.
- Target accessibility.
- Strength of the genjutsu.

This makes team awareness extremely valuable.

---

# 3.12.18 Pain and Physical Shock

Pain can sometimes disrupt genjutsu because it provides:

- strong conflicting sensory input,
- sudden mental focus,
- chakra disruption.

But it should **not be a universal cure**.

A simple illusion may break when the victim bites their tongue.

A sophisticated high-level genjutsu may incorporate or override the pain.

Techniques should define their vulnerability to external shock.

---

# 3.12.19 Closing Your Eyes Is Not Universal Protection

Visual genjutsu requiring initial eye contact may be avoided by not looking.

But once already induced:

> closing the eyes does not necessarily break the technique.

Likewise, an illusion transmitted through sound cannot automatically be escaped by closing the eyes.

Defense must match the delivery mechanism.

---

# 3.12.20 Breaking the Delivery Condition

Some genjutsu require continuous exposure.

Example:

> illusion persists only while music continues.

Stopping the sound may break it.

Others only need initial induction.

Once triggered, destroying the original delivery vector may no longer matter.

This must be technique-specific.

---

# 3.12.21 Resistance Is Not One Willpower Roll

Willpower should matter, but not universally dominate.

Different genjutsu test different defenses.

A sensory deception might primarily challenge:

> Perception + Genjutsu knowledge.

An emotional fear technique might strongly involve:

> Willpower.

A chakra-invasive illusion might emphasize:

> Chakra Control.

A sophisticated technique may test multiple defenses.

This makes genjutsu varied.

---

# 3.12.22 Strong Willpower Does Not Grant Immunity

A fearless, disciplined shinobi can still see something that is not there.

Willpower may help them:

- question it,
- resist emotional manipulation,
- continue acting despite perceived pain.

But it doesn't automatically reveal every illusion.

---

# 3.12.23 Perception Does Not Grant Immunity Either

Exceptional Perception can notice contradictions.

But a perfectly constructed illusion may provide fewer contradictions to notice.

High Perception helps.

It doesn't automatically cancel genjutsu.

---

# 3.12.24 Chakra Control Defense

High Chakra Control can help a target:

- recognize abnormal internal chakra patterns,
- perform Kai,
- recover from interference.

This is another major benefit of Chakra Control.

But someone with Master Chakra Control and no genjutsu knowledge can still be deceived if they don't realize anything is wrong.

---

# 3.12.25 Genjutsu Knowledge Defense

Studying genjutsu should improve defense even for characters who rarely cast it.

Training may teach:

- common delivery vectors,
- illusion tells,
- chakra disruption methods,
- reality-testing procedures,
- known enemy techniques.

This creates a legitimate reason for ordinary shinobi to receive basic anti-genjutsu education.

---

# 3.12.26 Duration

Genjutsu may be:

- Instantaneous.
- Brief.
- Sustained.
- Fixed-duration.
- Persistent until broken.

Some techniques produce an immediate false perception and end.

Others trap the target in an ongoing illusion.

Duration is separate from induction strength.

---

# 3.12.27 Maintenance

A genjutsu may be:

### Self-Sustaining

Once induced, it continues without active caster attention.

### Lightly Maintained

Requires limited ongoing attention.

### Actively Controlled

Caster continuously manipulates the experience.

### Tethered

Requires ongoing connection to the victim.

Reactive illusions generally require greater maintenance.

---

# 3.12.28 Caster Awareness

A genjutsu caster should not automatically know everything the victim experiences.

Some techniques may provide:

> no feedback at all.

Others may allow limited awareness.

Advanced abilities may create a genuine mental connection.

This must be part of the technique.

Otherwise genjutsu does not secretly become telepathy.

---

# 3.12.29 Target Awareness

Likewise, the target should not always know:

> “I just made a resistance roll.”

The simulation can resolve detection secretly.

A victim may continue believing the illusion is real.

This is an ideal place for hidden resolution because revealing the check itself could reveal information the character does not possess.

---

# 3.12.30 Multiple Targets

Multi-target genjutsu should be possible.

But increasing targets should increase one or more of:

- Chakra cost.
- Output requirement.
- Chakra Control demand.
- Concentration.
- Difficulty.
- Reduced individual illusion detail.

The technique determines its scaling method.

---

# 3.12.31 Area Genjutsu

Some techniques may affect anyone within:

- a sound radius,
- a visual field,
- a mist,
- a barrier,
- a marked area.

These can be extremely useful but may have less precise targeting.

Friendly-fire concerns may exist unless the technique can discriminate targets.

---

# 3.12.32 Target Discrimination

A caster should not automatically be able to exclude allies from every area genjutsu.

Selective targeting may require:

- greater mastery,
- higher Control,
- a more advanced technique.

This gives mass illusions meaningful drawbacks.

---

# 3.12.33 Genjutsu and Chakra Cost

Most genjutsu probably should not require enormous burst Output compared with destructive ninjutsu.

Their difficulty is often concentrated in:

- Control.
- Complexity.
- Knowledge.
- Concentration.

This allows low-reserve but technically skilled genjutsu specialists to remain dangerous.

However, massive multi-target or extremely deep effects can become expensive.

---

# 3.12.34 Genjutsu and Shape Transformation

Ordinary Shape Transformation should matter less than it does for conventional ninjutsu.

Genjutsu is primarily about:

- chakra interference,
- sensory pattern construction,
- cognition.

Some specific genjutsu may involve structured chakra forms, but Shape Transformation should not automatically be a core requirement.

This helps keep disciplines mechanically distinct.

---

# 3.12.35 Yin Release

Advanced genjutsu should often interact with **Yin Release**, since Yin principles relate to form, imagination, and spiritual manipulation.

However:

> Basic Genjutsu Skill does not require formal Yin mastery.

At high levels, specialized Yin Transformation may become a prerequisite for certain advanced techniques.

---

# 3.12.36 Illusionary Damage

Most genjutsu should not directly create ordinary physical wounds.

If a victim believes they are:

> stabbed,

they may experience pain.

But their body should not automatically develop a real knife wound.

This distinction is important.

---

# 3.12.37 Indirect Physical Harm

Genjutsu can still be extremely dangerous.

A victim might:

- run off a cliff,
- attack an ally,
- freeze during combat,
- exhaust themselves fighting imaginary enemies,
- fail to notice a real attack.

The physical consequences are real because the victim's behavior is real.

---

# 3.12.38 Direct Physiological Effects

Some high-level genjutsu may explicitly cause:

- nervous-system disruption,
- unconsciousness,
- seizures,
- genuine chakra damage,
- severe psychological stress.

But those effects must be part of that particular technique.

We should not assume all illusions physically harm the brain.

---

# 3.12.39 Mental Trauma

Extremely intense illusory experiences can have psychological consequences.

However, trauma should not simply be:

> “Target failed, gains permanent insanity.”

Severity should depend on:

- Experience.
- Duration.
- Content.
- Existing resilience.
- Technique.
- Circumstances.

A future mental-health/stress system can handle long-term consequences if we develop one.

---

# 3.12.40 Perceived Time Manipulation

Certain genjutsu may manipulate the victim's **perception of time**.

This does not necessarily mean actual time has changed.

A short real-world interval might feel much longer.

Such techniques should be extremely demanding because they require sophisticated cognitive manipulation.

They may cause severe mental strain without granting actual time travel.

---

# 3.12.41 Paralysis-Like Genjutsu

Some techniques may make victims perceive themselves as:

- bound,
- unable to move,
- trapped.

Mechanically, this should not automatically be literal physical paralysis unless the technique actually disrupts motor control.

A sufficiently resistant victim may still force movement despite the illusion.

Different techniques can produce different depths of immobilization.

---

# 3.12.42 Compulsion

Directly forcing a person to perform actions should be significantly harder than deceiving perception.

Compulsion techniques should generally require:

- High Genjutsu Skill.
- Strong Chakra Control.
- Significant technique mastery.
- Strong delivery conditions.
- Explicit resistance opportunities.

More extreme commands should be harder than subtle behavioral pressure.

---

# 3.12.43 No Universal “Do Anything” Mind Control

Even advanced compulsion should define its scope.

A technique might:

> force hesitation,

> compel answering a question,

or:

> strongly suggest a simple action.

That is different from:

> “Target becomes my permanent slave.”

Broad, durable domination should require extraordinary special abilities and major costs or vulnerabilities.

---

# 3.12.44 Memory Manipulation

Memory techniques should be among the most technically demanding genjutsu-related abilities.

Possible effects include:

- Temporarily obscuring memory.
- Altering recall.
- Implanting false recollection.
- Suppressing access to information.

Permanent rewriting should be exceptionally rare and dangerous.

Memory effects also need explicit limits on:

- how much memory,
- how far back,
- how detailed,
- whether contradictions can expose manipulation.

---

# 3.12.45 Genjutsu Within Genjutsu

Nested illusions should be possible but difficult.

A victim might believe they escaped one illusion while still trapped in another.

This should require:

- high complexity,
- substantial mastery,
- careful construction.

It should not become the default outcome every time someone attempts Kai.

Otherwise escape b

---
