**Capture warning: truncated at the 20,000-character reader limit; the ending is missing.**

## Ruleset 3.25 — Kinjutsu & Forbidden Techniques

Kinjutsu should be treated primarily as a **classification imposed on a technique**, not as a separate power system.

The central rule is:

> **“Forbidden” describes how a technique is regulated, regarded, or restricted. It does not automatically describe how powerful the technique is.**

A technique may be forbidden because it is extraordinarily dangerous, unethical, politically sensitive, strategically classified, sacrificial, or simply illegal under a particular authority.

Likewise, an extremely powerful S-rank technique may be perfectly legal for qualified shinobi.

---

### 3.25.1 Kinjutsu Is Not a Rank

Rank and prohibition must remain separate.

For example:

> **B-rank Kinjutsu**

is completely legitimate.

So is:

> **S-rank unrestricted technique.**

Rank tells us about things like:

- Complexity.
- Expertise.
- Danger.
- Training burden.

Kinjutsu tells us:

> **someone has decided this technique should not be freely used or distributed.**

---

# 3.25.2 Kinjutsu Is a Tag

Like Hiden, **Kinjutsu** should normally function as an additional classification.

A technique might be:

> Ninjutsu + Medical + Kinjutsu

or:

> Fūinjutsu + Kinjutsu

or:

> Transformation + Kinjutsu.

The Kinjutsu tag doesn't change what type of technique it mechanically is.

---

# 3.25.3 Why Is It Forbidden?

Every Kinjutsu should internally record an explicit **Restriction Reason**.

Possible categories include:

### Extreme User Risk
Technique is likely to severely injure or kill the user.

### Public Danger
Failure or misuse poses extreme collateral risk.

### Sacrificial Requirement
Technique requires another person's life, body, chakra, soul, or comparable resource.

### Bodily Autonomy Violation
Technique forcibly modifies, controls, implants, or extracts from others.

### Corpse / Soul Manipulation
Technique interferes with the dead or postmortem consciousness.

### Dangerous Experimentation
Technique requires unacceptable biological or chakra experimentation.

### Irreversible Modification
Technique permanently changes the user or target.

### Containment Risk
Technique involves something extraordinarily dangerous to release or manipulate.

### Strategic Security
Technique is prohibited because a village considers the knowledge too dangerous to spread.

### Legal / Cultural Prohibition
A government, clan, religion, or society bans it for reasons unrelated to raw mechanics.

Several can apply simultaneously.

---

# 3.25.4 Mechanical Danger and Legal Status Are Separate

This distinction is essential.

Suppose a technique has:

> **User Risk: Extreme**

That does not automatically mean it is legally forbidden.

A village might allow qualified volunteers to use it.

Conversely:

> **User Risk: Low**

does not prevent a technique from being prohibited if it steals memories, violates corpses, or exposes a critical village secret.

Therefore we separately track:

> **Mechanical Risk**

and:

> **Restriction Status.**

---

# 3.25.5 “Forbidden” Is Not a Universal Property

Kinjutsu status should usually depend on **jurisdiction**.

For example:

> Konohagakure: Prohibited  
> Kusagakure: Restricted Research Only  
> Missing-nin faction: Unrestricted

The actual technique hasn't changed.

The laws and attitudes surrounding it have.

This is much more useful for a living world.

---

# 3.25.6 Classification Can Change Over Time

A technique may be legal in one era and forbidden in another.

For example:

> New technique invented.

> Used successfully during war.

> Catastrophic accident occurs.

> Village prohibits further use.

Decades later:

> improved safer variant developed.

> restrictions loosen.

Technique classifications should therefore be persistent world state rather than immutable labels.

---

# 3.25.7 Characters May Disagree With the Classification

A researcher might argue:

> “This technique is safe when performed correctly.”

The government may still prohibit it.

Another technique may be technically legal while medical shinobi consider it horribly unethical.

The simulation should not treat the government's classification as objective moral truth.

It merely records:

> **who prohibits what and why.**

---

# 3.25.8 Knowledge Can Be Forbidden Separately From Use

Different actions can have different restrictions.

A village might permit:

> storing a technique in a secure archive,

while prohibiting:

> unauthorized study.

Or permit:

> sanctioned medical research,

while prohibiting:

> battlefield use.

We should be able to distinguish:

- Possession.
- Study.
- Teaching.
- Experimentation.
- Performance.
- Distribution.

Social/legal rules later determine consequences.

---

# 3.25.9 Restricted Access

Kinjutsu records may exist in:

- sealed archives,
- classified libraries,
- clan vaults,
- research facilities,
- intelligence repositories.

Access may require:

- military rank,
- research authorization,
- specific role,
- Kage approval,
- clan permission.

That access provides **knowledge opportunities**, not automatic mastery.

---

# 3.25.10 Kinjutsu Still Uses Normal Learning Rules

Forbidden techniques follow:

> **Access → Comprehension → Acquisition → Functional Use → Mastery**

exactly like everything else.

Stealing an S-rank forbidden scroll does not mean:

> “Technique learned.”

The thief may discover they lack:

- Chakra Control.
- medical knowledge,
- Fūinjutsu,
- anatomy,
- Output,
- required materials,
- required special abilities.

The ban does not alter prerequisites.

---

# 3.25.11 Illegality Does Not Make Learning Mechanically Harder

A legal restriction creates **access difficulty**, not supernatural learning difficulty.

If two equally qualified shinobi receive identical complete instruction:

> one learning legally

and:

> one learning illegally,

the technique itself doesn't care.

The illegal learner simply faces different social consequences.

---

# 3.25.12 Forbidden Doesn't Mean Evil

Some Kinjutsu may be prohibited entirely for safety.

For example, imagine a technique that:

> increases Chakra Output enormously but carries a 30% risk of fatal pathway rupture.

It may have no malicious purpose whatsoever.

It is forbidden because authorities decided:

> nobody should be using this casually.

This gives Kinjutsu much more variety.

---

# 3.25.13 Dangerous Self-Modification

Some forbidden techniques may deliberately alter the user's own body.

Examples include:

- artificial organs,
- modified chakra pathways,
- extreme metabolic changes,
- permanent transformations,
- implanted tissues.

These must still use:

> Special Ability  
> Medical  
> Transformation  
> Injury

frameworks where relevant.

“Kinjutsu” doesn't let us skip the consequences.

---

# 3.25.14 Permanent Changes Must Actually Persist

If a Kinjutsu permanently changes a character:

> it modifies persistent character state.

It is not a temporary combat buff merely because it originated from a technique.

For example, an experiment altering the chakra pathways may permanently affect:

- Control.
- Output.
- health.
- compatibility.

Reversal then requires an actual method.

---

# 3.25.15 Human Experimentation

Forbidden research should obey ordinary research logic.

Using unwilling subjects does **not** make experiments magically successful.

Research still involves:

> hypothesis → procedure → observation → failure → revision.

Unethical researchers may gain access to experiments ethical researchers refuse to perform.

That expands what they are willing to test.

It does not grant scientific omniscience.

---

# 3.25.16 Experimentation Produces Uncertainty

An experimental procedure might result in:

- intended success,
- partial success,
- unexpected mutation,
- instability,
- injury,
- death,
- useless results.

This should be especially important for artificial bloodlines and body modification.

We don't want:

> “Villain experimented on 50 people, therefore automatically invented ultimate power.”

They still need genuine expertise.

---

# 3.25.17 Subjects Are Persistent Characters

Human or intelligent experimental subjects are not abstract research resources.

They retain:

- injuries,
- modifications,
- memories,
- relationships,
- trauma,
- potential escape or retaliation.

This can create long-term world consequences.

---

# 3.25.18 Sacrificial Requirements

If a forbidden technique requires a sacrifice, that requirement should be **mechanically real**.

For example:

> living vessel required,

or:

> lifespan consumed,

or:

> another person's chakra permanently extracted.

The cost is not:

> “Technique costs 100 CP but morally it's bad.”

The specific resource is part of the mechanism.

---

# 3.25.19 Ordinary Chakra Cannot Substitute Automatically

If a technique requires:

> a living sacrifice,

the user cannot simply say:

> “I'll spend an extra 500 chakra instead.”

Alternative requirements exist only if someone actually develops a variant capable of changing the mechanism.

This prevents severe costs from becoming decorative.

---

# 3.25.20 Lifespan Costs

Some techniques may directly consume:

- cellular capacity,
- life force,
- remaining lifespan.

If so, this should create persistent consequences.

It should not merely be:

> “You feel tired.”

The system may track the biological effect even if the character cannot know their exact remaining lifespan.

---

# 3.25.21 Mastery Does Not Erase Fundamental Cost

If a technique fundamentally requires:

> sacrificing ten years of life,

Mastery 100 should not turn that into:

> zero years.

Mastery might reduce:

- waste,
- accidental additional damage,
- execution instability.

It cannot remove the defining mechanism unless a genuinely modified technique is created.

---

# 3.25.22 Corpse Manipulation

Techniques involving corpses need to explicitly answer:

> Is the actual deceased person involved?

or:

> Is the corpse merely being physically controlled?

Those are completely different.

For example:

**Corpse Puppetry**

> manipulates a body physically.

**Reanimation**

> restores some form of consciousness or soul.

**Imitation**

> creates a construct using the person's appearance/memories.

These should never be treated as equivalent.

---

# 3.25.23 Soul Mechanics Are Exceptional

Techniques involving souls or postmortem consciousness should require explicit mechanics.

The technique must define what it interacts with:

- soul,
- consciousness,
- memory,
- chakra imprint,
- corpse,
- artificial copy.

We should not casually assume all of these are interchangeable.

---

# 3.25.24 Death Remains Meaningful

The existence of resurrection-related Kinjutsu should **not** make death trivial.

Ordinary death remains death.

A technique capable of undoing or circumventing death must have:

- extraordinary prerequisites,
- explicit mechanism,
- limitations,
- actual costs.

There is no generic:

> “High-level Fūinjutsu can revive people.”

---

# 3.25.25 Resurrection vs. Reanimation

We should distinguish these carefully.

### Resurrection

> The deceased individual genuinely returns to life.

### Reanimation

> The deceased consciousness/soul/body is temporarily or conditionally restored through another mechanism.

### Replication

> A copy of memories/personality is produced.

### Corpse Control

> Dead body moves without restoring the original person.

These can look similar from the outside while being mechanically very different.

---

# 3.25.26 Reanimated Characters Still Need Mechanics

A reanimated person should define:

- consciousness,
- autonomy,
- chakra source,
- bodily regeneration,
- command/control,
- memory continuity,
- release conditions.

They are not just:

> “Normal NPC with infinite HP.”

Their specific technique establishes the new state.

---

# 3.25.27 Control and Reanimation Are Separate

Restoring someone and controlling them should not automatically be the same ability.

A technique might:

> restore a dead shinobi

without giving the caster authority over them.

If control is included, that needs its own mechanism.

This preserves agency.

---

# 3.25.28 Dangerous Techniques Can Affect the World Permanently

A Kinjutsu may create persistent consequences such as:

- contaminated areas,
- modified organisms,
- broken seals,
- artificial bloodlines,
- long-term curses.

Those effects stay in the simulation.

The technique does not vanish from history after the encounter ends.

---

# 3.25.29 Strategic Kinjutsu

Some techniques may be forbidden mostly because of military consequences.

For example:

> a technique capable of bypassing village barrier systems.

It might be mechanically safe for its user but strategically disastrous if widely distributed.

The village may therefore prohibit:

- possession,
- teaching,
- export.

This is a completely valid Kinjutsu reason.

---

# 3.25.30 Classified Does Not Always Mean Forbidden

We should still distinguish:

> **Secret**

from:

> **Forbidden.**

A technique can be:

> highly classified but legal for authorized operators.

That's closer to Hiden/restricted access.

Kinjutsu applies when actual prohibition or exceptional restriction exists.

A technique can carry both tags.

---

# 3.25.31 Forbidden Hiden

A clan could possess a technique that is:

> **Hiden + Kinjutsu.**

For example:

> only clan elders know it,

and:

> clan law prohibits its use except during existential emergencies.

Hiden describes secrecy.

Kinjutsu describes prohibition.

---

# 3.25.32 Forbidden Kekkei Genkai Applications

A Kekkei Genkai itself generally should not be labeled Kinjutsu simply because authorities dislike it.

But a specific application of that bloodline might be forbidden.

For example:

> hereditary ability = legal biological trait.

> technique using it to permanently overwrite another person's body = Kinjutsu.

We should classify the technique, not criminalize the biology mechanically.

---

# 3.25.33 Prohibited Transformations

Likewise, a transformation can be Kinjutsu if:

- it is dangerously unstable,
- requires prohibited modification,
- risks uncontrollable destruction.

It still uses the normal Transformation framework.

---

# 3.25.34 Sanctioned Use

Some forbidden techniques may have emergency exceptions.

For example:

> prohibited except under Kage authorization during existential threat.

That makes them more like restricted strategic weapons.

The simulation should track authorization separately from capability.

---

# 3.25.35 Emergency Use Does Not Remove the Cost

Authorization changes:

> legal consequence.

It does not change:

> chakra cost, bodily damage, sacrifice, failure risk.

The technique remains mechanically identical.

---

# 3.25.36 Safer Variants

A researcher may redesign a forbidden technique to remove the reason it was banned.

For example:

Original:

> massively boosts Output but destroys chakra pathways.

Improved version:

> lower peak increase but stays within safe tolerance.

The variant can have a different classification.

This creates meaningful technological advancement.

---

# 3.25.37 A Variant Must Actually Remove the Problem

Renaming a technique doesn't change its classification.

If the supposed “safe version” still creates the same catastrophic risk:

> authorities have no mechanical reason to treat it differently.

Its actual structure must change.

---

# 3.25.38 Reverse Engineering Kinjutsu

Forbidden techniques can still be:

- copied,
- analyzed,
- reverse engineered,
- independently reinvented.

Prohibition doesn't make the formula unknowable.

It simply influences how easily information is available and what happens if someone is caught.

---

# 3.25.39 Lost Forbidden Techniques

Some Kinjutsu may disappear because authorities deliberately destroy:

- scrolls,
- labs,
- teachers,
- research records.

But true destruction of knowledge is difficult.

Fragments may survive.

A lost forbidden technique can therefore return centuries later.

---

# 3.25.40 Independent Reinvention

Someone can also independently rediscover a banned technique's underlying principle.

They might have no idea it existed before.

Authorities may still classify the newly invented technique as forbidden once discovered.

Again:

> technique origin and legal status are separate.

---

# 3.25.41 Knowledge of a Kinjutsu Can Be Partial

A character might know:

> the technique exists,

without knowing:

- exact mechanism,
- requirements,
- cost,
- failure behavior.

Another might possess:

> incomplete notes.

The player should not automatically receive complete information simply because the technique has been named.

---

# 3.25.42 Hidden Costs

Some Kinjutsu may have consequences unknown even to the user.

For example:

> long-term chakra-pathway degradation.

The simulation can track those effects internally.

Characters discover them through:

- symptoms,
- research,
- medical analysis,
- prior records.

This makes dangerous forbidden techniques especially appropriate for hidden state.

---

# 3.25.43 Rumored Kinjutsu

Some techniques may have exaggerated reputations.

Characters might believe:

> “Anyone who performs it dies.”

The actual mechanism might be:

> extremely dangerous but survivable under specific conditions.

Or the rumor could be accurate.

World knowledge and mechanical truth remain separate.

---

# 3.25.44 Technique Ethics Can Be Contextual

A technique requiring corpse use might be:

- culturally horrifying in one village,
- legally accepted under donor consent elsewhere.

We shouldn't hardcode one universal fictional moral code into the mechanical tag.

Instead, relevant institutions determine classification.

---

# 3.25.45 User Risk Profile

Kinjutsu particularly benefits from our existing **User Risk** field.

Potential ratings might range from:

> Negligible → Low → Moderate → High → Extreme → Catastrophic

But the exact technique describes what the danger actually is.

Two “Extreme Risk” techniques may threaten the user in completely different ways.

---

# 3.25.46 Target Threat Is Separate

A technique can be:

> extremely dangerous to the target

while perfectly safe for the caster.

Or:

> minimally dangerous to the target

while potentially fatal to the caster.

Again, this validates separating:

> User Risk

from:

> Target Threat.

---

# 3.25.47 Kinjutsu and Research Institutions

Villages may maintain controlled research programs studying prohibited techniques.

Those institutions can have:

- authorized personnel,
- safety procedures,
- secure facilities,
- test restrictions.

A legitimate researcher studying Kinjutsu should not automatically be treated mechanically as a criminal.

Whether they're authorized is world-state information.

---

# 3.25.48 Researchers Can Become Experts Without Using the Technique

A scholar might understand a forbidden technique extraordinarily well while never personally performing it.

This is especially plausible for:

- lethal self-sacrifice techniques,
- biological experiments,
- tailed-beast extraction methods.

Knowledge and performance remain separate.

---

# 3.25.49 Learning Dangerous Techniques Safely

Training methods may deliberately isolate safer components first.

For example:

> theoretical instruction → harmless sub-process → controlled simulation → limited version → full technique.

This is an important way institutions can reduce training casualties.

A stolen scroll user may lack those safeguards.

---

# 3.25.50 Player Freedom and Consequences

If the player acquires a Kinjutsu, the game should not simply say:

> “Forbidden technique—you cannot use it.”

If they mechanically satisfy the requirements, they can attempt it.

The world then responds appropriately.

That maintains player agency.

Similarly, NPCs can make the same choice.

---

# 3.25.51 NPC Decisions

NPCs should decide whether to use forbidden techniques based on:

- personality,
- desperation,
- loyalty,
- ethics,
- law,
- risk tolerance,
- missi

---

### Assistant — d026f05e-a919-4d7d-8880-be73a369db12

**Capture warning: truncated at the 20,000-character reader limit; the ending is missing.**

## Ruleset 3.26 — Jutsu Copying, Observation & Reverse Engineering

Copying should be powerful, especially for abilities specifically designed for it, but it should never collapse the entire learning system into:

> **“I saw the technique, therefore I know it.”**

The core model is:

> **Observe → Capture Information → Understand Mechanism → Verify Prerequisites → Reproduce → Refine**

A character can succeed at one stage while being completely blocked at the next.

---

### 3.26.1 Observation Is an Access Source

Observation should plug directly into the learning framework from 3.7.

Instead of obtaining access through a teacher or scroll, the learner receives information by watching another person perform the technique.

What they obtain depends on what was actually observable.

A simple Fire Release with obvious hand seals might reveal a great deal. A technique involving subtle internal chakra circulation could reveal very little from the outside.

Observation therefore produces **evidence**, not automatically a complete technique record.

---

### 3.26.2 What Can Be Observed?

Potentially visible information includes the user's hand seals, stance, body movements, chakra buildup, elemental manifestation, shape, timing, targeting, range, resulting effect, and environmental interaction.

With specialized sensory abilities, the observer may additionally perceive chakra flow, Output changes, internal transformation timing, or other normally hidden information.

But information that was never exposed remains unknown.

A viewer cannot infer every hidden chakra process merely because they saw the final explosion.

---

### 3.26.3 Information Quality

Observation quality should depend on the actual circumstances.

A clear demonstration performed slowly at five meters provides far better information than glimpsing a technique through smoke during a chaotic battle.

Relevant factors include Perception, relevant technical knowledge, distance, viewing angle, technique speed, concealment, sensory abilities, dōjutsu, number of repetitions, familiarity with related techniques, and whether the observer knew what details to watch for.

This gives ordinary observation meaningful gradations.

---

### 3.26.4 One Observation Can Sometimes Be Enough

We should not artificially require repeated observation for every technique.

If a technique is simple, the observer already possesses all supporting Skills, and a powerful copying ability records the entire execution precisely, one observation may be sufficient to progress extremely rapidly.

This is especially important for Sharingan-style copying.

The rule should be:

> **Repeated observations are needed when information remains incomplete—not because the game requires an arbitrary observation count.**

---

## 3.26.5 Ordinary Observation vs. Copying Abilities

Ordinary shinobi can learn from watching others.

A dedicated copying ability dramatically changes the **quality and speed of information acquisition**.

For example, a copying dōjutsu might allow the user to precisely perceive:

> hand-seal sequence → finger timing → chakra buildup → Nature Transformation → release timing → body movement.

That can collapse what might normally require hours or days of study into seconds of highly accurate observation.

That is legitimately extremely powerful.

But it still does not create prerequisites the observer does not possess.

---

## 3.26.6 Perfectly Recorded ≠ Performable

Consider a copying user observing a complicated Ice Release technique.

They may record its execution nearly perfectly.

Internally:

> Technique Information: Extremely Complete  
> Required Wind Nature: Available  
> Required Water Nature: Available  
> Required Ice Release Kekkei Genkai: **Unavailable**

The copying succeeded.

The reproduction fails because the character physically lacks the required fusion mechanism.

The technique can remain remembered for future analysis even though it is currently unusable.

---

## 3.26.7 Prerequisites Cannot Be Observed Into Existence

Observation cannot grant:

- Kekkei Genkai.
- Specialized anatomy.
- Dōjutsu.
- Summoning contracts.
- External entities.
- Required equipment.
- Required materials.
- Nature Transformation proficiency.
- Shape proficiency.
- Chakra Output.
- physical conditioning.
- secret biological modifications.

A copying ability can show the observer **what needs to happen**.

It cannot automatically make them capable of making it happen.

---

## 3.26.8 Supporting Skills Determine Copy Speed

This gives us an elegant way to model someone who knows many techniques.

Suppose a character already has:

> Advanced Fire Nature, Advanced Shape Transformation, strong Chakra Control, excellent Handsign Speed.

They watch another Fire jutsu.

Most of the underlying concepts are already familiar.

Their learning gap may therefore be tiny.

They might reproduce it almost immediately.

A novice observing the exact same technique could understand the hand seals but still need months of foundational training.

---

# 3.26.9 Copying Does Not Transfer Mastery

Even when a character reproduces a technique immediately, they do not inherit the original user's 0–100 mastery.

At best they begin as a newly functional user.

For example:

> Original user's mastery: 91  
> Copying observer's new mastery: 3

The observer knows how to perform it.

The original has used it for fifteen years.

Those are completely different states.

---

# 3.26.10 Highly Related Techniques Can Start With More Familiarity

We should allow some transfer from related experience.

If someone has mastered ten extremely similar Fire Release projectile techniques, copying an eleventh may not reasonably begin at the same practical unfamiliarity as their first jutsu ever.

The new jutsu still gets its own mastery record, but related Skills and technique familiarity can provide:

- faster stabilization,
- fewer beginner mistakes,
- quicker mastery growth.

This should be handled as transfer learning rather than free mastery inheritance.

---

# 3.26.11 Hand Seals Are Only One Layer

A character can copy every hand seal perfectly and still fail the technique.

This reinforces 3.9.

A complete execution might require:

> hand seals + chakra amount + internal routing + Nature Transformation + Shape Transformation + Output timing + release method.

Hand seals help structure those processes.

They aren't a magical password that produces the technique by themselves.

---

# 3.26.12 Movement Copying

Physical techniques use the same principle.

A dōjutsu may perfectly capture:

- stance,
- foot placement,
- attack angle,
- muscle timing.

But the observer still needs the required:

- Agility,
- Strength,
- flexibility,
- anatomy,
- Taijutsu Skill.

A character can know exactly how a movement works while being physically incapable of executing it at the observed level.

---

# 3.26.13 Copying a Style vs. Copying a Technique

We should distinguish individual maneuvers from complete fighting styles.

Watching one punch doesn't teach:

> an entire martial tradition.

A fighting style includes:

- transitions,
- defensive habits,
- positioning,
- timing principles,
- counters,
- tactical philosophy.

A copying ability can accelerate acquisition enormously through repeated observation, but whole styles contain much more information than one visible technique.

---

# 3.26.14 Technique Recognition Is Separate

Recognizing a technique is not the same as copying it.

A veteran might instantly see:

> “That's the Great Fireball Technique.”

without being capable of Fire Release.

Recognition comes from knowledge.

Likewise, a copying user might reproduce an unfamiliar technique without knowing:

- its name,
- creator,
- classification,
- history.

Mechanics and labels are separate information.

---

# 3.26.15 Combat Identification

During battle, characters may identify techniques from clues such as hand seals, stance, elemental buildup, equipment, known users, previous encounters, or distinctive effects.

Identification can occur at different times.

A knowledgeable shinobi might recognize the opening seal sequence **before release**.

Another might identify it only after seeing the effect.

That difference creates real reaction opportunities.

---

# 3.26.16 Misidentification

Technique recognition should not always be certain.

Similar techniques might share:

- seals,
- stance,
- chakra nature,
- initial preparation.

A character may think:

> “He's preparing Technique A.”

when the opponent is actually using Technique B.

Feints and modified techniques can exploit this.

Ruleset 2 resolves uncertain recognition.

---

# 3.26.17 Hidden Recognition Checks

Recognition is another good use of hidden resolution.

If the player fails to identify something, we should not necessarily display:

> “Technique Identification Check Failed.”

That tells them there was something important to recognize.

Instead they simply receive the information their character actually notices.

---

# 3.26.18 Observation Memory

Characters should retain meaningful observations.

For example, after fighting an enemy once, an NPC might remember:

> the enemy uses an unusual Lightning technique requiring four seals.

The next encounter can therefore be different.

They may recognize the preparation earlier.

This applies equally to the player and NPCs.

---

# 3.26.19 Partial Technique Models

A character doesn't need to be in a binary state of:

> Knows nothing / Knows everything.

They can possess an incomplete **Technique Model**.

For example:

> Effect understood.  
> Nature identified.  
> Hand seals known.  
> Internal Shape Transformation uncertain.  
> Activation timing unknown.

This information contributes directly to learning or research progress.

We can keep the detailed model internal unless gameplay requires exposing it.

---

# 3.26.20 Multiple Information Sources Can Combine

Someone trying to reconstruct a technique might accumulate information from several places.

For example:

> observed twice in combat,

> later found a damaged scroll,

> received a sensor's description of the chakra pattern,

> experimented with a related jutsu.

Those pieces can combine into a much more complete understanding.

This is much more natural than saying each source independently teaches the jutsu.

---

# 3.26.21 Scrolls vs. Observation

A high-quality scroll may reveal internal processes that are impossible to see.

Observation may reveal timing and real-world execution that a written description fails to communicate well.

Therefore neither information source should universally be superior.

The ideal learning situation may combine:

> written theory + teacher demonstration + supervised practice.

---

# 3.26.22 Sensor-Assisted Analysis

Sensory Ninjutsu can help analyze techniques.

A skilled chakra sensor might detect:

- sudden Output changes,
- concentration points,
- chakra division,
- sustained connections.

A specialized dōjutsu might provide even finer data.

But sensory information still requires interpretation.

Seeing an unfamiliar chakra pattern does not automatically explain what it means.

---

# 3.26.23 Fūinjutsu Analysis

Fūinjutsu is especially resistant to superficial copying.

An observer may visually reproduce all visible markings but miss:

- hidden chakra channels,
- activation sequence,
- stored conditions,
- layered formulae.

A true Fūinjutsu specialist can analyze the architecture.

This is closer to reverse engineering software than copying handwriting.

---

# 3.26.24 Genjutsu Analysis

Genjutsu can be similarly difficult.

Observing someone affected may reveal:

> that a Genjutsu occurred,

but not necessarily:

> how induction happened.

A user with relevant knowledge may study:

- delivery method,
- chakra interference,
- behavioral effect,
- escape behavior.

Again, visible result and internal mechanism are different things.

---

# 3.26.25 Reverse Engineering

Reverse engineering begins when the character has enough evidence to ask:

> **“How could I reproduce this effect?”**

They don't necessarily know the original process.

They reconstruct a plausible mechanism using their own knowledge.

The workflow is conceptually:

> Evidence → Hypothesis → Prototype → Testing → Failure Analysis → Revision → Functional Technique.

This links directly into the upcoming Jutsu Creation rules.

---

# 3.26.26 Exact Reconstruction

If sufficient information is available and the character uses essentially the same mechanism, the result may be an exact reconstruction.

It receives the same technique identity/version where appropriate.

The user still has independent mastery.

---

# 3.26.27 Functional Reconstruction

A reverse-engineered technique may instead achieve the same general effect differently.

Suppose an observer sees:

> a bloodline user create razor-sharp bone projectiles.

The observer cannot reproduce the biological mechanism.

But they might later create:

> Earth Release stone needles shaped to imitate the projectile behavior.

That is **not copying the Kekkei Genkai**.

It is a new technique inspired by observed functionality.

This distinction opens enormous creative potential.

---

# 3.26.28 Derivative Techniques

A derivative technique can borrow:

- tactical concept,
- Shape Transformation,
- targeting principle,
- timing,
- delivery mechanism.

It gets its own:

- name,
- requirements,
- cost,
- mastery,
- weaknesses.

This lets the world's jutsu ecosystem evolve organically.

Innovation doesn't always require inventing from nothing.

---

# 3.26.29 Imperfect Copies

An incomplete reconstruction might still function.

It could have:

- greater chakra cost,
- more hand seals,
- shorter range,
- lower stability,
- slower activation,
- different Nature,
- higher failure risk.

That isn't necessarily a failed design.

It may become a legitimate variant.

---

# 3.26.30 Copies Can Eventually Surpass Originals

There should be no rule saying a copied technique is permanently weaker.

The copy begins with less mastery and perhaps incomplete information.

But after years of development, the new user could:

- master it,
- optimize it,
- create superior variants.

At that point their version may exceed the person they originally observed.

Knowledge acquisition method should not impose a permanent power penalty.

---

# 3.26.31 Copying Restricted Techniques

Copying a Hiden or Kinjutsu does not alter its social classification.

If someone successfully copies a clan Hiden:

> they mechanically know it.

The clan may still consider that theft.

Likewise, copying a Kinjutsu may create legal consequences.

The technique itself doesn't refuse execution because the user lacked permission.

---

# 3.26.32 Copying Summoning Techniques

A character may copy the physical execution of a summoning technique but cannot copy the required **contract**.

They may know:

> exactly how the summoning method works

while receiving:

> no valid contract target.

This is an ideal example of information versus access.

---

# 3.26.33 Copying Special-Ability Techniques

When a technique requires a special ability, observation can still provide valuable knowledge.

For example, someone could learn:

> activation timing,

> tactical weaknesses,

> range,

> behavior.

They might become excellent at countering the technique despite never being able to use it.

Copying therefore has defensive intelligence value even when reproduction is impossible.

---

# 3.26.34 Techniques Designed to Resist Analysis

Some techniques may intentionally obscure their mechanism.

Methods can include:

- minimal seals,
- hidden internal processes,
- misleading movements,
- chakra masking,
- Fūinjutsu obfuscation,
- variable activation.

This can make them harder to recognize or reverse engineer.

But “uncopyable” should require an actual reason.

---

# 3.26.35 Anti-Copying Does Not Mean Magical Copyright

We should avoid arbitrary rules like:

> “This jutsu cannot be copied because it is unique.”

Instead, ask why.

Maybe it requires:

- unique anatomy,
- inaccessible chakra nature,
- hidden seal,
- personal stored resource.

If another character somehow obtains all necessary capabilities and knowledge, uniqueness alone does not stop them.

---

# 3.26.36 Personal Techniques Can Become Widespread

A shinobi may invent a signature technique.

Later:

> student learns it,

> enemy copies it,

> village standardizes it,

> future generations improve it.

Eventually the technique may cease being associated exclusively with its creator.

This is exactly how the world's jutsu knowledge should evolve.

---

# 3.26.37 Knowledge Provenance

For important techniques, the simulation can track how a character learned them:

> Academy  
> Teacher  
> Clan  
> Scroll  
> Observation  
> Copying Dōjutsu  
> Reverse Engineered  
> Self-Developed

Provenance doesn't change the technique's inherent strength.

But it can matter for:

- legality,
- relationships,
- historical research,
- teaching lineage.

---

# 3.26.38 Copying Does Not Steal the Original

Information can be duplicated.

Unlike a physical resource, learning another person's technique does not remove it from them.

What spreads is **knowledge**.

This makes information security fundamentally different from resource security.

---

# 3.26.39 Observation and Counter Development

A character doesn't need to reproduce a technique to benefit from understanding it.

Repeated encounters can reveal:

- activation tells,
- minimum range,
- blind spots,
- required conditions,
- recovery windows.

Eventually someone may design an explicit counter.

This integrates copying/analysis directly with our later counter-system rules.

---

# 3.26.40 NPC Learning From the Player

This rule must apply equally in reverse.

If the player repeatedly uses the same unusual technique in front of surviving intelligent enemies, information about it can spread.

NPCs may:

- recognize it later,
- warn allies,
- develop counters,
- attempt to copy it,
- report it to intelligence divisions.

The player does not possess information immunity.

That will make signature techniques powerful without making repetitive tactics consequence-free.

---

# 3.26.41 Intelligence Organizations

Villages can maintain technique records.

Reports may contain:

> observed seals, estimated range, known users, suspected nature, known counters.

Different villages can possess different levels of knowledge about the same technique.

That becomes persistent intelligence state rather than global encyclopedia knowledge.

---

# 3.26.42 Copying Record

We don't need another universal stat for every observed jutsu.

Instead, the character's existing learning record can store things such as:

> **Observation Source**  
> **Information Completeness**  
> **Known Components**  
> **Unknown Components**  
> **Recognized Prerequisites**  
> **Blocked Prerequisites**  
> **Reproduction Status**  
> **Reverse-Engineering Progress**  
> **Known Counter Information**  
> **Provenance**

This extends the learning system rather than duplicating it.

---

## 3.26 Core Principles

The essential rules are:

> **Observation grants information, not automatic ownership of a technique.**

> **Seeing, understanding, reproducing, and mastering are separate stages.**

> **Copying abilities can dramatically accelerate observation and comprehension without creating missing biological, elemental, physical, contractual, or resource prerequisites.**

> **A perfectly copied technique begins with the observer's own mastery, not the original user's mastery.**

> **One observation may be enough when the technique is sufficiently visible and the observer already possesses the necessary foundations; repeated viewing is needed only when information remains incomplete.**

> **Technique recognition and technique reproduction are separate capabilities.**

> **Physical movement can be perfectly underst

---

**Capture warning: truncated at the 20,000-character reader limit; the ending is missing.**

## Ruleset 3.27 — Jutsu Creation, Research & Invention

Jutsu creation should be a genuine **research and engineering process**.

The central rule is:

> **A character does not create a technique by deciding what effect they want. They must develop a chakra mechanism capable of producing that effect, make it function reliably, and then learn to use the finished result.**

This makes invention an extension of everything we've already built: Chakra Control, Output, Nature, Shape, medical knowledge, Fūinjutsu, special abilities, observation, experimentation, and individual mastery.

---

### 3.27.1 Jutsu Are Designed From Mechanisms

The starting question should never simply be:

> “What power do I want?”

It should be:

> “How could my character produce this effect using capabilities they actually understand?”

Suppose someone wants to invent a Fire technique that changes direction after being launched.

They need to solve problems involving:

> Fire Nature Transformation + Shape Transformation + post-release control + chakra connection or autonomous guidance.

Simply wanting “homing fire” doesn't establish a technique.

---

## 3.27.2 The Research Cycle

A useful conceptual development process is:

| Stage | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Concept** | Desired function has been imagined |
| **Theory** | Character has a plausible mechanism |
| **Prototype** | Something can actually be attempted |
| **Unstable Prototype** | Works inconsistently or dangerously |
| **Functional Prototype** | Reliably produces the basic intended effect |
| **Stable Technique** | Repeatable and suitable for normal use |
| **Refined Technique** | Major design flaws have been optimized |

These are research states, not mandatory visible progress bars.

The engine can track development underneath ordinary gameplay.

---

# 3.27.3 Concept

A concept describes the intended result.

For example:

> “Create a Lightning technique that travels through metal wires and incapacitates targets touching them.”

At this stage, the character hasn't necessarily solved:

- chakra delivery,
- targeting,
- voltage/intensity,
- duration,
- friendly-fire risk.

Ideas are easy.

A functioning mechanism is the hard part.

---

# 3.27.4 Theoretical Model

The researcher develops an explanation for how the effect should work.

Using the previous example:

> generate Lightning chakra → inject it into conductive wire → maintain controlled current → regulate Output so it incapacitates rather than destroys the wire.

The more established principles the character already understands, the easier this stage becomes.

---

# 3.27.5 Existing Knowledge Matters Enormously

Researchers do not invent in a vacuum.

Someone who already knows:

> several Lightning techniques,

> Chakra Flow,

> wire-based combat,

> electrical physiology,

is much closer to solving the problem than someone who only recently learned Lightning Nature Transformation.

Prior knowledge acts as a library of reusable solutions.

---

# 3.27.6 Innovation Distance

A useful hidden concept should be **Innovation Distance**.

This represents how far the proposed technique lies from things the researcher already understands.

Low innovation distance:

> Modify an existing Fireball to make it smaller and faster.

Moderate distance:

> Create a Fire projectile with mid-flight guidance.

High distance:

> Invent a completely new chakra phenomenon with no close precedent.

Greater novelty means more theoretical uncertainty and experimentation.

---

# 3.27.7 Modification Is Easier Than Invention

Improving an existing jutsu should usually be easier than creating an unrelated one.

The researcher already understands:

- the core mechanism,
- chakra routing,
- activation structure,
- failure behavior.

They only need to solve the changed component.

This is how we can naturally support incremental technological development.

---

# 3.27.8 Prototypes

Once enough theory exists, the researcher builds a prototype.

The first successful prototype may be terrible.

It might require:

> twelve hand seals,

> enormous chakra expenditure,

> ten seconds of charging,

> completely stationary use,

just to produce an effect that eventually becomes:

> three seals and one-second activation.

That improvement process is what refinement represents.

---

# 3.27.9 A Successful Test Is Not a Finished Jutsu

One successful attempt proves:

> the concept can work.

It does not necessarily prove:

> the technique is reliable.

A prototype might work once in controlled conditions and fail repeatedly afterward.

The character needs reproducibility.

---

# 3.27.10 Failed Tests Are Valuable

Failure can still advance research if it produces useful information.

Examples include:

> chakra dispersed too early,

> Shape Transformation collapsed at maximum range,

> Output damaged the weapon,

> Fire Nature destabilized the containment shell.

The researcher now knows what went wrong.

This should produce meaningful research progress.

---

# 3.27.11 Not Every Failure Teaches Something

Repeating the exact same mistake without changing anything should quickly become inefficient.

Research progression should reward:

> experimentation + analysis + adjustment

rather than mindless repetition.

This follows the same philosophy as ordinary Skill growth.

---

# 3.27.12 Research Skill Comes From Relevant Expertise

I do **not** think we need a universal “Jutsu Research Skill.”

Different inventions rely on different intellectual foundations.

A Medical technique might depend heavily on:

> Intelligence + Medical Knowledge + Chakra Control.

A new seal:

> Intelligence + Fūinjutsu.

A combat projectile:

> Nature + Shape + Control + tactical experience.

The relevant disciplines already exist.

---

# 3.27.13 Intelligence Has a Major Role

Intelligence should be especially important during:

- theory,
- troubleshooting,
- pattern recognition,
- optimization.

A highly intelligent character may require fewer experiments to understand why something failed.

But Intelligence does not replace practical Skills.

A brilliant theorist with poor Chakra Control may design something they cannot personally execute.

---

# 3.27.14 Researcher and User Can Be Different People

This opens up an important possibility.

One person might:

> design the technique.

Another might:

> possess the physical/chakra capability needed to perform it.

For example, a research team could design a technique intended for a powerful jinchūriki even if none of the researchers possess enough Output to test the final version themselves.

This makes institutional research much more interesting.

---

# 3.27.15 Technique Design Can Exceed the Creator's Personal Capability

A researcher may correctly design:

> an S-rank-scale barrier

without individually being able to power it.

The design could require:

> four elite shinobi + prepared anchors + stored chakra.

This is particularly relevant to Fūinjutsu and village infrastructure.

Knowledge and personal power remain distinct.

---

# 3.27.16 Research Difficulty

Creation difficulty should emerge from several factors rather than one rank check.

Important factors include:

> novelty, technical complexity, number of interacting chakra processes, prerequisite gaps, desired efficiency, Output requirements, precision, environmental conditions, materials, safety, and how much existing theory is available.

We don't need to expose the entire calculation.

Ruleset 2 resolves uncertain research events.

---

# 3.27.17 Rank Is Not Chosen First

The researcher should **not** decide:

> “I want to invent an A-rank jutsu.”

They decide what they're trying to make.

The technique's eventual rank is derived from its:

- complexity,
- danger,
- requirements,
- training burden,
- execution difficulty.

Rank describes the finished technique.

It isn't a design budget.

---

# 3.27.18 Rank Can Change During Development

A concept expected to be C-rank might prove far harder than anticipated.

The stabilized version could ultimately be:

> B-rank.

Alternatively, a brilliant design breakthrough might dramatically simplify something once considered A-rank.

Classification should be revised when the mechanics justify it.

---

# 3.27.19 Research Does Not Guarantee the Concept Is Possible

Sometimes the correct research conclusion is:

> “This cannot work using the proposed mechanism.”

That is legitimate progress.

The researcher may then:

- abandon it,
- alter the goal,
- seek a special ability,
- invent a different mechanism.

Characters shouldn't be guaranteed success simply because they invested enough time.

---

# 3.27.20 Impossible Effects Need New Mechanisms

Suppose someone wants:

> instant teleportation across the continent.

Ordinary Shape Transformation doesn't eventually accomplish that through enough grinding.

They need to discover or acquire a legitimate:

> Space-Time mechanism.

Research can identify missing requirements.

It cannot brute-force every limitation.

---

# 3.27.21 Component Reuse

Known jutsu can provide components for new designs.

A character might understand:

> rotating chakra sphere,

> Wind Nature infusion,

and try combining the principles.

But combining two independently mastered processes may create new problems.

Knowing both components doesn't guarantee successful integration.

---

# 3.27.22 Integration Complexity

Combining systems produces interactions.

For example:

> Nature A works.

> Shape B works.

But:

> Nature A destabilizes Shape B.

Now the researcher needs a solution.

This is why combining known techniques can still require substantial research.

---

# 3.27.23 Fūinjutsu as Research Infrastructure

Our programming-style Fūinjutsu system becomes especially powerful here.

Researchers can reuse:

- known modules,
- containment arrays,
- triggers,
- storage structures.

A laboratory might maintain standardized testing seals specifically for jutsu research.

Fūinjutsu therefore becomes an enabling technology for wider ninja research.

---

# 3.27.24 Sensory Analysis Helps Research

Sensory techniques can provide better experimental data.

A researcher might inspect:

- where chakra destabilized,
- Output spikes,
- Nature conversion failure,
- leakage.

Better observation accelerates troubleshooting.

A specialized dōjutsu can be exceptionally valuable here without automatically solving the design.

---

# 3.27.25 Medical Research

Medical jutsu creation should demand genuine medical understanding.

Someone cannot invent:

> “chakra technique that regenerates the liver”

merely through high Chakra Control.

They need to understand:

- liver structure,
- vascular repair,
- cellular processes,
- required biological material.

This preserves the rules established in 3.13.

---

# 3.27.26 Dangerous Experimentation

Research can be dangerous.

Potential consequences include:

- chakra pathway damage,
- burns,
- explosions,
- poisoning,
- mutations,
- injuries to test subjects.

The researcher chooses how aggressively to test.

More dangerous testing can sometimes provide valuable information faster, but consequences remain real.

---

# 3.27.27 Controlled Testing

Responsible researchers can reduce risk through:

> low Output → small scale → controlled targets → safety barriers → gradual escalation.

This should generally slow access to full-power data while greatly reducing catastrophic failure.

Research institutions become valuable because they provide safe facilities.

---

# 3.27.28 Battlefield Testing

Actual combat can reveal problems controlled training never exposed.

For example:

> activation is too slow,

> targeting fails against erratic movement,

> technique leaves the user defenseless afterward.

Real use can therefore accelerate refinement.

But it can also get someone killed.

---

# 3.27.29 Prototype Flaws

Early techniques may have explicit flaws.

Examples include excessive cost, poor range, unstable shape, too many hand seals, long charging time, severe recoil, high concentration burden, or inconsistent activation.

Those aren't necessarily permanent properties.

They are research problems that might be solved.

---

# 3.27.30 Some Flaws Are Fundamental

Other limitations come directly from the mechanism.

A technique requiring physical contact because chakra must enter through touch may not simply gain 100-meter range through mastery.

To remove that limitation, the creator may need to redesign the delivery system.

That can turn refinement into a new variant or new jutsu.

---

# 3.27.31 Refinement

Once functional, the technique can be optimized.

Refinement might improve:

> efficiency,

> hand-seal count,

> activation speed,

> stability,

> safe Output,

> targeting,

> maintenance burden.

But improvement should be technically justified.

There is no generic “research points → +10% damage.”

---

# 3.27.32 Optimization Tradeoffs

Improving one property can worsen another.

For example:

> greater range → lower precision,

> faster activation → higher chakra waste,

> larger area → reduced intensity.

The researcher may create specialized versions instead of one objectively superior form.

That encourages actual technique families.

---

# 3.27.33 Variant vs. New Technique

We need a practical distinction.

A **variant** keeps the same fundamental mechanism and identity while changing relatively limited dimensions.

For example:

> Fireball Technique: Wide Burst Variant.

A **new jutsu** exists when the changes materially alter the mechanism, requirements, execution, effect, or tactical role.

This can be judgment-based rather than an inflexible percentage formula.

---

# 3.27.34 Minor Variants

Minor variants can inherit much of the original technique's mastery with an unfamiliarity adjustment.

Examples:

- slightly larger version,
- shortened range,
- different trajectory.

This follows 3.8.

---

# 3.27.35 Major Variants

A major modification may be similar enough to derive from the original but distinct enough to deserve:

> its own mastery record.

For example:

> ordinary projectile → remotely guided projectile.

The original provides transfer familiarity, but the new control mechanism requires separate mastery.

---

# 3.27.36 Technique Families

One invention can lead to an entire family.

A creator might develop:

> Base Technique

then later:

> Rapid Variant  
> Defensive Variant  
> Long-Range Variant  
> Cooperative Variant

Over generations, descendants and students may expand that family dramatically.

This is how the world's jutsu ecosystem should grow.

---

# 3.27.37 Inventor Mastery

The creator should **not** automatically begin at Mastery 80 because they invented the technique.

However, they generally have an advantage.

They understand:

- internal mechanism,
- failure states,
- design intent.

So once the technique becomes functional, the creator may begin with more familiarity than someone who merely receives finished instructions.

But battlefield execution still needs practice.

---

# 3.27.38 Research Familiarity vs. Combat Mastery

A scientist may have:

> extraordinary theoretical understanding

while still being slow to perform the jutsu under pressure.

Conversely, a student may eventually become a superior combat user despite understanding less of the underlying theory.

This is an important distinction.

---

# 3.27.39 Teaching the Finished Technique

Once stabilized, a creator can teach it through the normal learning system.

Their research notes may provide exceptionally high-quality instructional material.

A refined curriculum can later make the technique much easier for others to learn than it was to invent originally.

This is how innovation becomes institutional knowledge.

---

# 3.27.40 Invention Is Harder Than Learning

Generally:

> **inventing from scratch > reverse engineering > learning from complete instruction**

in difficulty.

That isn't absolute, but it is a useful default.

An inventor must discover information that a student is simply given.

---

# 3.27.41 Collaborative Research

Multiple researchers can work together.

One might contribute:

> Fire Nature expertise.

Another:

> Shape Transformation.

Another:

> Fūinjutsu containment.

Another:

> sensory analysis.

Collaboration expands the team's combined knowledge.

But coordinating different systems can itself introduce difficulty.

---

# 3.27.42 Contributors Don't Automatically Learn the Finished Jutsu

A researcher can contribute one component without becoming capable of executing the entire result.

For example, a Fūinjutsu expert might design the containment array for a Fire technique while being incapable of Fire Release.

They understand their contribution, not necessarily the full execution.

---

# 3.27.43 Institutional Research

Villages can maintain:

- research laboratories,
- technique-development divisions,
- archives,
- training grounds.

This allows new jutsu to emerge from institutions rather than only legendary lone geniuses.

One village's technological advantage may partly come from superior research infrastructure.

---

# 3.27.44 Research Secrecy

New techniques can be classified before widespread deployment.

A village might restrict:

- prototype knowledge,
- research notes,
- finished techniques.

This connects directly to Hiden and Kinjutsu.

A new technique might begin as:

> classified experimental research

and eventually become:

> standard academy curriculum.

Knowledge status evolves.

---

# 3.27.45 Accidental Discovery

Characters can discover useful effects accidentally.

For example:

> two chakra processes interact unexpectedly during training.

That can create:

> **Discovery: unusual phenomenon observed.**

It does not instantly create:

> Learned Jutsu.

The character must determine:

- what happened,
- whether it is repeatable,
- how to activate it deliberately,
- whether it is safe.

Accidents create research opportunities.

---

# 3.27.46 Serendipitous Breakthroughs

Ruleset 2 can occasionally produce exceptionally useful discoveries during appropriate experimentation.

These should be grounded in what the character was actually researching.

A Water researcher shouldn't randomly discover teleportation because of a critical success.

Breakthroughs accelerate plausible progress rather than ignore causality.

---

# 3.27.47 Failed Research Branches

Not every research project needs to end successfully.

A character might spend months discovering:

> the proposed mechanism fundamentally cannot produce the intended effect.

That knowledge can still matter.

The failed research may later help:

- another project,
- a counter-technique,
- an entirely different invention.

Research history persists.

---

# 3.27.48 Derivative Invention

Observation and reverse engineering from 3.26 can directly feed invention.

Someone might observe a bloodline technique they cannot reproduce and ask:

> “Can I achieve the same tactical function through a mechanism I do possess?”

That can produce a completely original derivative jutsu.

This will be a major source of emergent innovation.

---

# 3.27.49 Counter-Invention

Characters can deliberately research jutsu designed to counter known enemy techniques.

For example:

> enemy develops dangerous mist technique.

Researchers analyze it and create:

> airflow-clearing jutsu,

> specialized sensors,

> sealing countermeasure.

Arms races can therefore develop naturally between villages and clans.

---

# 3.27.50 Original Does Not Mean Privileged

Player-created or simulation-generated original jutsu use exactly the same mechanics as canon techniques.

They require:

- coherent mechanism,
- appropriate prerequisites,
- resources,
- limitations,
- testing.

The game should be highly permissive creatively but strict mechanically.

---

# 3.27.51 NPC Innovation

NPCs must be able to invent techniques independently.

A talented NPC might:

- develop a personal technique,
- improve a clan Hiden,
- c

---
