## Ruleset 3.31 — Non-Chakra Resources, Tools, Consumables & Material Requirements

The game should treat physical equipment and consumable resources as **real, persistent assets** without forcing the player to manage every kunai individually unless doing so actually matters.

The central rule is:

> **Abstraction can reduce bookkeeping, but it cannot create resources that the character did not plausibly possess.**

If someone uses their last specialized antidote, it is gone. If their sword breaks, they need another sword. If a technique requires a prepared seal tag, having enough chakra does not substitute for the missing tag.

---

### 3.31.1 Three Levels of Inventory Tracking

Inventory detail should scale with importance.

| Tracking | Best Used For | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Exact** | Rare, expensive, unique, high-impact resources | special antidote, legendary weapon, experimental pill |
| **Supply** | Frequently used tactical consumables | kunai, shuriken, basic explosive tags |
| **Abstract** | Mundane background necessities | ordinary writing supplies, basic food during routine village life |

This should dramatically reduce micromanagement.

A character doesn't need:

> Kunai: 14  
> Shuriken: 22  
> Wire: 17.3 meters

during every ordinary mission unless those quantities become tactically relevant.

But rare resources should be tracked precisely.

---

## 3.31.2 Abstraction Is Not Infinite Supply

Suppose someone's standard equipment state is:

> **Kunai Supply: Adequate**

They can reasonably throw kunai during normal combat.

Repeated use might eventually change that to:

> **Low**

then:

> **Depleted**

The abstraction represents an underlying finite supply.

It doesn't mean the character owns infinite kunai.

---

## 3.31.3 Tactical Supply States

For bulk equipment, qualitative states can often be enough:

> **Full → Adequate → Low → Critical → Depleted**

We don't necessarily need to expose those states constantly.

The simulation can maintain them internally and only surface shortages when relevant.

Exact quantities can still be used whenever precision matters.

---

## 3.31.4 Standard Loadouts

Characters should be able to establish **standard loadouts**.

For example, a conventional shinobi combat loadout might establish that the character normally carries:

- basic throwing weapons,
- wire,
- several tags,
- basic medical supplies,
- mission-specific equipment.

Once established, we don't repeatedly ask:

> “Did you remember to pack a kunai?”

The loadout answers that.

Rare or specialized items remain explicit.

---

## 3.31.5 Loadouts Can Differ by Mission

A shinobi may maintain different preparations for:

> routine patrol,

> assassination,

> wilderness mission,

> medical deployment,

> reconnaissance.

Mission preparation therefore matters without requiring item-by-item packing every time.

If a character knowingly travels into a desert for two weeks, their loadout should reflect that.

---

## 3.31.6 No Retroactive Convenience Inventory

This is important for simulation fairness.

If an unexpected situation occurs, the game should not simply invent:

> “Luckily, you packed exactly the specialized sealing tool required.”

Availability should follow:

- established loadout,
- profession,
- prior preparation,
- reasonable standard equipment.

When genuinely uncertain, Ruleset 2 can resolve whether a plausible mundane item was packed.

But rare or specialized items should never appear retroactively without justification.

---

# 3.31.7 Resource Roles

A jutsu requiring physical resources should specify **what role that resource plays**.

A useful distinction is:

**Tool / Focus** — reusable item required to perform the process.

**Consumable** — destroyed or spent during use.

**Catalyst** — enables a process but may or may not be consumed.

**Payload** — substance/effect the technique delivers.

**Reagent** — chemically/biologically participates in the effect.

**Anchor** — physical component stabilizing a seal, barrier, or technique.

**Storage Medium** — contains an object, chakra, information, etc.

**Environmental Source** — existing material manipulated by the technique.

This prevents ambiguity.

---

# 3.31.8 Reusable Equipment Is Not Automatically Consumed

If a technique requires:

> chakra-conductive sword,

the sword isn't spent every time the technique is used.

It persists until:

- lost,
- stolen,
- damaged,
- destroyed.

Conversely, a technique requiring:

> one explosive tag

actually consumes the tag when detonated.

---

# 3.31.9 Equipment Does Not Replace Skill

Possessing a master-crafted sword does not grant swordsmanship.

Equipment modifies what a trained character can accomplish.

The actual result still depends on:

> weapon Skill + Attributes + equipment properties + technique mastery.

A novice with an exceptional weapon remains a novice.

---

# 3.31.10 Equipment Quality

Physical items can vary in quality.

Useful qualitative categories could include:

> Poor → Standard → High Quality → Exceptional → Masterwork

But quality shouldn't simply become a universal bonus.

Different items benefit differently.

A better sword might have:

- stronger material,
- improved balance,
- superior chakra conductivity.

A better medical kit might offer:

- greater precision,
- broader treatment capability.

---

# 3.31.11 Equipment Condition

Durable items should also track **Condition** separately from quality.

Conceptually:

> **Pristine → Worn → Damaged → Critical → Broken**

A masterwork blade can still be damaged.

A cheap weapon can still be in perfect condition.

Quality and condition therefore answer different questions.

---

# 3.31.12 No Universal Equipment HP

We don't need every kunai to have:

> 17/20 HP.

Damage should be tracked meaningfully.

For example:

> sword edge chipped,

> armor plate cracked,

> chakra conductor burned out.

Condition states are enough until exact structural simulation becomes relevant.

---

# 3.31.13 Equipment Overload

Chakra-enhanced equipment has limits.

A weapon may tolerate:

> moderate Chakra Flow

but crack under:

> extreme Lightning infusion.

Relevant equipment properties can include:

> Chakra Conductivity  
> Chakra Capacity  
> Structural Tolerance  
> Nature Compatibility  
> Retention

These should only be defined when relevant.

---

# 3.31.14 Chakra-Conductive Materials

Special materials can make Chakra Flow substantially easier or more efficient.

But “chakra conductive” should not mean:

> infinitely capable.

Different materials may excel at different things.

One alloy might:

> conduct chakra extremely efficiently

but:

> tolerate Fire poorly.

Another may be highly durable but inefficient.

This creates meaningful equipment specialization.

---

# 3.31.15 Weapons Are Persistent Objects

Weapons can be:

- dropped,
- stolen,
- broken,
- confiscated,
- recovered,
- inherited.

An important weapon should maintain persistent world state.

If a famous sword is stolen by an NPC, the original owner no longer somehow still possesses it.

---

# 3.31.16 Thrown Weapons

Thrown weapons obey conservation.

Throwing a kunai removes it from the character's immediate inventory.

After combat it may be:

> recovered,

> broken,

> lost,

> embedded somewhere inaccessible.

Routine recovery can usually be abstracted.

Only consequential shortages need detailed handling.

---

# 3.31.17 Ammunition

Ammunition-heavy fighting styles need actual supply constraints.

This can include:

> arrows,

> senbon,

> specialized cartridges/projectiles,

> poison darts.

Bulk standard ammunition can use Supply tracking.

Special ammunition should generally be exact.

---

# 3.31.18 Recoverable Ammunition

Some ammunition can be recovered.

Recovery depends on:

- battlefield access,
- damage,
- time,
- whether the user fled.

A character forced to abandon the battlefield may lose much more equipment than one who wins and spends time collecting it.

---

# 3.31.19 Explosive Tags

Explosive tags should function as actual prepared tools.

Each tag combines:

> physical medium + formula + stored/triggered chakra mechanism.

Its effect depends on its design.

“Explosive Tag” can be a standardized common product, while more sophisticated tags may differ dramatically.

---

# 3.31.20 Prepared Tags Are Manufactured

Explosive tags do not spontaneously appear because someone knows Fūinjutsu.

They must be:

- created,
- purchased,
- issued,
- stolen,
- otherwise acquired.

Creating them consumes:

- material,
- time,
- formula knowledge,
- appropriate preparation.

This gives prepared Fūinjutsu real logistical cost.

---

# 3.31.21 Triggered Tools

Prepared chakra tools may use:

- contact triggers,
- timed triggers,
- remote activation,
- proximity,
- linked seals.

These follow the Fūinjutsu logic from 3.15.

Their physical inventory state remains separate from the technique knowledge needed to create them.

---

# 3.31.22 Scrolls

Scrolls can serve multiple roles.

A scroll may contain:

> written information,

> Fūinjutsu formula,

> sealed equipment,

> stored technique preparation.

These are mechanically distinct.

We should never treat “scroll” as one universal item type.

---

# 3.31.23 Losing a Scroll Does Not Erase Knowledge

If someone has already learned a technique from a scroll:

> destroying the scroll does not erase their memory.

However, if the scroll contained:

> information they had not yet learned,

that information may now be lost.

Likewise, a storage scroll can contain physical resources whose loss matters independently.

---

# 3.31.24 Storage Scroll Conservation

A scroll containing:

> 20 kunai

has actual 20 kunai sealed inside it.

Retrieving them removes them from storage.

Destroying, stealing, or losing the scroll may make those items unavailable depending on the sealing system.

Storage Fūinjutsu does not create duplicates.

---

# 3.31.25 Medicines

Medicine should be treated as an actual consumable with defined properties.

Relevant fields can include:

> Dose  
> Delivery  
> Onset  
> Duration  
> Intended Effect  
> Side Effects  
> Contraindications  
> Overdose Behavior

The Health/Injury system later handles the precise patient effects.

---

# 3.31.26 Medicine Is Not Generic HP Potion Logic

A medicine might:

- reduce pain,
- increase clotting,
- restore fluids,
- stimulate chakra recovery,
- suppress poison symptoms.

It doesn't automatically:

> restore 50 HP.

The effect should follow its actual medical mechanism.

---

# 3.31.27 Performance-Enhancing Pills

Soldier-pill-style consumables deserve explicit mechanics.

A pill may temporarily modify:

- Chakra generation.
- Output.
- fatigue tolerance.
- Strength.
- metabolism.

But it may also create:

- crash,
- dehydration,
- metabolic strain,
- temporary recovery reduction.

The exact mechanism defines both benefits and consequences.

---

# 3.31.28 Repeated Dosing

Repeated doses should not automatically stack linearly.

A substance can define:

> stacking behavior,

> tolerance,

> toxicity,

> diminishing returns.

One drug might safely stack twice.

Another might become extremely dangerous after the first dose.

There should be no universal consumable stacking rule.

---

# 3.31.29 Tolerance

Some substances may produce tolerance after repeated use.

That could mean:

> reduced benefit,

> larger dose requirement,

> stronger withdrawal/crash.

But tolerance should only exist when the substance's mechanism supports it.

We shouldn't impose RPG-style potion cooldowns without justification.

---

# 3.31.30 Poisons

Poisons are also physical resources.

A poison record can define:

> substance,

> dose,

> delivery route,

> onset,

> symptoms/effects,

> duration,

> treatment.

Poison mechanics interact with Medical Knowledge and the future Health system.

---

# 3.31.31 Poison Delivery

Possible delivery methods include:

- injection,
- ingestion,
- inhalation,
- wound contamination,
- skin contact.

A poison that must enter the bloodstream shouldn't work simply because it touched armor.

Delivery mechanics matter.

---

# 3.31.32 Poisoned Weapons

Applying poison to a weapon consumes poison.

The coating can potentially:

- wear off,
- contaminate another surface,
- degrade over time.

A poisoned sword is therefore a temporary prepared state rather than permanently magical equipment.

---

# 3.31.33 Antidotes

Antidotes should be substance-specific or mechanism-specific where appropriate.

A generic antidote should only handle broad categories if it actually has a plausible mechanism.

Identifying the poison remains important.

This reinforces the Medical framework.

---

# 3.31.34 Specialized Reagents

Certain advanced jutsu may require:

- rare plants,
- blood,
- minerals,
- chakra-reactive compounds,
- biological samples.

These requirements can strongly limit technique availability despite the user's chakra capability.

Rare material access therefore becomes a legitimate progression constraint.

---

# 3.31.35 Material Substitution

A character may attempt to substitute a missing ingredient.

Whether that works depends on:

> what property the original ingredient provides.

A chemically similar reagent might work.

A random expensive ingredient does not automatically substitute merely because it is valuable.

Research may produce alternative formulations.

---

# 3.31.36 Crafting and Manufacturing

Producing equipment should require some combination of:

> design/recipe + materials + relevant knowledge/Skill + time + tools/facilities.

A blacksmith can create ordinary weapons.

An advanced chakra weapon might additionally require:

- rare materials,
- Fūinjutsu specialist,
- Chakra Flow expertise.

Complex items can involve teams.

---

# 3.31.37 Knowing How an Item Works ≠ Being Able to Manufacture It

Someone may completely understand:

> a special chakra-conductive sword

without possessing:

- metallurgy Skill,
- furnace,
- required alloy.

Technical knowledge and production capability remain separate.

---

# 3.31.38 Quality Control

Manufacturing can produce:

- defective,
- standard,
- exceptional

results depending on:

- Skill,
- materials,
- facilities,
- process quality.

A defect might remain hidden until the item is heavily stressed.

This is particularly relevant for experimental equipment.

---

# 3.31.39 Repair

Damaged equipment requires appropriate:

> time + material + expertise.

Some field repairs can restore partial function.

Major damage may require a workshop.

Certain unique items may be impossible to restore if their special material is lost.

---

# 3.31.40 Maintenance

Some equipment needs routine maintenance.

Examples include:

> sharpening blades,

> replenishing medical kits,

> maintaining puppets,

> inspecting seal arrays.

This should normally be abstracted during ordinary downtime.

Maintenance only becomes explicit when:

- neglected,
- unavailable,
- narratively/tactically important.

---

# 3.31.41 Carrying Capacity

We should avoid exact kilogram accounting under normal circumstances.

Instead, use meaningful **Carry Burden** such as:

> Light  
> Standard  
> Heavy  
> Overloaded

Burden can affect:

- mobility,
- Endurance,
- stealth,
- climbing,
- access speed.

Exact weight becomes relevant only when there's a genuine edge case.

---

# 3.31.42 Storage Does Not Remove All Logistics

Storage scrolls can dramatically reduce bulk.

But they may still have:

- capacity,
- access time,
- security,
- chakra requirements.

Putting everything into storage also creates concentration risk:

> lose one critical scroll → lose many supplies.

Convenience creates different vulnerabilities rather than removing logistics entirely.

---

# 3.31.43 Equipment Access Time

Having an item does not always mean it is instantly accessible.

A kunai in a thigh pouch may be quick.

A specialized device buried inside:

> a sealed storage scroll inside a backpack

may require meaningful retrieval time.

Loadout organization can matter in combat.

---

# 3.31.44 Resource Preparation

Preparing supplies before a mission can provide a legitimate advantage.

Examples include:

> antidote for known poison,

> extra wire,

> specialized sealing tags,

> additional water supply.

This rewards intelligence gathering and planning.

It is not an arbitrary “preparation bonus.”

The character simply brought useful resources.

---

# 3.31.45 Resource Depletion Persists

If the player uses:

> their only specialized sealing tag,

then it remains unavailable afterward.

Resources replenish only through actual mechanisms:

- purchase,
- village issue,
- crafting,
- recovery,
- trade,
- theft,
- resupply.

Combat ending does not refill inventory.

---

# 3.31.46 NPCs Also Have Supplies

NPCs follow the exact same rule.

Enemy shinobi can:

- run out of ammunition,
- exhaust explosive tags,
- use their last antidote,
- lose their weapon.

Elite NPCs do not receive invisible infinite consumables.

Likewise, poorly equipped enemies shouldn't mysteriously have every countermeasure necessary for the player's strategy.

---

# 3.31.47 Institutional Supply

Villages and organizations can provide equipment.

A shinobi may receive:

- standard mission gear,
- medical supplies,
- specialized intelligence equipment.

Availability can depend on:

- mission importance,
- stock,
- authorization,
- village resources.

This makes logistical strength one component of institutional power.

---

# 3.31.48 Scarcity Can Be Local

An item can be common in one region and rare elsewhere.

A specialized medicinal herb might be:

> abundant in one country

but:

> extremely expensive in another.

Resource geography can therefore affect both technique and economic development.

---

# 3.31.49 Rare Materials Stay Rare

Access to a rare material should not become permanent infinite access merely because the character obtained one sample.

The simulation tracks:

> the actual amount.

Research might eventually create substitutes or new supply chains.

But knowledge of the material doesn't produce more of it.

---

# 3.31.50 Resource Loss

Supplies can be lost through:

- theft,
- confiscation,
- destruction,
- abandoned battlefield,
- damaged storage.

Loss should matter.

A captured shinobi stripped of equipment genuinely has fewer options even if all their Skills remain intact.

---

# 3.31.51 Loot

Defeated enemies can leave equipment behind.

But loot is whatever they actually possessed.

The game should not generate:

> random treasure tables

that weren't represented in world state.

Weapons, documents, medicine, and other supplies can legitimately be recovered.

This is persistent-world resource transfer.

---

# 3.31.52 Unique Equipment

Important named tools should have their own persistent records.

That can include:

> owner,

> location,

> condition,

> special properties,

> stored contents.

Only one copy exists unless there are actually multiple physical copies.

This is especially important for legendary weapons.

---

# 3.31.53 Clones and Inventory

Clone equipment follows the Clone framework.

A clone may reproduce temporary chakra versions of equipment if its technique permits.

Those copies:

> disappear with the clone

and cannot become permanent inventory.

A clone cannot duplicate:

- medicine,
- money,
- permanent weapons,
- consumables

for economic exploitation unless the clone technique explicitly produces real permanent matter.

---

# 3.31.54 Summoning and Inventory

Summoning equipment relocates existing resources.

It doesn't create more.

If someone summons:

> their sword,

the sword is no longer wherever it was stored.

Again, conservation remains consistent.

---

# 3.31.55 Inventory UI Philosophy

The player should primarily need to think about:

> **meaningful shortages and meaningful preparation.**

They should **not** need to operate a spreadsheet after every mission.

Routine resupply can be summarized.

For example:

> 

---

## Ruleset 3.32 — NPC Jutsu Selection, Training & Off-Screen Usage

NPCs should follow the **same mechanical rules as the player**, but we should not simulate every hour of every NPC’s training in full detail.

The central rule is:

> **Players and NPCs share one mechanical reality; only the level of simulation detail changes.**

When an NPC matters directly, we use their detailed Attributes, Skills, jutsu, mastery, chakra, equipment, injuries, knowledge, and tactical situation.

When they are living their ordinary life hundreds of miles away, we can summarize routine development from their established circumstances without secretly giving them free abilities.

---

### 3.32.1 NPC Arsenals Must Come From Their History

An NPC should never possess a technique simply because:

> “A chunin should probably know this.”

Their arsenal should emerge from things such as:

- Village.
- Academy curriculum.
- Clan.
- Hiden access.
- Kekkei Genkai.
- Teachers.
- Teammates.
- Profession.
- Missions.
- Elemental affinities.
- Attributes.
- Skills.
- Personal interests.
- Research access.
- Equipment.
- Available training time.

Rank correlates with opportunity and experience.

It does not grant techniques.

---

## 3.32.2 Two Shinobi of the Same Rank Can Be Very Different

Consider two chunin.

One is:

> tracker + sensor + Wind specialist.

The other is:

> close-range Taijutsu fighter + Earth defense specialist.

They may have dramatically different arsenals despite identical formal rank.

That's desirable.

Ninja rank describes organizational standing and expected capability—not a standardized spell list.

---

# 3.32.3 Career Role Influences Training

NPCs should generally invest in techniques useful to their actual work.

A medical shinobi is more likely to train:

- diagnostics,
- healing,
- stabilization,
- medical combat methods.

An ANBU-style infiltrator might prioritize:

- concealment,
- assassination,
- mobility,
- intelligence gathering.

A village barrier specialist may possess very few offensive jutsu while being extraordinarily skilled in Fūinjutsu.

That creates believable specialists.

---

# 3.32.4 Role Does Not Dictate Everything

NPCs remain individuals.

A medic might love Fire Release.

A sensor might obsess over kenjutsu.

A clan heir may hate their clan's traditional discipline.

So the NPC's priorities should emerge from:

> **role + personality + opportunities + goals + history**

rather than an optimized class template.

---

# 3.32.5 NPCs Can Make Suboptimal Choices

This is important for life-simulation behavior.

NPCs should not always pursue mechanically optimal development.

They can:

- waste time on techniques beyond their ability,
- neglect useful Skills,
- over-specialize,
- refuse family training,
- pursue techniques they personally admire.

Humans do not optimize themselves like build calculators.

Neither should simulated characters.

---

# 3.32.6 Training Time Is Finite

Every NPC has limited time.

Learning:

> Advanced Medical Ninjutsu

means that time was not spent becoming:

> an elite swordsman.

This opportunity cost should be one of the strongest natural limits on enormous jutsu arsenals.

There is no universal jutsu-slot cap.

There is simply:

> **a finite life with finite training opportunities.**

---

# 3.32.7 Broad Arsenals Need Explanations

Characters with extremely broad arsenals can absolutely exist.

But there should be a reason.

Possible explanations include:

- Long career.
- Exceptional Intelligence.
- Copying ability.
- Multiple elite instructors.
- Extensive archives.
- Specialized training environment.
- Deliberate generalist career.
- Exceptional aptitude.

The simulation should never casually give every veteran:

> fifty unrelated high-level jutsu.

---

# 3.32.8 Specialization Produces Depth

Someone who spends ten years specializing in one technique family may develop:

- much higher mastery,
- personal variants,
- lower costs,
- better activation,
- stronger tactical understanding.

A broad generalist may have more options but less depth.

Neither approach is universally better.

---

# 3.32.9 Training Priorities

An NPC can maintain an internal set of current development priorities.

For example:

> **Primary:** improve Water Nature  
> **Secondary:** master Water Dragon  
> **Maintenance:** Taijutsu conditioning  
> **Long-Term Goal:** learn sensory ninjutsu

This gives the simulation a coherent direction for off-screen development.

We don't need to expose this to the player.

---

# 3.32.10 Priorities Can Change

NPC development should respond to life events.

A character who nearly dies to genjutsu might decide:

> “I need better anti-genjutsu training.”

A newly promoted squad leader might study:

- barrier techniques,
- communication,
- capture methods.

An injured shinobi might abandon a physically demanding fighting style.

Development therefore reacts to experience.

---

# 3.32.11 Event-Driven Development

Rather than continually simulating every training session, important events can cause major reevaluation.

Examples include:

- Graduation.
- Promotion.
- New mentor.
- Joining a specialized unit.
- Clan initiation.
- War.
- Major injury.
- Defeat.
- Encountering an unusual technique.
- Acquiring a scroll.
- Awakening a bloodline.
- New mission role.
- Becoming an instructor.

These events can change the NPC's future development path.

---

# 3.32.12 Routine Off-Screen Training

During ordinary periods, we can summarize consistent training.

For example:

> NPC trains Wind Nature regularly for six months.

Instead of rolling hundreds of practice events, the engine evaluates:

- available time,
- instructor access,
- current Skill,
- difficulty,
- consistency,
- competing responsibilities.

Then it applies appropriate progression.

This is **compression**, not free progression.

---

# 3.32.13 Important Training Still Gets Detail

We should use full mechanics when training involves:

- dangerous Kinjutsu,
- Sage training,
- new Kekkei Genkai expression,
- major jutsu research,
- severe prerequisite gaps,
- potentially consequential injury,
- important narrative events.

Those deserve actual uncertain resolution.

Routine tree-walking practice by an established shinobi does not.

---

# 3.32.14 Off-Screen Training Cannot Produce Impossible Results

Abstraction never overrides prerequisites.

An NPC cannot disappear for six months and return knowing:

> Ice Release

without possessing:

- the bloodline mechanism,
- or another valid access mechanism.

Likewise, someone without a summoning contract cannot abstractly train themselves into one.

The same underlying rules always remain.

---

# 3.32.15 Promotion Changes Opportunity

Promotion can improve access to:

- teachers,
- archives,
- restricted techniques,
- specialized units,
- advanced missions.

But promotion itself does not produce Skill increases or new jutsu.

A newly promoted chunin has the same knowledge they possessed the moment before promotion.

Their **future opportunities** change.

---

# 3.32.16 Village Doctrine

Different villages can train shinobi differently.

A village might emphasize:

- coordinated elemental warfare,
- assassination,
- Fūinjutsu,
- intelligence gathering,
- battlefield medicine.

This should influence statistical trends in NPC arsenals.

But not every village shinobi becomes identical.

Doctrine affects probability.

---

# 3.32.17 Academy Curriculum

Academies establish a shared baseline.

Common academy techniques can therefore be widespread because:

> large numbers of children are systematically taught them.

If a village changes curriculum, future generations of shinobi may possess different baseline capabilities.

That means institutional decisions can reshape the population over decades.

---

# 3.32.18 Clan Curriculum

Clans create a second layer of structured training.

A clan child may have:

> Academy Curriculum  
> + Clan Curriculum  
> + Individual Mentor

This can create substantial advantages in particular disciplines.

But again, institutional access isn't automatic mastery.

---

# 3.32.19 Teacher Availability

A character cannot easily learn something nobody around them knows how to teach.

Teacher availability strongly influences NPC development.

A village losing its only master barrier specialist may experience:

> reduced barrier expertise in the next generation.

Knowledge transmission should have real demographic effects.

---

# 3.32.20 Teammates Matter

Squad composition can shape learning.

A shinobi may learn:

- counters to teammates' techniques,
- combination attacks,
- tactical support jutsu.

Long-term teammates naturally develop complementary capabilities.

This can occur without each teammate becoming generally proficient in everyone else's specialty.

---

# 3.32.21 Missions Create Experience

Mission history should influence what NPCs become good at.

Someone repeatedly assigned to:

> infiltration missions

develops different practical mastery than someone defending fortified positions.

Mission exposure can improve:

- relevant jutsu mastery,
- tactical knowledge,
- environmental familiarity,
- technique recognition.

This is part of individual history.

---

# 3.32.22 NPC Mastery Is Persistent

Every important NPC technique should have actual mastery where detail warrants it.

The player generally does **not** know:

> Enemy's Fireball Mastery = 73.

They observe:

> extraordinarily fast seals and extremely stable Fireball execution.

Exact state remains hidden.

---

# 3.32.23 Signature Techniques

NPCs can develop signature techniques under exactly the same rules as the player.

A technique becomes signature-like because the NPC:

- uses it frequently,
- masters it deeply,
- develops tactics around it,
- creates variants.

There is no NPC-only signature bonus.

---

# 3.32.24 Familiar Techniques Are Attractive Under Pressure

When surprised or under extreme pressure, characters often rely on what they know best.

Therefore an NPC with:

> 90 mastery signature technique

may choose it over:

> theoretically stronger 25 mastery technique.

This is rational.

Reliability matters.

---

# 3.32.25 Combat Selection Is Not “Highest Rank”

NPC combat logic should never simply be:

> Select highest-ranked available jutsu.

Instead consider:

- Objective.
- Opponent.
- Known information.
- Range.
- Terrain.
- Allies.
- Civilians.
- Chakra remaining.
- Activation speed.
- Output.
- Mastery.
- Reliability.
- Expected counters.
- Collateral risk.
- Secrecy.
- Future mission needs.

The best jutsu is situational.

---

# 3.32.26 Cheap Techniques Stay Relevant

An elite shinobi may deliberately use:

> D-rank technique

against an ordinary threat.

Why spend enormous chakra if a small technique solves the problem?

This preserves low-rank techniques throughout the entire game.

---

# 3.32.27 Resource Conservation

NPCs should consider whether they expect:

- further enemies,
- long mission duration,
- reinforcements,
- escape requirements.

A disciplined shinobi may conserve chakra.

A reckless character may not.

Personality matters.

---

# 3.32.28 Desperation Changes Behavior

As the stakes increase, NPCs may become willing to use:

- higher Output,
- dangerous transformations,
- Kinjutsu,
- rare consumables,
- last-resort techniques.

A character protecting their child may behave very differently from the same character sparring.

Resource behavior should reflect stakes.

---

# 3.32.29 Personality Influences Tactical Risk

Useful personality factors can include:

- cautious vs. reckless,
- patient vs. aggressive,
- secretive vs. showy,
- merciful vs. lethal,
- disciplined vs. impulsive.

These modify decisions.

They do not alter the mechanics of the jutsu itself.

---

# 3.32.30 NPCs Can Retreat

The AI should not treat every battle as:

> fight until HP reaches zero.

Characters can:

- retreat,
- surrender,
- hide,
- stall,
- call for reinforcements,
- negotiate.

A shinobi who realizes:

> “I cannot win this matchup”

should potentially act on that conclusion.

This is essential for believable survival.

---

# 3.32.31 NPCs Can Be Wrong

NPC tactical reasoning uses what **they know**, not what the simulation knows.

An opponent may falsely believe:

> player's technique has a 20-meter maximum range.

They make a decision based on that belief.

The player then surprises them from 30 meters.

This is much better than omniscient enemy AI.

---

# 3.32.32 NPC Knowledge Sources

Their knowledge can come from:

- Personal observation.
- Mission reports.
- Village intelligence.
- Teachers.
- Clan archives.
- Rumors.
- Prior battles.

Information can be:

- correct,
- incomplete,
- outdated,
- wrong.

This creates realistic tactical uncertainty.

---

# 3.32.33 NPCs Learn During Battle

An NPC can revise strategy mid-fight.

After seeing:

> Fire technique absorbed,

they may infer:

> ordinary ninjutsu is ineffective.

They can then try:

- physical attacks,
- environmental effects,
- overload,
- retreat.

This doesn't require the NPC to know the absorber's exact mechanics immediately.

They learn from evidence.

---

# 3.32.34 NPCs Learn Between Battles

If an enemy survives an encounter with the player, they may remember what happened.

They can:

- report it,
- research it,
- prepare a counter.

A recurring enemy should therefore become more difficult to surprise with the same tactic.

The player likewise gains information about them.

---

# 3.32.35 Intelligence Can Spread

Suppose the player reveals a unique technique to several surviving enemy shinobi.

They report:

> appearance, seals, range, observed effects.

Their village intelligence service can then update its records.

Future unrelated NPCs with access to those reports may know about the technique despite never meeting the player.

Information is persistent world state.

---

# 3.32.36 Secret Techniques Create Tactical Decisions

NPCs may avoid revealing certain abilities unnecessarily.

Reasons include:

- village secrecy,
- hidden trump card,
- fear of copying,
- preserving surprise.

Therefore someone may choose a weaker public technique even when they possess a stronger secret technique.

This is particularly important for elite operatives.

---

# 3.32.37 Revealing a Secret Can Be Worth It

Conversely, survival may outweigh secrecy.

If an NPC is about to die, they may reveal:

> hidden bloodline ability.

That decision can permanently change what others know about them.

There is no reset afterward.

---

# 3.32.38 Team Coordination

Established teams should be able to coordinate much better than random collections of strong individuals.

A practiced team may know:

> Sensor locates → Earth user constrains → Fire user attacks.

They can execute this rapidly because they have trained together.

No telepathy is assumed.

They simply understand their doctrine and teammates.

---

# 3.32.39 Combination Techniques

A team can deliberately practice cooperative techniques.

These have their own:

- timing,
- mastery/familiarity,
- roles.

Two strangers possessing compatible elements should not automatically perform a perfect combination.

Coordination must be learned.

---

# 3.32.40 Protecting Setup

Teams can tactically protect characters using:

- long hand-seal sequences,
- Sage gathering,
- Fūinjutsu preparation,
- medical procedures.

One teammate buying time can be more useful than everyone attacking simultaneously.

The AI should understand roles rather than merely maximizing individual damage.

---

# 3.32.41 Off-Screen Combat

Most NPC battles do not need complete action-by-action simulation.

We can abstract them based on:

- participant capabilities,
- known arsenals,
- objectives,
- terrain,
- preparation,
- resources,
- injuries,
- intelligence,
- tactical tendencies.

Ruleset 2 can resolve important uncertainty.

Then we persist meaningful consequences.

---

# 3.32.42 Abstraction Must Respect Mechanics

Off-screen resolution cannot become:

> “Higher PAL NPC wins.”

A lower-powered specialist may have:

- ideal counters,
- better preparation,
- terrain advantage.

Likewise, overwhelming differences can make outcomes nearly automatic.

The abstraction summarizes the same underlying factors.

It doesn't replace them with narrative fiat.

---

# 3.32.43 Consequential Off-Screen Outcomes

We should explicitly persist things such as:

- deaths,
- major injuries,
- chakra exhaustion where relevant,
- rare consumables used,
- equipment destroyed,
- secret abilities revealed,
- captured intelligence,
- significant environmental damage.

We don't need to record every shuriken thrown.

---

# 3.32.44 Returning NPCs Carry Consequences

An NPC returning from an off-screen mission may come back with:

- injuries,
- new mastery,
- lost equipment,
- new knowledge,
- psychological consequences,
- changed relationships.

They do not return automatically reset simply because the player wasn't watching.

---

# 3.32.45 Off-Screen Chakra

We don't need to maintain exact:

> 73/118 CP

for every NPC at every moment in the world.

When they're not in a consequential situation, recovery can be summarized based on:

- time,
- rest,
- health.

Exact current reserves become relevant when they enter active simulation.

But prior exertion must still matter.

---

# 3.32.46 No Convenient Full Recovery

If an NPC fought a major battle ten minutes before encountering the player, abstraction cannot silently restore them to full chakra.

Their recent activity affects:

- reserves,
- injuries,
- consumables.

Simulation detail increases as the timelines converge.

---

# 3.32.47 NPC Research

NPCs can independently:

- develop jutsu,
- reverse engineer enemies,
- improve Hiden,
- create equipment.

They follow 3.27.

A village's researchers continue working even when the player ignores them.

---

# 3.32.48 NPC Innovation Can Affect the Player

The player may eventually encounter:

> a technique invented years into the simulation that did not exist when the game began.

This is desirable.

The Naruto world should continue developing.

It should not remain frozen at the initial canon state.

---

# 3.32.49 NPC Knowledge Loss

NPCs can also lose practical sharpness through long disuse.

Someone who retired for twenty years may retain theoretical knowledge but perform less cleanly than during their prime.

Their technique isn't erased.

Their practical execution may need refreshing.

---

# 3.32.50 Aging and Career Changes

As NPCs age, their priorities can shift.

A veteran whose physical capability declines may move toward:

- teaching,
- Fūinjutsu,
- strategy,
- research,
- administration.

Others may adapt techniques around their changing body.

This prevents NPCs from remaining statically frozen forever.

---

# 3.32.51 Injuries Can Change Fighting Styles

Permanent injury should affect NPC development.

A swordsman losing an arm might:

- retrain one-handed,
- adopt ninjutsu,
- retire.

They do not magically continue using the exact same arsenal unchanged if it is no longer mechanically possible.

---

# 3.32.52 Layered NPC Simulation

To manage a large world, NPCs should exist at different levels of simulation detail.

### Tier A — Fully Detailed

Important recurring NPCs.

Track:

- exact Skills,
- jutsu,
- mastery,
- resources,
- equipment,
- hidden information.

### Tier B — Persistent Relevant

Named supporting NPCs.

Track:

- major Attributes/Skills,
- role-appropriate arsenal,
- key mastery/special abilities,
- important resources.

### Tier C — Background Population

Minor/background shinobi.

Use:

- rank,
- profession,
- clan,
- village,
- experience,
- statistical templates

until additional detail becomes necessary.

This is simulation granularity—not a mechanical hierarchy.

---

# 3.32.53 Background NPC Instantiation

When a background NPC becomes important, their detailed state can be instantiated.

But the generated details must follow everything already establi

---

## Ruleset 3.33 — Jutsu Knowledge, Rarity & World Distribution

Jutsu rarity should never be treated as a disguised power rating.

The central rule is:

> **How difficult a technique is to perform and how difficult its knowledge is to obtain are separate questions.**

A technically simple technique might be extremely rare because only one remote monastery still teaches it.

An extremely difficult technique might be documented in every major village archive while only a handful of people possess the prerequisites to actually perform it.

---

### 3.33.1 Rarity Has Multiple Dimensions

Instead of one universal rarity number, important techniques can have several different dimensions:

- **Public Awareness** — how widely people know it exists.
- **Knowledge Availability** — how many usable sources exist.
- **Practitioner Population** — how many people can actually perform it.
- **Geographic Distribution** — where those practitioners/sources are.
- **Institutional Availability** — which villages, clans, schools, etc. teach it.
- **Restriction** — whether access is secret, classified, illegal, or controlled.
- **Technical Accessibility** — how unusual its prerequisites are.

This prevents misleading classifications.

---

## 3.33.2 Publicly Famous Does Not Mean Accessible

A technique might be famous throughout the shinobi world.

People may know:

> its name, famous users, visual appearance, general effect.

That doesn't mean they know how to perform it.

For example, a shinobi could recognize:

> “That is the Flying Thunder God Technique.”

while knowing absolutely nothing about:

- formula construction,
- space-time mechanics,
- targeting,
- chakra requirements.

Public awareness is not learning access.

---

# 3.33.3 Knowing the Name Gives No Mechanical Access

This should be absolute.

Hearing:

> “There is a technique called X”

does not advance someone straight to the Acquisition phase.

At most, it gives them:

> **knowledge that something exists.**

That may help them:

- search for a teacher,
- locate an archive,
- recognize it later.

But it doesn't contain the technique.

---

# 3.33.4 Knowledge Sources Must Actually Exist

Usable jutsu knowledge resides somewhere.

Possible carriers include:

- Living practitioners.
- Teachers.
- Scrolls.
- Books.
- Village archives.
- Clan records.
- Research notes.
- Oral traditions.
- Summon societies.
- Training institutions.
- Recorded observations.
- Damaged fragments.

For important techniques, the world should actually know where meaningful sources exist.

---

# 3.33.5 Living Knowledge

A living practitioner is especially valuable because they may provide:

- demonstration,
- correction,
- hidden details,
- contextual knowledge.

But knowing a technique doesn't automatically make someone a good teacher.

A master combatant might struggle to explain what they perform instinctively.

Another shinobi with lower mastery might be an exceptional instructor.

---

# 3.33.6 Written Knowledge

Written sources can preserve techniques long after their creators die.

But records differ enormously in quality.

A scroll might contain:

> complete step-by-step instruction.

Another might contain:

> only hand seals.

Another:

> theoretical notes from an unfinished prototype.

The physical existence of a document doesn't guarantee completeness.

---

# 3.33.7 Knowledge Completeness

Useful internal states could include:

> **Fragmentary**  
> **Partial**  
> **Mostly Complete**  
> **Complete**  
> **Comprehensive**

A comprehensive source might contain:

- mechanism,
- training exercises,
- failure warnings,
- variants,
- troubleshooting.

That can make it dramatically easier to learn from than a merely complete execution description.

---

# 3.33.8 Incorrect Knowledge

Information can also be wrong.

Sources might be:

- outdated,
- mistranslated,
- intentionally falsified,
- copied incorrectly,
- based on misunderstanding.

A false scroll doesn't magically become correct because it was stored in an old ruin.

Characters need to assess sources.

---

# 3.33.9 Multiple Partial Sources

Incomplete knowledge can sometimes be combined.

For example:

> one damaged scroll contains seals,

> another contains chakra theory,

> a surviving practitioner remembers the first half.

Research under 3.26–3.27 may reconstruct the missing pieces.

This is a natural way to rediscover old techniques.

---

# 3.33.10 Rarity Labels Are Descriptive

We can still use convenient labels such as:

> **Common**  
> **Uncommon**  
> **Rare**  
> **Very Rare**  
> **Lost**  
> **Unique**

But these labels summarize world state.

They do not mechanically strengthen the jutsu.

A Common C-rank technique may be much stronger than a Rare D-rank utility jutsu.

---

# 3.33.11 Rarity Is Regional

Rarity should often be evaluated locally.

For example:

> Common in Kirigakure  
> Uncommon elsewhere  
> Virtually unknown in inland minor villages

This is more believable than assigning one global rarity label.

The shinobi world has geography and institutional boundaries.

---

# 3.33.12 Common Techniques

A Common technique usually has several qualities:

- Many instructors.
- Good documentation.
- Standardized teaching methods.
- Few access restrictions.
- Reasonably attainable prerequisites.

Academy techniques are the clearest example.

Their prevalence comes from infrastructure.

---

# 3.33.13 Uncommon Techniques

An Uncommon technique may be:

- profession-specific,
- region-specific,
- difficult enough that most shinobi ignore it,
- taught by specialized units.

It isn't particularly secret.

It simply isn't widespread.

---

# 3.33.14 Rare Techniques

A Rare technique might have:

- few instructors,
- difficult prerequisites,
- geographic isolation,
- limited archives,
- narrow utility.

Rare doesn't necessarily imply secret.

A difficult medical procedure may be openly documented but known by only twenty people capable of performing it.

---

# 3.33.15 Very Rare Techniques

These may have only:

- one institution,
- a handful of practitioners,
- one surviving archive.

Losing a few key people could realistically threaten the technique's survival.

This should matter in wars and disasters.

---

# 3.33.16 Secret Is Not the Same as Rare

This distinction is extremely important.

A clan Hiden might have:

> 500 practitioners.

Inside the clan, it is relatively common.

Outside the clan:

> essentially no legitimate access.

Therefore it is:

> **Secret**

without necessarily being:

> **Rare among authorized users.**

Secrecy describes distribution control.

Rarity describes actual distribution.

---

# 3.33.17 Restricted Is Not the Same as Rare Either

A village might have hundreds of copies of a technique in secure archives.

The knowledge is plentiful institutionally.

But ordinary shinobi have:

> no authorization.

That's restricted access, not knowledge scarcity.

---

# 3.33.18 Kinjutsu Status Is Separate Again

A technique can be:

> Common + Forbidden

if many people already know it but the government later bans its use.

It could also be:

> Rare + Legal.

These classifications should remain independent.

---

# 3.33.19 Lost Techniques

“Lost” should mean:

> **No currently known complete usable source remains available.**

This does not mean the universe itself erased the technique.

Possible remnants may include:

- partial scrolls,
- damaged ruins,
- historical descriptions,
- descendants,
- incomplete observations.

A lost technique can potentially be reconstructed.

---

# 3.33.20 Lost Knowledge Can Be Recovered

Recovery might happen through:

- finding an archive,
- discovering surviving practitioner,
- reverse engineering,
- reconstructing fragments,
- independently rediscovering the same mechanism.

Once recovered and taught again, the technique is no longer truly lost.

Rarity is dynamic.

---

# 3.33.21 A Technique Can Be Believed Lost Without Being Lost

Character knowledge and world truth remain separate.

The world might believe:

> “The technique died with its final master.”

Meanwhile:

> that master's student survives in hiding.

Internally, the technique isn't actually lost.

Publicly, everyone believes it is.

This is excellent hidden-world state.

---

# 3.33.22 Unique Techniques

“Unique” should mean:

> **Only one known functional implementation or practitioner currently exists.**

It should not automatically mean:

> “Nobody else can ever learn this.”

Maybe nobody else currently has.

That's different.

---

# 3.33.23 Truly Non-Transferable Techniques Need a Reason

A technique may genuinely be unique because it requires:

- unique anatomy,
- one-of-a-kind special ability,
- irreplaceable artifact,
- personal soul/chakra property.

That is a mechanical limitation.

Simply calling something:

> “my unique technique”

doesn't prevent copying or teaching.

---

# 3.33.24 Signature Does Not Mean Unique

A famous shinobi may be strongly associated with a technique.

That technique might still have:

- students,
- imitators,
- copied variants.

Signature describes identity and reputation.

Unique describes distribution.

---

# 3.33.25 Practitioner Rarity vs. Knowledge Rarity

These should be separate.

Consider a powerful Kekkei Genkai technique.

Its complete instructions might be:

> carefully documented in clan archives.

Knowledge scarcity:

> Low inside clan.

But only a few people possess the bloodline stage required.

Practitioner rarity:

> Very High.

This distinction is critical.

---

# 3.33.26 Technical Barriers Can Create Practical Rarity

A technique may be openly available but rarely performed because it requires:

- Master-level Control.
- Huge Output.
- rare Nature combination.
- specialized anatomy.

That doesn't make the knowledge itself rare.

It makes successful users rare.

---

# 3.33.27 Social Disinterest Can Create Rarity

Some techniques may disappear simply because nobody values them.

For example:

> obsolete communications technique replaced by better methods.

The scrolls might still exist.

But few people bother learning it.

Rarity can emerge through culture rather than secrecy or difficulty.

---

# 3.33.28 Geographic Distribution

Knowledge should have a physical/social map.

A technique can be concentrated in:

- one village,
- one clan compound,
- a remote temple,
- coastal nations,
- traveling mercenaries.

Where the character lives matters.

---

# 3.33.29 Travel Matters

If the only known teacher lives:

> three countries away,

the character can't instantly learn from them.

They need:

- information,
- travel,
- access,
- relationship,
- payment/permission if relevant.

World geography constrains knowledge acquisition.

---

# 3.33.30 Migration Spreads Jutsu

When shinobi relocate, they carry knowledge with them.

A defector may introduce:

> previously village-exclusive technique

to another country.

A refugee population may preserve lost regional traditions elsewhere.

Population movement should slowly reshape distribution.

---

# 3.33.31 War Spreads Knowledge

Wars are especially powerful knowledge-transfer events.

Techniques can spread through:

- battlefield observation,
- captured prisoners,
- seized scrolls,
- destroyed archives,
- defectors,
- allied training.

War can simultaneously:

> spread some techniques

and:

> cause others to vanish.

---

# 3.33.32 Espionage

Villages can deliberately target technique knowledge.

Intelligence operations might aim to:

- steal scrolls,
- identify instructors,
- observe demonstrations,
- kidnap specialists.

This gives jutsu knowledge genuine strategic value.

---

# 3.33.33 Copying Abilities Accelerate Spread

A capable copying user can dramatically accelerate knowledge transfer.

But the same prerequisite rules remain.

They may bring home:

> detailed models of twenty enemy techniques,

while only being capable of personally using ten.

Their village's researchers may still benefit from the other ten.

---

# 3.33.34 Trade and Alliances

Allied villages can deliberately exchange techniques.

For example:

> Village A teaches medical techniques.

> Village B shares barrier formulas.

Political relationships can therefore alter future military capabilities.

Technique knowledge becomes a strategic asset.

---

# 3.33.35 Institutional Standardization

An invention initially known only by:

> one creator

can eventually become:

> standard village doctrine.

The process might be:

> inventor → testing → instructor curriculum → specialized unit → widespread training.

Rarity can therefore fall dramatically over time.

---

# 3.33.36 Standardization Requires More Than Possession

A village finding one scroll doesn't automatically make the technique common.

To standardize it, the organization may need:

- qualified instructors,
- training exercises,
- safety procedures,
- suitable students,
- resources,
- institutional desire.

A difficult technique might remain rare despite complete documentation.

---

# 3.33.37 Teaching Infrastructure Matters

One reason Common techniques remain common is not just:

> “many people know them.”

It is:

> **the world has systems designed to create new people who know them.**

Academies and clan schools are knowledge replication infrastructure.

Destroying that infrastructure can affect the next generation.

---

# 3.33.38 Knowledge Bottlenecks

Some traditions may depend heavily on one master.

Suppose:

> 40 students know beginner techniques.

But only:

> one elder knows the final stages.

If that elder dies unexpectedly, the advanced portion may become lost despite the tradition surviving generally.

Knowledge should therefore be granular.

---

# 3.33.39 Compartmentalization

Institutions may deliberately divide information.

For example:

> Team A knows formula section 1.

> Team B knows section 2.

> Only commander knows full activation.

This improves security but creates vulnerability to knowledge loss.

Our Fūinjutsu and Hiden systems support this naturally.

---

# 3.33.40 Archives

Archives should be persistent world resources.

Important archive properties can include:

- Location.
- Security.
- Contents.
- Completeness.
- Classification.
- Copies/backups.
- Condition.

Destroying one archive matters much less if:

> four redundant copies exist elsewhere.

---

# 3.33.41 Redundant Knowledge Storage

Sophisticated institutions may deliberately preserve important techniques across:

- multiple scrolls,
- separate facilities,
- trusted instructors.

This protects against catastrophic loss.

A secretive clan might do the opposite and store everything with one elder, increasing secrecy but also extinction risk.

---

# 3.33.42 Knowledge Security Is a Tradeoff

Greater secrecy can reduce:

- leaks,
- enemy copying.

But it may also reduce:

- number of instructors,
- redundancy,
- innovation.

That means extreme secrecy can eventually cause a tradition to stagnate or disappear.

This is a strong natural-world dynamic.

---

# 3.33.43 Oral Traditions

Some techniques may be transmitted primarily by teacher demonstration.

This can preserve subtle execution knowledge well.

But it is vulnerable when practitioners die.

Written traditions are easier to preserve but may miss experiential subtleties.

Different institutions can choose different approaches.

---

# 3.33.44 Independent Invention

Two people can separately invent similar techniques.

If the mechanisms are effectively identical, they may later be recognized as parallel discoveries.

If different mechanisms achieve similar effects:

> they are separate techniques.

Independent invention can therefore increase world distribution without direct knowledge transfer.

---

# 3.33.45 Convergent Jutsu

Simple useful concepts are especially likely to arise independently.

For example:

> basic projectile techniques.

Highly specific complex techniques are less likely to be independently recreated.

This means some capabilities can be globally widespread despite no common historical origin.

---

# 3.33.46 Technique Families Can Diverge Regionally

A technique imported into another village may evolve differently.

After generations:

> Village A uses original form.

> Village B developed faster short-range variant.

They may eventually become separate jutsu records.

Knowledge distribution therefore drives mechanical evolution.

---

# 3.33.47 Language and Encoding

Old records may become harder to use because of:

- obsolete terminology,
- cipher,
- language change,
- clan shorthand.

Possessing the scroll still provides a source.

But decoding it may require additional research.

This is especially useful for ancient or lost techniques.

---

# 3.33.48 False Technique Records

Counterintelligence may deliberately distribute incorrect versions.

A stolen scroll could contain:

> subtly altered hand seal sequence.

An enemy attempting to use it might fail dangerously.

Fūinjutsu-heavy societies could be especially capable of protecting knowledge this way.

---

# 3.33.49 Source Reliability

Characters may judge sources based on reputation and evidence.

A village master archive is likely more trustworthy than:

> rumor from a traveling merchant.

But even respected institutions can contain outdated errors.

No source should automatically become divine truth unless the world genuinely supports that.

---

# 3.33.50 Information About Sources Can Also Be Rare

A character may know:

> technique exists

but not know:

> who teaches it.

Finding the source becomes its own investigation.

Examples:

> locate surviving master,

> find lost temple,

> identify archive containing correct scroll.

Acquisition can therefore become meaningful gameplay.

---

# 3.33.51 No Global Technique Marketplace by Default

The player should not receive a universal menu containing:

> every technique that exists in the world.

Available learning opportunities should reflect:

- current location,
- relationships,
- organization,
- intelligence,
- known teachers,
- accessible archives.

The world is not an RPG skill shop.

---

# 3.33.52 Player Knowledge vs. Character Knowledge

This is particularly important in a Naruto setting.

The real-world user may know from the anime/manga:

> exactly how a technique works.

Their character may not.

Gameplay should use **character knowledge**.

The player's outside canon knowledge does not grant their character internal technical information unless legitimately acquired.

---

# 3.33.53 Canon Knowledge Does Not Become Universal NPC Knowledge

Likewise, because *we* know:

> a certain clan possesses a secret technique,

ordinary NPCs shouldn't automatically know that.

Each character's knowledge is determined by:

- education,
- intelligence,
- rumors,
- observation,
- archives.

This protects mystery.

---

# 3.33.54 Technique Reputation

A technique can have a reputation distinct from its actual mechanics.

Characters might believe it is:

> unstoppable,

> impossible to copy,

> fatal to use.

The real mechanism may differ.

Reputation spreads socially.

Technical truth does not automatically spread with it.

---

# 3.33.55 Rarity Can Increase

Knowledge doesn't only spread.

It can contract.

Causes include:

- war,
- purges,
- clan extinction,
- secrecy,
- destroyed archives,
- loss of interest,
- teacher deaths.

A once-common technique can become rare centuries later.

---

# 3.33.56 Rarity Can Decrease

Likewise:

> rare → uncommon → common

can occur through:

- standardized teaching,
- publication,
- copied archives,
- political reform,
- new instructors.

Rarity is dynamic world state.

---

# 3.33.57 Knowledge Events

Significant events can change the jutsu distribution map.

Examples:

> archive stolen,

> master defects,

> clan destroyed,

> new academy curriculum adopted,

> forbidden technique leaked.

These events should update future NPC generation and access.

The world evolves.

---

# 3.33.58 Rare Techniques and NPC Generation

If a technique has only:

> three kn

---
